Reach

the whole thing, one page, no clicking

← Back to story index

Chapter 1: Cent: Some numbers don't feel real until you've done the arithmetic yourself.

Kristen Station didn't look any different on a Tuesday than it did any other day, which was the whole point of it. I was anchored against the hydroponics rack in the greenhouse, a sealed pressure vessel with its own atmosphere, elbow-deep in a nutrient line that had started reading low on nitrogen for no reason anybody could find, when Dad's voice came over the comm.

Nobody jumped in straight from vacuum anymore, not since we'd converted two rooms of the old Detroit hotel into a proper equalization booth: jump in from a spacewalk, let the booth handle whatever pressure differential and bled-off suit gas came along for the ride, then take the short jump up to the sphere already clean. It had solved two problems at once, the obvious one and the slower one nobody had really noticed creeping up on us for years: cabin nitrogen, inching upward every time somebody's own body kept quietly outgassing for hours after they'd already floated in and gone back to work. Cory's idea, originally, adapted years later into something that actually worked. I still thought of it as the hotel some mornings, out of habit, even though nobody had rented a room there in a very long time.

"Cent, you have a visitor that needs to talk to you."

"Who?"

"Come find out."

I pulled my arm free, wiped my hand on my thigh, and gave the sensor housing one more look before I sealed the access panel. Whoever it was could wait the ninety seconds it took me to not leave a nutrient line half open on a working greenhouse. Dad knew that about me. He also knew, apparently, that not telling me who it was would get me moving faster than telling me would, which was an irritating thing to be right about.

He was drifting near the viewport when I jumped over, arms crossed, doing a bad job of not smiling.

"You're enjoying this," I said.

"A little."

"Who is it?"

"Go see."

I went to see.

Osei was anchored near the viewport frame with a tablet in one hand and an expression I recognized from a dozen engineering reviews: focused, patient, the look of someone who already knows the answer and is waiting to see if you'll find it yourself. She looked up when I came in and didn't smile exactly, but something in her face eased.

"Cent."

"Osei. You're a long way from Houston."

"Your dad brought me up. I've been trying to track him down all morning." She turned the tablet so I could see it, a plain spreadsheet, nothing dramatic about the layout at all. "I want to show you something. I want you to do the math, not me."

"Ominous."

"It's not ominous. It's arithmetic." She tapped the first cell. "Top speed we've actually proven, point to point, no theoretical padding. What is it?"

I knew that number the way I knew my own name. "Four hundred ten kilometers a second. The Europa run."

"Right. Now." She tapped the second cell, empty, waiting. "Alpha Centauri. Distance."

"4.24 light years." I said it automatically, the way you'd recite a fact you'd read once and never needed since. Then I heard what I'd just agreed to calculate and felt the shape of the problem arrive a half second behind the number, the way cold water finds your skin after you're already standing in it.

Dad had come in behind me. Nobody said anything. Osei just waited, tablet held out, patient as a woman with nothing but time.

I did it in my head first, the way I did all the math that mattered, and then I did it again on the tablet because the first answer felt wrong even though I already knew it wasn't.

Light years to kilometers. Kilometers divided by our proven speed. Seconds to years.

The number that came out the other side sat on the screen looking perfectly ordinary, four digits and a comma, nothing about its typeface suggesting it was any different from a grocery total or a flight time.

Three thousand, one hundred years.

I looked at it for a while.

"That's with no acceleration curve," Osei said quietly. "Straight-line, already-at-speed math. It's not a real flight plan. It's just the shape of the problem."

"It's still the shape of the problem." I clipped the tablet back against the wall strip, carefully, the way you handle something when you don't entirely trust your hands not to do something else with it. "Three thousand years."

"Three thousand years," Dad repeated, from behind me, and I heard in his voice the same thing I was feeling, which was not fear exactly. Something quieter and stranger than fear. The specific vertigo of watching a number turn a word you'd used a hundred times, casually, without ever meaning it, into something that actually meant what it said.

Farther.

I'd used that word about Mars. About Europa. I'd used it the way you use any word that's done nothing but grow a little bigger each time, comfortable, familiar, still basically the same shape it started as.

This wasn't that. This was a wall with a number on it, and the number didn't care how many times I'd already proven I could push past the last one.

"Okay," I said, and heard how flat it came out, and let it stay flat because that was actually where I was. Not scared. Not excited. Just holding very still in front of a fact that hadn't finished landing yet. "Okay. So that's what we've actually got."

Osei watched me do the math and watched me finish doing it, and didn't say anything else, which I appreciated more than I could have told her. She hadn't come up here to talk me into anything. She'd come up here to make sure I did the arithmetic myself, right there in the sphere, before anyone else got to hand me an opinion about what it meant.

I looked at the number a while longer.

"So," I said. "Farther, then."

Nobody answered that. Nobody needed to. It wasn't actually a question.

✳

Chapter 2: Cent: The problem wasn't a secret. Everyone who'd ever set foot on Kristen Station knew it ran ninety-eight percent oxygen at ten psi, because that was the entire reason the station existed on the timeline it existed on in the first place: Grandmother's lungs, not some engineering wish list somebody eventually got around to. What none of us had actually solved, in all the years since, was how anybody who wasn't family, wasn't trained, wasn't willing to go through my own long prebreathing routine first, was supposed to cross that boundary safely on a normal, regular basis. Seeana crossed it constantly, coming and going with Grandmother's own care schedule. So did half the specialists who came up for a few hours at a time and went home the same day. I'd known that was a real problem for years. I just hadn't done anything about it beyond making everyone suffer through the same routine I used for vacuum work, which worked fine for me and badly for everyone whose whole life wasn't already built around tolerating it.

Cory called it a problem worth solving properly the same week I finally said it out loud instead of just living with it, and looking back, that's the whole difference between the two of us in one sentence.

I'd been prebreathing pure oxygen before every vacuum job for years by then, forty-five minutes with a mask on while I did the rest of my own prep around it, checklist items, suit inspection, whatever else needed doing, flushing nitrogen out of my own blood the honest, slow way, the way anybody does it who doesn't want the bends. It solved that part completely. It didn't solve the rest of it, not even for me.

Prebreathing clears blood and tissue. It does nothing for whatever's soaked into your own clothes and skin, and it does nothing for the ordinary gas trapped in every pocket, seam, and fold of fabric, held at whatever pressure it started at, that simply leaks out into the new air once there's a differential to leak across, higher pressure toward lower, plain physics, no jump-specific magic required. The transition itself isn't instantaneous either, whatever it looks like from the outside, and that's exactly the part that matters here: even one single jump, no lingering, no repeats, still spans a real if brief span of time with a real pressure difference sitting across it the whole way through.

It's the same underlying gap that makes twinning work at all, if you think about it long enough. All any of it ever actually needs is a differential and time, that's the whole of the physics. A jump is just that gap closing itself almost as soon as it opens. Twinning is nothing more exotic than choosing not to let it close yet.

Leakage, Cory started calling it once he'd actually bothered to measure it, and it turned out to matter more than either of us had assumed, coming off a body dressed for ordinary Earth pressure and stepping straight into a sphere running thin at ten psi. I'd been quietly adding to the exact same problem I thought I'd already solved, just slower than everyone else added to it. It didn't work at all for Seeana, who had actual patient care waiting on the other side of that mask, and it didn't work for specialists who came up for three hours of consulting and had zero interest in spending half of it walking around with a mask strapped to their face before they'd even started the actual work.

"You're all breathing each other's slow exhale too, you know," Cory said, once I finally laid the rest of it out for him, the part I actually had noticed myself and just hadn't gotten around to prioritizing. "Nitrogen creep. Every unpurged body that comes through outgasses for hours afterward. On a sealed sphere that size, it adds up." He said it like a bonus discovery, which, next to what it actually meant for Seeana's day, it honestly was.

"I know what I need. I don't have anywhere to put it."

"You have a hotel."

The Detroit place, by then, wasn't the boarded-up wreck Mom had first scouted years back. We'd already fixed the roof, run real plumbing, put working locks on twenty doors, because a family that spent as much time bouncing between cities as ours did needed somewhere with actual walls more than it needed another line item sitting idle. Two of those twenty rooms happened to be empty that particular month, which was really the whole reason Cory picked it.

We didn't need to budget anything, and I want to be honest about that instead of pretending otherwise. That's not how the money worked for us. We were a family running our own satellite business, not a program answering to anybody else's fiscal year. Cory brought the pressure engineering, the same discipline he'd spent his whole career putting into suits instead of rooms: staged volumes, controlled bleed rates, redundant seals on everything, because a man who builds garments meant to keep a human body alive in vacuum does not, it turns out, know how to build anything casually.

His first instinct was an airlock. A real one, the NASA kind, two doors and a chamber between them and a checklist as long as his arm, because that was the way it had always been done, the only way he'd personally ever seen it done, and I watched him try to make that shape fit a problem it didn't actually match. He started explaining why it had to work that way, the way pressure engineering had always worked.

"You're arguing the way it has to be because that's what's actually been necessary for NASA," I said, before he got more than a few words into it. "It's not necessary for my parents and me. It's never been necessary for any of us. I don't need doors. I need a room. The room does the equalizing. I do the moving. Nobody's ever opening a hatch onto vacuum by hand, because there's never a hatch to open. I jump in. I jump out. The room just needs to be a different atmosphere than either end by the time I do."

I'd had some version of this exact argument with him before, years earlier, back when the suit itself was still just parts on a bench. He'd wanted lithium hydroxide for the scrubber over plain soda lime, forty percent lighter, and I'd asked why that mattered when soda lime was so much cheaper. "Too heavy," he'd said, not even looking up from the sewing machine, doing the math the only way he'd ever been trained to do it: dollars per pound to orbit, a rocket's own economics, gravity well and all. It took me walking him through my actual cost structure, fixed once and effectively zero after, before something in his face genuinely broke open over how weird that was to him. I don't think either of us had noticed, back then, that it was the first time, not the last, that his instincts would default to a cost that simply didn't apply to me. I noticed it now, watching him build a second airlock he didn't need for the exact same reason.

He didn't love giving up the airlock instinct. I could see it costing him something, a whole career's worth of assumption about what a pressure boundary was supposed to look like. But I watched him actually let go of the shape he already knew and start building toward the shape the problem was actually asking for, which is the moment I trust an engineer completely, every time: not when they know the standard answer, but when they're willing to notice it doesn't fit.

We gave the room real space once we started building it properly. Enough to sit. Enough to lie down flat, comfortably, for however long a cycle actually took, with something to look at besides four bare walls, because Seeana was going to be the one using it most, and I wasn't going to ask a woman who spent her whole day taking care of somebody else to spend part of it bored out of her mind in a closet.

The oxygen concentrators were Cory's idea, adapted off equipment neither of us had originally built for this. Strip the nitrogen instead of just diluting it, run the room's own air through the cycle continuously instead of waiting on lungs to do it the slow way. We tested it on ourselves first, obviously, because neither of us was in the business of asking anyone else to trust something we hadn't already trusted with our own blood.

It worked, both ways at once. Seeana crossed in a fraction of the time, easy, actually rested instead of bored stiff on a cot. And cabin nitrogen on the station, the thing Cory had noticed almost as an aside, stopped creeping for the first time in years, solved by the same room that had really been built for an entirely different reason.

The second booth came later, once the greenhouse itself was going up, and it was a much smaller job by comparison, mostly because we already knew exactly what we were building the second time. Osei ran the structural side of that one, integrating the buffer into the greenhouse's own hull design from the first weld instead of retrofitting it in afterward the way we'd had to do with the hotel. Cory signed off on the pressure staging over a video call from Lubbock, said something dry about how he'd apparently become the guy people called about rooms now instead of suits, and I told him I'd take it as a compliment whether he meant it as one or not.

It solved a problem we hadn't actually gone looking to solve, too. Healthy plants under real light run the exact opposite drift we'd just spent months fixing on the human side, pulling CO2 down and pushing O2 up instead of the other way around, and left alone long enough, a sealed greenhouse would eventually get too rich to be safe for anyone working in it. Osei caught it early, the same instinct that had caught the off-axis chair problem years before anyone asked her to look for it, and once she'd caught it, the actual fix wasn't a fix at all. We just piped the surplus into storage tanks instead of venting it, the same kind of tanks we already used to top off the main sphere's own mix, and let the station draw on it whenever the level ran low instead of ever having to import oxygen from anywhere else. The greenhouse didn't need to get rid of what it made too much of. The station next door had been asking for exactly that, the whole time, without either structure knowing it.

I think about that hotel room more than people would probably expect, given how unglamorous the actual work in it was. It's not the kind of thing that ends up in any broadcast, no cameras, no press release, just two people who finally stopped letting a real problem sit there unsolved because the person it mattered to most had spent years not making a fuss about it. I've built a lot of things since that mattered more, by any obvious measure. I'm not sure I've built many that mattered more to the person actually living with the results of it, every single day, without ever once having to think about it again.

✳

Chapter 3: Truman: I've delivered a lot of news in my career. This was the first time I was actually afraid of how it would land.

Sterling met me at the pad an hour before, and we spent most of that hour not talking about the thing we were about to walk in and say, which suited both of us fine. He'd flown out from Colorado Springs; I'd come from the Cape. Neither trip had anything to do with the actual distance involved. Davy jumped us up together, the two of us and a briefing folder neither of us needed since we'd both memorized every line in it, and it was such an ordinary piece of logistics by now that nobody in the room even remarked on it. That was its own kind of strange, if I let myself think about it. A man could still fold space like it was nothing, and the strangest part of my day was going to be a conversation.

Millie, Davy, and Joe were all there when we came through, along with Cent. I read the room fast, out of habit: nobody surprised to see us, which meant Davy had warned them a visitor was coming and let the rest sit as a mystery, the same instinct I'd have had myself.

I started the way I'd rehearsed it, because rehearsing it was the only thing that had kept me from losing an hour of sleep the night before.

"I briefed the President personally. He initiated this through the normal channels, not around them. NASA's put together a feasibility package. About eleven thousand people, one way or another, touched some part of this before it got anywhere near a folder."

I heard myself say it and knew how it sounded: careful, institutional, a man protecting himself as much as informing anyone. I'd built that voice over four decades because it worked, because it kept a room from panicking before the facts were even on the table, and I hated a little that I still needed it here, in this kitchen, with people I actually cared about the answer for.

Sterling picked it up from there, the way we'd planned. "There's a destination. Alpha Centauri."

Nobody said anything for a second. Then Sterling added the line I hadn't planned, the one that wasn't in either of our folders.

"I promised to bring this up later."

Later, apparently, had just arrived. I watched that land. I hadn't been in the room for whatever conversation that callback belonged to, but I didn't need to have been. Cent's face did something quick and complicated, and Sterling's did something quieter, and I knew I'd just watched two people close a loop that had been open a long time.

Davy was the first to actually react, and I'd have known it was coming even without four decades of reading rooms for a living. I knew his history. I knew what "farther" cost a man who'd spent half his life being hunted for what he could do.

"Absolutely not," he said, quiet at first, the quiet of a man building toward something louder.

Joe was the opposite, fast and bright, leaning forward instead of back. "Alpha Centauri. Actually there. Not just..."

"Joe." Millie's voice, trying to get ahead of both of them. "Let's slow down and actually think about what's being..."

It went from there the way these things go, three voices at once, nobody actually listening, Davy's quiet climbing toward not-quiet at all. I'd seen this exact shape of argument before, in rooms with a lot more rank in them and a lot less love, and it never got anywhere on its own.

"Enough!"

Cent's voice, short and hard, cut clean through all three of them, and the room actually stopped. I mean that literally. Davy's mouth was still open on whatever he'd been about to say next, and it just didn't come.

Into that silence, she said the rest of it, quiet now, addressed to the whole room and not to any one person in it.

"We can argue about it later. I'm doing this."

I watched the whole arc of it happen in about ninety seconds: the chaos, the shout, the decision landing like something already true before she'd said it out loud. I'd delivered a lot of news in my career. I had never once watched the person receiving it end the argument about it herself, in front of her own family, before anyone else in the room had finished reacting.

Sterling caught my eye. We'd worked together long enough that the look meant exactly one thing: this isn't ours anymore.

"We'll leave you to it," I said. "There's no clock on this beyond the one we already told you about. Take whatever time the family needs."

Davy walked us out, still working his jaw the way a man does when he's got more to say and nowhere left to put it. He didn't say anything else, and neither did I.

I didn't fully process what I'd just watched until I was back at the Cape that night, going over the day the way I went over every day, looking for the places I might have done it better. I couldn't find one. The delivery had gone exactly the way I'd rehearsed it going, right up until the part I hadn't rehearsed at all, which was watching a family absorb something that size in real time and not knowing, when I left, whether they'd still be standing the same way in the morning.

I told myself that wasn't my business to know. I mostly believed it.

✳

Chapter 4: Millie: I never got to finish my sentence. I've had a while now to make my peace with that.

The door closed behind Truman and Sterling, and for about two seconds nobody in the kitchen said anything at all.

Then Davy said, "Absolutely not," again, quieter this time, like he was trying the sentence on now that there was no one official left in the room to hear it, and finding it didn't fit any better the second time.

"Davy."

"Don't Davy me. You heard the number same as I did. Three thousand years, Millie. Straight-line. That's not a mission, that's a fairy tale with a flight plan."

"I heard the same number you did." I kept my voice level, the way I'd learned to over twenty-some years of being the one who slowed things down in a family that mostly ran hot. "I also heard her say yes before either of us got a single word in. That happened, whether you want to argue with the number or not."

"That's exactly the problem."

I opened my mouth to actually make my case, the real one, the one about time and readiness and whether anyone should have to decide something this size in the space between one sentence and the next, and I got exactly as far as "I think we need to slow down and actually..." before Joe cut in, not rude about it, just too full of his own reaction to notice he was cutting in at all.

"Alpha Centauri. Dad. Actual proof there's something else out there besides us." He was practically vibrating. "You know what this means?"

"I know what it means for the girl I've spent twenty years trying to keep alive."

"She's not a girl anymore, Davy." That was as far as I got with that thought too. Davy was already moving on, Joe was already answering him, and I gave up on finishing anything. The decision had already happened, hours ago, in four words, in front of two men from NASA. This wasn't a debate. It was the rest of us catching up.

I stopped talking and started watching instead.

Davy wasn't arguing with Cent. He was arguing with being nineteen again, hunted, somebody else deciding his own body was theirs to use. I'd watched this exact fight three times before across our daughter's whole life. The night men with guns came to the house in New Prospect and they lost the whole ordinary life they'd built there in a single evening. The missile, years later, and the attacker who sent it. That stretch not so long ago when he called himself the rogue in the room, like the word could protect her by landing on him first. Four times now, and I didn't think he even knew he was doing it. It was never about the mission. It was about a girl he loved more than his own safety, doing the thing he'd spent her whole life trying to keep her from having to do without a choice.

Except she'd had a choice. That was the part I watched him work through, slow and visible, over the next hour. Not this decision specifically. Every decision she'd ever made, all the way back. He kept circling around it, arguing the same three points in different order, and I could see the exact moment it actually landed for him, somewhere past midnight, when he stopped mid-sentence and just looked at her for a second too long.

"You didn't think about this at all before you said yes, did you," he said. Not really a question.

"I thought about it for about four seconds," Cent said. "Which felt like plenty."

"That's not thinking. That's just you being you."

"Yeah," she said. "It is."

He didn't have an answer for that, because there wasn't one. She wasn't reckless. She wasn't calculating either, not the slow careful way the rest of us were. She was just faster than everyone in the room put together, and always had been. I watched her father finally understand it, somewhere around one in the morning: not a flaw he could argue his way around. Just who she'd always been.

"Fine," he said, and something in his shoulders came down that had been up since the moment the word Centauri first landed in the room. "Fine. I reserve the right to say I told you so."

"Duly noted."

"I mean it. Every single time something goes wrong out there, I'm bringing this exact conversation back up."

"I'd expect nothing less."

He almost smiled. It wasn't much, but I'd take it, after a night like this one.

Joe never needed talking down. His fear was real, a fine tremor under everything he said, but excitement kept winning against it, every time. He wasn't naive about the danger. He just wanted this badly enough to outrun it. I envied him that.

It was well past two by the time the house went quiet. Davy was asleep before his head hit the pillow, wrung out from finally putting down a fight he'd carried twenty years. I lay awake a good while longer, staring at the ceiling fan turning slow overhead.

I was proud of her. That part wasn't complicated. The rest of it I didn't say out loud, not even to Davy: how tired I was of being the reasonable one in a family that never once waited around for reasonable to catch up. I'd made my peace with it a long time ago. I'd just never gotten used to it.

Three thousand years, I thought, staring at the ceiling. Not really. Four hundred ten kilometers a second and a girl who'd never once found a wall she didn't want to know the actual shape of.

I closed my eyes and let myself be proud and scared at the same time, which was, I'd learned, mostly what being her mother had always actually felt like.

✳

Chapter 5: Holcomb: I told McGarrity what I wanted before anyone else arrived. I wanted him to hear it from me, not read it in a memo.

"I'm not going to have someone think they have the right to overrule me on this again."

He didn't ask who I meant. He just nodded, once, and wrote something down he probably didn't need to write down.

"Small room. People I trust." I sat down behind the desk, unhurried, and let the chair take my weight before I said the rest. "That's the policy. Not a suggestion. I wanted you to hear it from me before it looked like an accident of scheduling."

"Understood."

That was the whole exchange. It was enough.

Truman and Sterling came in not long after. I stayed where I was, hands flat on the desk, and let them talk without filling any of the silence myself. That's a habit that costs some men their patience. It's earned me more honest answers than any question I've ever asked.

"She said we can argue about it later," Truman said. "She's doing this."

I didn't say anything for a while. Mars had been a proving ground. Europa had been a promise kept. This was three thousand years of straight-line arithmetic and a girl who'd needed four seconds to decide.

"Her parents," I said.

"Not happy."

"They shouldn't be." I stood, walked to the window, stood there with my back to the room long enough that I heard Sterling shift in his chair behind me. "All right. Let's talk about where."

The destination meeting, when we had it properly, put the Joint Chiefs and NASA's own leadership in the same room and let them work the problem instead of presenting it to me for a signature. I sat at the head of the table and mostly listened. Alpha Centauri wasn't a hard call. Nearest system, wide enough margin that nothing else got much air. Proxima Centauri b as the target.

"Habitable zone," somebody said, halfway through, the word doing a lot of quiet work it hadn't earned yet.

"It's not habitable. It's a guess with good odds." I didn't raise my voice for it. I didn't need to. "Say what we actually know. I've sat in too many rooms where somebody picked a soft word so nobody had to own the hard one underneath it. Not in here. Not on this."

Nobody used the word again.

"Say the number," I said, once they'd worked through the rest of it. Somebody had already said it. I wanted it said again, on the record, with every person in that room hearing it a second time before I asked any of them to sign their name to what came next.

"Three thousand one hundred years. Straight line. No acceleration curve."

I let that sit in the room a moment. Then I stood, both hands flat on the table now, and leaned in enough that every eye came up to meet mine.

"Nobody in this government owns that young woman." I said it slow, no theater in it, just weight. "Not this room. Not this administration. Not whoever sits in this chair after me. She's choosing to be part of what we're building. The day that stops being true, the program stops. Not her. I want that written down as policy. Not sentiment."

Nobody argued. I hadn't expected them to. I'd paid for that lesson once already, and I wasn't paying for it twice.

McGarrity stayed after the room emptied, the way he usually did, gathering the loose threads nobody else remembered to gather. I watched him work for a minute before I said anything.

"You'll inherit this," I told him, plain, no ceremony attached to it. "Not the decision. The consequences of it. Whatever this becomes, good or bad, it'll still be running long after I'm out of this chair, and it'll land on whoever's sitting in it next."

He didn't flinch from that, which told me something about him I filed away without saying out loud. "I understand that."

"I don't think you do yet. Nobody does, the first time somebody hands them a thing this size and tells them it's theirs to carry now too." I studied him a moment longer. "You'll understand it eventually. I'd rather you hear it from me now than discover it cold, alone, in this exact room, someday."

"Duly noted, sir."

"See that it is."

I read the room when it broke, the way I always do, watching faces instead of listening to words. Real agreement looks different from the kind a man gives because arguing costs more than it's worth. I know the difference. I sat down again, finally, and let myself feel it.

✳

Chapter 6: Truman: I've run a lot of meetings in my career. I don't think I've ever run one carrying quite this much weight into the room with me, and almost nobody in it knew that.

I was nineteen the first time I watched something I couldn't stop. Not NASA yet, just a kid who'd talked his way into a spot close enough to the test stand to see it, because I wanted to be near it, near the actual work, before I knew what near could cost. Three men. A test they should have walked away from clean. I watched it happen through a window I was too far away to do anything through, and there was no failure in the review afterward that had my name anywhere near it, which somehow made it worse instead of better. Nothing to blame myself for meant nothing to fix in myself either, except everything, except the part where I'd stood there and watched and hadn't been able to do one single thing.

I joined this agency because of that day, not despite it. I've spent every year since building the kind of composure that looks like nothing rattles me, and it's mostly true, and it's built entirely out of that one afternoon and every afternoon like it that's come since. There've been more than I'd like to count. Each one adds a little more to both columns at once: what it costs me, and what I won't let happen again if I can help it. I don't know how to separate the two anymore. I'm not sure I want to.

None of that was in the room with me today, except the part that always is.

I ran the session myself. Sterling could have, and would have done it well, but this one needed to come from me, and I think everyone in the room understood that without my having to say it. The advisors actually advised, which I'd learned not to take for granted. Real engagement, real pushback on the how, nobody trying to relitigate the whether. Dr. Ronald Quincy, from Research, laid out an orbital insertion problem with a level of precision that made me lean back in my chair and just watch him work for a minute. NASA borrowed him for exactly this kind of thing every so often, whenever a problem needed a mind that didn't much care whose specialty it was supposed to be, and he'd never once turned down the invitation.

"You're pretty much the smartest man on this planet," I told him, meaning every word of it, and he didn't even look up from his notes.

"On this planet, sure. I don't make claims past the walls." He finally glanced up. "There's a woman in propulsion, though, who'd take real offense at 'pretty much.'"

The room actually laughed, which was worth more to me right then than I probably let on.

Proxima Centauri b got selected the way it should have: through actual argument, actual give-and-take, Quincy pushing back hard on habitable-zone assumptions until the language in the briefing got corrected twice over before anyone would let it stand. He didn't soften it for the room, the two advisors who'd drafted the original language, or me. He just kept saying he was right until the data actually caught up and proved it, the same way I imagined he said everything.

Somewhere in the middle of it, when the weight of what we were actually deciding started pressing down harder on the room than the technical details could distract from, I felt the old pattern start up in my chest, familiar as breathing.

Suck it up, man.

I didn't say it out loud. I never do, not really, not as more than a breath I let out slow through my nose. But I felt it do what it always does, which is hold the door shut on the part of me that wants, every single time, to just stop and feel the whole weight of it instead of doing the job in front of me.

You got this.

Different words, same door. I've never once said either phrase the same way twice. I don't think a mantra survives being recited like scripture. It survives by actually meaning something new each time you need it, which I have, more times than I'd have chosen if choosing had been on the table.

This time, the agreement came easy. I want to note that, because it wasn't always true, not with this program, not in the early days when every step forward came with somebody in a suit finding a reason to slow it down first. Nobody in this room needed convincing today. I didn't have to talk anyone into believing in this the way I'd had to once, a long time ago, in rooms that trusted us a great deal less. I just had to run the meeting, and let people who already understood the stakes do the actual work of getting us somewhere real.

The meeting closed in real agreement, the kind you can feel settle into a room rather than the kind that gets voted through because the alternative costs more political capital than anyone's willing to spend. I sat with that for a second before I let myself stand up.

Sterling caught me in the hallway after, both of us quiet for a minute before either of us said anything.

"I remember thinking this would be the easy part," he said.

"Which part."

"Getting everyone to agree it should happen. I thought that would be the hard one, and here we are, and it wasn't."

"No," I said. "It wasn't."

We didn't say what we were both thinking, which was that the actual hard part hadn't started yet, and both of us knew it, and neither of us needed to say it out loud for the other one to hear it anyway.

✳

Chapter 7: Harding: I was the one who pushed back on the habitable-zone language, and I'll admit I enjoyed it more than a scientist is strictly supposed to admit enjoying anything in a room that serious.

"The zone isn't a line," I said. "It's a guess wearing a line's clothing, and I've bet my truck on worse-dressed guesses than this one and lost every single time. If we let that phrasing stand in the final brief, in ten years some review board is going to quote it back at us as the exact moment this program stopped being careful about what it actually knew versus what it just plain hoped."

Nobody in that room enjoyed being corrected twice over on the same paragraph, least of all by the one specialist in it whose actual credentials came from a state college and a decade spent parked on the shoulder of two-lane highways instead of from any of the institutions everyone else's degrees came from. I'd gotten used to that particular flavor of silence a long time before NASA ever asked me into a room like it. A man who reads weather off a ditch instead of a satellite learns early which rooms are going to look at him like he tracked something in on his boots.

I read sparse, contradictory, half-trustworthy data for a living, and had for most of my life, long before anyone paid me to do it.

I grew up eleven miles outside a town most people have never heard of, in the part of Oklahoma where the land runs flat enough that you can watch weather build for forty miles before it ever reaches you. My father drove storm patrol for the county, volunteer radio truck, the kind of man who could read a wall cloud the way other men read a newspaper, calm about it in a way that used to embarrass me a little as a kid, like he was showing off by not being scared of something that scared everyone else.

I was eleven the Tuesday it mattered. He went out that afternoon the way he'd gone out probably two hundred afternoons before it, and I stood on our porch and watched the sky do something I still don't have fully adequate language for, even now, even with every degree I eventually earned trying to give me better words for it. Green, and patient, and enormous. I learned something that day I didn't have the vocabulary for yet: that the sky wasn't malicious. It didn't have to be. Indifference at that scale does all the same damage malice would, and asks for none of the blame.

He didn't come home. He'd gone back for a family whose car had stalled crossing a low bridge, got them clear, and didn't get himself clear in time behind them. I found out the actual facts of it years later, piecing them together the patient way I've since learned to piece together everything, because nobody sat an eleven-year-old down and explained it properly at the time. They just told me he was gone, and let me build the rest myself out of scraps, which is, I've come to think, how I ended up doing what I do for a living.

I didn't grow up afraid of storms. Everyone assumes that's the shape it should have taken, and I understand why, but fear was never the actual inheritance. What I got instead was something I'd apparently had since before I had words for it, a way of noticing a sky that nobody had ever actually taught me to notice, not when I was small enough that nobody thought twice about a kid who stared at clouds instead of playing. I didn't so much learn to read a storm as finally find people who could put a name to what I'd already been reading my whole life without knowing that's what I was doing. I started riding along with the same county spotter network he'd volunteered for before I was old enough to drive myself, less to be taught from zero than to finally have somewhere to put a thing I'd apparently been carrying around since before I could explain it, logging pressure drops and wind shifts in a notebook not so different from the one Grace still carries. I got good at it faster than anyone expected, because I was never actually starting from nothing the way everyone assumed I was.

It wasn't the instruments that made me good. Instruments lie by omission constantly, telling you everything they're pointed at and nothing about the ninety percent of the sky they aren't. What made me good was learning to read the parts nobody was measuring: the specific wrongness in a cloud base's color, the way birds went quiet twenty minutes before anything showed up on radar, a hundred small, unmeasured, dismissible details that added up, if you paid the right kind of attention, to knowing what a storm was about to do before it did it.

There was a physical piece to it too, one I've never once explained to a review board's satisfaction, because there's no clean unit to measure it in. I still catch myself doing it without thinking, out in a field, on a shoulder, anywhere the ground's loose enough to cooperate: bend down, take a handful of dirt, let it go loose through my fingers instead of dropping it all at once, and read the way it scatters on the way down, which direction it drifts, how long it hangs before it settles. Doesn't sound like science. Isn't, not in any form a review board would ever sign off on. But dirt falling wrong, catching a current that shouldn't exist yet by any reading on any instrument, told me things ten, sometimes twenty minutes before our own equipment agreed with it on paper. Laurence started calling me the human barometer the first time he watched me call a shift off a fistful of dust instead of off any of his own gear, and it stuck the way the good nicknames always do, half joke and half plain fact, because it was both at once.

Academic meteorology took a long time to forgive me for learning the field that way instead of the approved way. I spent years being the guy with the state-school degree and the highway miles instead of the postdoc, tolerated at conferences more than welcomed, until enough of my sparse-data calls turned out right that tolerance quietly became something closer to respect.

NASA found me the way NASA found most of this program's exceptions, apparently: someone read a paper of mine about extracting real signal out of catastrophically incomplete atmospheric data, the kind of paper written for people chasing tornadoes on a two-lane highway, and realized it described, almost exactly, the exact problem a probe skimming past an alien planet at a fraction of light speed was going to hand somebody someday. Pathfinder wasn't going to orbit. It wasn't going to linger. It was going to get one clean pass and a handful of seconds of usable data, and somebody was going to have to build a real, actionable picture of an entire planet's weather out of scraps thinner than anything I'd ever worked with on Earth, and do it with actual human lives riding on getting it right.

I said yes before Truman finished the sentence explaining what the job actually was. I already had the shape of it before he'd even gotten to the part where he expected me to hesitate. He looked a little thrown by that, honestly, like he'd rehearsed a whole second paragraph of persuading me that he suddenly didn't need. I told him not to feel bad about it. Man spends his whole career reading a sky that never once phones ahead to tell you what it's about to do, a job offer's about the easiest thing you'll ever have to read.

He asked me once, point blank, why anybody should trust a read that came out of a fistful of dirt instead of a model. I gave him the only honest answer I had. I told him I'd said "saddle up" to my own team more times than I could count, over the years, on calls I couldn't have defended to a review board if my life had depended on it, and not one of them had ever once asked me to explain myself first. "You got it, boss," and they were already moving. That's not ego. That's just what happens when you've been right often enough, for long enough, that nobody's got a reason left to hesitate. He wrote that down, actually wrote it down, like it was the part of the interview that mattered most.

I think about my father sometimes, reading Pathfinder's telemetry, turning the sparse and the contradictory into something usable the same patient way he used to read a wall cloud from our own front porch. I don't know if this is what he'd have wanted for me. I know it's the only version of this work I ever actually wanted for myself, and that the two things turned out, eventually, to be the same thing.

✳

Chapter 8: Harding: Six days after the wedge came through Coyote County, the seven of us were still finding pieces of Dorothy V scattered in trees a quarter mile from where we'd lost her, and none of us had said much of anything worth remembering out loud yet.

Dorothy V was the whole apparatus: the steel drum and its hundreds of small sensor pods, the release rig, the mast of anemometers and wind vanes bolted around the frame reading the approach in real time, the beacon, the antenna, all of it strapped down in the bed of my own truck, brand new three weeks earlier, replacing the one the tornado took from me the last time any of this actually worked. Jo and I had designed her together, years back, before Wichita and everything that came after it pulled me away from actually building anything myself, and Jo's own team had built the physical units off that same design, one after another, exactly because they'd known from the start how many of them the work was probably going to cost. This was the fifth one off that line, built the same week as the rest and held in reserve until now. Jo drove, same as she always had, and I rode the release rig myself in the bed behind her, both of us running the same two-person sequence neither of us had apparently ever actually forgotten how to work together, and this was the first time she'd actually gone all the way through one clean, every pod transmitting, every reading intact, right up to the moment we ran out of time getting clear. I got the release fully cycled and we bailed, both of us clear of the cab before the wedge ever actually reached the truck itself, and it still wasn't distance enough. The wedge crossed our position directly, took the truck bed and the drum both, and scattered the wreckage across three different fence lines. We'd wanted this exact data for years. Getting it cost me a second truck in as many weeks, and cost Preacher most of the feeling in his left hand where a piece of the drum's own housing had caught him on the way past, and none of us were in any real hurry to celebrate yet.

We were in what was left of Meg's place, the pole barn, the one structure on the whole property that had come through the other tornado standing, gear spread out across two workbenches doing double duty now as the only flat surfaces she had left. Sorting salvage into piles that made sense to nobody but us, watching the six of them work in the kind of silence that only comes after everybody's nearly died together, I already knew I was looking at something that had started ending, even before anybody said so out loud. Everybody in the gang had called her Aunt Meg since long before I ever joined it, an honorary title she'd earned the old-fashioned way, by feeding and housing and occasionally bailing out every single one of us at one point or another, and by never once asking to be paid back for any of it.

Meg: I'm sixty-one years old, and I have chased more storms than I can accurately count anymore, and I saw the one I actually wanted to see six days ago, so I am done, plainly, so nobody spends the next year wondering if I meant it.

I lost the house days before that, and I want it on the record that the storm that took it and the wedge that just took Dorothy V aren't the same event in my own accounting, whatever the timing looks like from the outside. One made me finally admit I was tired. The other made me admit I still had one more good look left in me before I let myself be. Both things are true. I've stopped expecting anybody to find that tidy.

I taught Bill everything his own father didn't get the chance to finish teaching him, and I did it because he was worth teaching, not because I owed anybody's memory a debt. He surpassed me a long time ago in the actual reading of a storm, which I've never once resented, whatever he might tell you. What I taught him was patience. The reading, he built entirely himself, out of grief and stubbornness and more nights on a truck hood than any sane person's spine should have to forgive.

I'm not sad, watching this end. I chased the big one for thirty years and I finally got a clean look at her, and I walked away standing upright, which is more than a great many people in my line of work ever get to say. That's not a tragedy. That's the whole rare, lucky shape of a life well spent doing something worth doing.

Jo: I watched that wedge take the only thing I ever built that worked exactly the way I'd drawn it, twice now, in one lifetime, and it doesn't get any easier the second time, whatever anybody tells you about growing a thicker skin for it.

We designed her together, back before either of us had any business designing anything, back when we were both still trying to prove something to a field that had already decided neither of us was worth listening to. He moved on to Wichita not long after, chasing a career instead of a storm for a while, and it was my own team that actually got her built, real, unit by unit, on my own time, off a design neither of us had ever quite let go of even while we weren't speaking. Losing this one doesn't undo everything the rest of them already proved. I know that. I still stood in that field longer than I needed to, watching the last piece of her come down out of a tree, before I could make myself go help sort the rest.

I'm not the one deciding anything tonight. Meg's earned her ending. Laurence's about to earn his beginning. I'm just the one who's sat through enough of these nights to know this gang doesn't hold its shape forever, and that whatever comes next for the rest of us is going to ask something real of every single person in this barn, whether we're ready for it yet or not.

Laurence: I built the relay rig that actually caught all of it, this exact one, still in one piece in the truck bed we didn't lose, and I am not, generally, an emotional person about hardware, but I will admit I sat with the recording a solid five minutes before I could make myself start logging it properly.

It worked. That's the part I keep coming back to. Six years of relays that half-worked, mostly-worked, worked right up until the exact moment it mattered and then didn't, six years of building toward a day I genuinely didn't know I'd ever actually see, and the one time everything actually rode on it, mine caught every single pod's signal clean, start to finish, zero dropout, the first time any of us had ever actually gotten that. Bill and Jo designed Dorothy together, years before I ever showed up to this gang, and Jo's team built her real, every piece, the same week as the rest of the line, years after that, and I've spent six years just trying to build something worthy of actually hearing her properly. I've got an offer sitting in my inbox from an actual aerospace instrumentation lab, the kind of place that would've laughed me out of the building three years ago, and I think I'm finally going to answer it, because I built something that worked when it counted, and I want to find out what I can build next when somebody hands me an actual budget instead of a mother's garage and a credit card.

I'm going to miss this more than I'm letting myself admit right now. I'm also, if I'm honest, more excited than I've been in years. Both of those are true at once, and I've stopped expecting that to stop being confusing.

Mara: I drove every mile of every chase this gang ever ran, and I'll keep driving after the rest of you scatter, because I don't actually know how to stop, and I stopped needing anybody else in this barn to understand why, a good while back.

I'm not built for the version of this life that ends with a garage full of salvage and a decision to walk away clean. I watched Meg get her clean ending and I was happy for her, and watching it, I knew it isn't going to be mine. There's always another season. Another cell building somewhere past the horizon that nobody's read right yet. I don't know how to want anything else, and I've stopped apologizing to myself for that.

I'll miss all of you specifically. I won't miss this particular life, because I'm not actually leaving it. Somebody has to keep driving, and it was always going to be me.

Preacher: I've got two fingers on my left hand that don't answer the phone anymore, so to speak, and I'll say plainly that I consider that a fair price, all things considered, for the best look at a wedge any of us ever got in six years of trying.

I'm the one who doesn't get a clean ending or a bright new beginning, and that settled into me easier than I expected it to. I'm going home to my wife, who has waited eleven years for me to say a sentence like the one I'm about to say, and I'm going to say it: I'm done chasing anything I can't feel with both hands anymore. Not because the storm took something from me. Because it gave me what I came for, and a man's allowed to recognize when a debt's been paid in full and walk home instead of asking for more.

I'll tell this story for the rest of my life, missing fingers and all, and I intend to tell it well.

Dusty: I'm twenty-three years old and I got launched about eleven feet by a gust that peeled a tin roof off a grain silo like it was wrapping paper, and the second thing I felt, right after landing, was pure, uncomplicated joy, because I am, by every clinical measure Reyes would probably apply to me if she ever got the chance, not entirely right in the head about any of this, and that's just who I turned out to be, plain as anything.

Six years chasing with this gang and I still can't wait for the next one. Meg's earned her rest and Preacher's earned his and I understand both of those completely and want no part of either one. Wake me up the second something's building. I'll have my boots on before anybody's finished saying the word tornado.

Rabbit: I called the wedge's exact heading off a hand-drawn line on a paper map eleven seconds before the truck radio confirmed the same number, and that map was rolled, not folded, same as every map I've touched since the fight I had with Laurence about it three days before we ever left for Coyote County. He still thinks I overreacted about Wichita. I don't. A crease through the wrong county costs you real seconds when it actually matters, and on the one night it actually mattered, that exact habit is the reason Mara had us moving before the number ever came through official.

I don't chase for the rush. I know that's not the popular thing to admit around this particular table. I chase because somebody in every truck has to be the one still counting exits while everyone else is watching the sky, and I decided a long time ago I was going to be good at the job nobody else wanted. Tonight it almost wasn't enough, and I keep circling back to that word, almost, because almost is the only reason I'm sitting in this barn instead of standing in a field explaining to somebody's family why the map wasn't fast enough this time.

I don't know what I do once this gang scatters the way it's about to. I've never once chased for the storm itself, if I'm honest with myself about it. I chased for exactly this: everybody left, crowded around whatever table somebody could still find the next morning, alive enough to argue about something that doesn't matter at all. I'll take that over a clean look at the big one any day of the week, and I don't care who at this table thinks less of me for saying so.

Truman: I saw the house before I saw anyone in it, what was left of it, a chimney and half a staircase standing alone in a field the tornado had otherwise scraped clean, somebody's whole kitchen still hanging in the tree line a quarter mile out like the storm hadn't finished deciding whether to give any of it back.

I'd read the county's own preliminary damage assessment on the drive down, numbers on a page, the kind of report I'd read a hundred times before about a hundred other places. It hadn't prepared me for the actual smell of it, wet insulation and cut lumber, or for the sound underneath the sound of my own tires on the gravel: nothing. No power tools yet, no crews, just wind moving through a structure that used to keep wind out.

What I hadn't expected, coming around the last bend in that drive, was the table. Somebody had dragged sawhorses and a sheet of plywood out from wherever they'd survived and set up breakfast in the middle of it, eight people crowded around a griddle running off a propane tank, laughing about something I couldn't hear yet from that distance. I sat in the car a full minute before I made myself get out, trying to understand what I was actually looking at. Not denial. Not shock wearing a strange face. Just people who'd already decided, faster than I ever could have, that the only sane answer to a morning like that one was breakfast, together, out loud, before anybody let themselves feel the rest of it.

I got out of the car eventually. It took me longer than I'd have admitted to anyone standing there watching.

Harding: I didn't have my own piece ready to say out loud that night, still turning over what Meg's ending and Laurence's beginning and Mara's staying all meant sitting in the same barn together, and I still didn't have it ready the next morning, when Aunt Meg put out a breakfast big enough to feed twice our number off a two-burner camp stove and a folding table dragged out of the barn, same as she'd apparently decided to keep doing the morning after any chase that had come close enough to actually scare her, house or no house.

Eggs, biscuits, a whole skillet of sausage gravy, the good coffee she only broke out for occasions she considered worth marking properly, and all eight of us crowded around that folding table passing dishes and not talking much about the wedge at all, which was its own kind of talking about it. That was when the car pulled up outside, government plates, entirely too clean for the mud every one of our own trucks had tracked across what was left of that yard for a week straight.

Aunt Meg clocked it coming up the drive before any of the rest of us did. "Well," she said, not looking up from the burner, "somebody's lost."

Nobody was lost. The man who got out introduced himself as Truman, NASA, standing there in a suit that had no business anywhere near a working farm, let alone one still missing its own house, and got about four words into explaining himself before Aunt Meg cut him off entirely.

"You can talk once you've eaten something," she said, already dragging a second folding chair over next to Laurence. "Nobody negotiates anything serious around me on an empty stomach. Sit."

He looked, for one honest second, like a man who'd planned out how this conversation was going to go and had not planned for this particular obstacle. Then he sat, and didn't argue again, which told me something about him before he'd said a single useful word about why he'd actually come.

He ate like he meant it, too, which told me something else. Two biscuits, a full plate of the gravy, and he was three bites into the second biscuit before Dusty decided he'd been quiet long enough.

Dusty passed him the biscuits before anybody had properly introduced him to the table. "Pass the gravy, Extreme," he said, over Truman's head, like the man in the government suit sitting between them wasn't even there.

Truman looked up from a biscuit he hadn't been offered yet. "Extreme?"

"Because he is," Dusty said, like that settled the whole question on its own.

"Man is the single most reckless human being I have personally ever chased weather with," Preacher said, "and I have chased weather with some genuinely unwell people."

"Second most," I said. "There's a name in front of mine on that particular list, and everybody at this table knows exactly whose."

"Oh, here we go," Mara said, not looking up from her coffee.

Jo pushed back from the table, coffee still in hand, before the story had even properly started. "I've heard four different versions of this exact story in the six days I've been back around this table," she said, "and I'm not sitting through a fifth before I've had actual water today." She was already moving toward the tank rigged up behind what was left of the barn's back wall before she added, without turning around, "Don't let him talk anybody down from the honest version. I mean it."

"I always tell the honest version," I said, to her back.

"You have never once told the honest version in your life," she said, not breaking stride, and it landed sharper than the joke it was pretending to be.

"So we get this one near Daleton, right?" Dusty said, undeterred, "and we've got a wedge on the ground, and we are way too close, closer than any of us should've admitted to being at the time, and out of absolutely nowhere this shitty-looking green Valiant comes fishtailing around the bend and about takes off what was left of Mara's front end."

Rabbit set his fork down. "Oh, God," he said, to no one, already knowing exactly where this was going.

"There wasn't a front end left to take off by that point in the season," Mara said.

Beltzer pointed his own fork at me, delighted, like a man presenting evidence. "He's naked."

"Butt naked," Rabbit said, upgrading his own testimony for the record.

"Naked," Beltzer agreed.

"NOT naked," I said. "I was NOT naked!"

Beltzer leaned toward Truman, not quite quietly enough to actually count as an aside. "He was without apparel."

"Half naked," I said.

"Naked," Dusty said, final word on the subject, and the whole table laughed.

"And he's got a bottle in his hand," Dusty said, riding straight over the objection, "a full bottle, and to this day I do not know where a man wearing absolutely nothing was also keeping room to carry a bottle, and he walks straight past every one of us, straight at the wedge, calm as church, and holds it up like he's fixing to make a toast."

"I was making a barometric point," I said.

"You were not making a barometric point," Dusty said. "You looked that thing dead in whatever a tornado's got for an eye, and you said, 'you look thirsty,' and you drank a good third of it yourself first, and then you pitched the rest straight in, and it never came back down."

"Caught it clean," Preacher said. "Sucked it right up."

"It's a magic trick," Dusty said, "and it's the only one you've got, and it's why we call you what we call you, and no review board in this country is ever going to talk you out of it, so stop trying."

"For what it's worth," I said, "there was another Bill. An evil Bill. And I killed him."

Aunt Meg came back with a fresh plate she hadn't strictly needed to make and set it down in front of Truman without comment. "That man they're describing doesn't live anywhere near me," she said. "That one only shows up when something's actually about to try and kill him. You got the quiet one today. Count yourself lucky."

"I'd still like to hear more about the bottle," Truman said, and he meant it.

"Later," Meg said, in a tone that ended the matter, and refilled Truman's coffee a second time without being asked.

It was a solid fifteen minutes before she finally nodded at him like a woman granting a permit.

"All right," she said. "Now you can talk business."

"I read your data," he said, to me specifically, like he already knew exactly who at that table he needed to be talking to. "The pressure signature you pulled out of the wedge, six days ago. Most of it shouldn't have been readable at all, by any standard model. You read it anyway."

"That's the whole job," I said. "Nothing about it's supposed to be readable. You learn to read it anyway or you don't do the job at all."

"Your own team's file called you the human barometer," he said, not quite a question. "I wanted to know if that was a nickname or an actual working method before I drove out here."

"Little of both," I said. "Ask me again once you've watched it happen instead of just read about it."

I got up from the table, still holding my coffee, and walked out toward what was left of the foundation with him following, curious despite himself. Bent down, took a handful of the yard's own dirt, loose and dry from a week without rain, and let it go through my fingers slow instead of all at once, watching the way it caught, drifted, hung a beat longer on one side than the other before it settled.

"Front's building faster than anybody's forecast has it," I said, straightening back up. "Eighteen, twenty hours out. Nothing on your phone's weather app is going to agree with me on that for at least another six."

He didn't say anything for a second, looking at the dirt still settling around my boots like it might explain itself further if he waited long enough.

"I'll want that in writing eventually," he said. "For now, I'll just remember you said it. I've got a problem that works the same way, as it happens. Different sky. Same actual problem, underneath it. I'd like to talk to you about it, if you've got the time."

I looked around that table once, at Meg already at peace with an ending, at Laurence halfway into a beginning he'd earned honestly, at Mara who was never going to stop no matter what any of the rest of us did next, at Dusty already half done with a second plate and utterly unbothered by any of it. Looking at all four of them at once, I finally had an answer to the question none of us had said out loud yet.

"I've got the time," I said.

"Good," Aunt Meg said, refilling his coffee a second time without being asked. "Then you can finish that plate while he tells you about it. I didn't cook all this so it'd go cold over business."

The front I'd read off that handful of dirt arrived almost exactly on schedule, eighteen hours later, right as Truman and I were still working through the actual shape of what he was offering. Mara was already moving before the first outflow boundary showed up on anybody's radar, same as always, truck loaded, gear checked, that particular restless energy she carried into every chase she'd ever run.

"You coming?" she asked, at the door, and hearing the question, I realized it was the first time in eleven years anybody in that gang had needed to actually ask it instead of just assuming the answer.

"Not this one," I said.

Nobody made a thing of it, exactly, which somehow made it land harder than if they had. Laurence stopped halfway through loading a case of spare sensors and just looked at me a second, working something out silently before he nodded once and kept loading. Aunt Meg didn't say a word, just came over and put her hand flat against my cheek for a moment, the same gesture she'd have given a kid heading off to his first day of school, and went back to clearing the table like that settled it, because for her it did.

Mara paused longest, keys already in hand. "Read it right, though? The front."

"Read it right."

"Then it'll still be there when you catch the next one," she said, which wasn't quite forgiveness and wasn't quite permission either, just Mara's own particular way of saying she understood, and drove off without waiting on anything more from me.

I stood at the edge of Aunt Meg's ruined yard and watched three trucks pull out down the same muddy road Truman's sedan had come in on, chasing a storm I'd called, for the first time in longer than I could remember, from the ground instead of from the middle of it. Watching them go, I knew how much of my old life I'd just quietly agreed to trade for whatever came next.

✳

Chapter 9: Cent: I don't have a speech. I told them that going in, and I meant it, and it turned out to be the most useful thing I said all night.

They put us on a stage with the kind of lighting that makes everyone look slightly more important than they actually feel, and I stood there in a row with Sterling and Truman while the President stepped up to the podium first, because that was the order we'd agreed on, and because I already knew, without needing it explained to me twice, why it mattered that he go first.

"Four years ago," he said, "a young woman pushed against a satellite tank because she believed she could, and the world got a little smaller for it."

I'd heard him rehearse pieces of it. Hearing it live, in front of actual cameras, was different. I felt Sterling shift slightly beside me, and I knew that feeling too. It's one thing to know your own history. It's another to hear it read back to you as the opening line of something enormous.

"We do not yet know if the method that took us to Mars and Europa can be scaled to the distance between stars," he said. "We have not proven it. What we have is a real number, a real destination, and a real, disciplined plan to find out honestly, one tested step at a time, the same way we have done everything else.

"The destination is Alpha Centauri, the nearest star system to our own. The program is called Project Reach."

I watched that name land across the room, watched a few reporters actually sit up a little straighter, and thought about the fact that nobody had forced that word into an acronym, and how much I appreciated that more than I could easily explain.

"The name is not an acronym," he went on, "and I want to be plain about why we chose it. This is about more than velocity. It is about the distance between what we can see and what we can actually touch. It is about whether a family's private courage can become something the whole world shares in, carefully, honestly, without anyone losing what makes it theirs to give. And it is about how far any of us are willing to reach, together, before we know for certain what's on the other end."

He stepped back, and Truman stepped up, and I watched him settle into it the same way I'd watched him settle into every hard room I'd ever seen him walk into: shoulders square, voice level, a man carrying more weight than he ever let show on his face.

"I want to talk for a minute about what this actually takes," he said, "not the poetry of it, the mechanics. Eleven thousand people, give or take, work for this agency. Every one of them is going to touch some part of getting a ship to another star, whether their name ever ends up on a mission patch or not. This isn't one woman's miracle. It never has been. It's a lot of ordinary, disciplined work, done by a lot of people who show up every day because they believe it's worth doing right." He paused, and something in his voice dropped a register, quieter, more his own. "I've spent my whole career watching what happens when we cut corners on that discipline. I don't intend to start now, on the biggest thing we've ever tried."

Then it was my turn, and I stepped up to the microphone with absolutely nothing prepared, which I'd told them going in was how it was going to be.

"I don't have a speech," I said. "I'm not going to pretend this is easy, or that we know how it ends, because that's not how any of this has ever actually worked. What I can tell you is what I told my own family before any of you knew about this: I want to know how far this goes. Not because I'm not scared. Because I've never once found a wall I didn't want to know the true shape of, and I'm not going to start pretending otherwise now that the whole world's watching."

I could feel the cameras on me, more of them than I usually let myself think about while I was actually talking.

"We're going to do this the way we've done everything else," I said. "Carefully. Honestly. One tested step at a time. I'm not promising you a miracle. I'm promising you the truth, whatever it turns out to be."

I stepped back. The President came forward one more time, and this was the part I hadn't heard rehearsed, the part he'd apparently been saving.

"You will not be reading about this after the fact," he said. "You'll all have the opportunity to watch this unfold. Live. Starting right now. The successes and the setbacks both, the same way you watched Mars and Europa. We owe you that, and we intend to keep owing it to you for as long as this takes."

The four of us stood there a moment after he finished, not quite a tableau, not quite anything so composed as that, just four people who'd each said their own true thing back to back, in front of the entire world, and meant every word of it.

I thought, standing there under those lights, about the word he'd chosen. Reach. I'd have picked something else myself, if anyone had asked me, something smaller, something that didn't sound so much like a promise. But standing there, I knew it wasn't really about the word yet. It would mean something different to me in a year than it did tonight. I just didn't know yet what that would be.

My phone was already lit up with texts by the time I got backstage, half the country's press corps still filing out front. Tara, first, same as always: Saw it live. Proud of you, you enormous idiot. Jade right behind her: SHE'S CRYING. SHE WON'T ADMIT IT BUT SHE'S CRYING. I stood there backstage laughing at my own phone in a dress I'd been told cost more than my first ballast rig, and thought that if the whole world was going to watch me do this, I was glad at least two people in it were going to keep making fun of me the entire way through.

✳

Chapter 10: Joe: I knew what it felt like to do it once. That turned out to be the worst possible thing to know, when I couldn't make it happen a second time.

They gave me a room at Kristen, a chair not unlike the ones they'd built for Cent, a handful of engineers with monitors and a whole lot of patience, and one very simple instruction: reach for her. On command. In a controlled setting, with everybody watching and measuring, so we'd know whether the thing that happened during the attack on mission control was real capability or a one-time miracle nobody could ever count on again.

I already knew it was real. I'd done it. I'd felt Cent and Davy both, half a solar system apart, in the same terrible second, and landed where I needed to be. I knew what it felt like from the inside, its exact shape, which made this so much worse than starting from nothing would have been.

"Whenever you're ready," someone said, over the comm.

I closed my eyes. I thought about Cent, specifically, deliberately, the way I'd have thought about a math problem if math problems mattered this much. I reached.

Nothing happened.

"Try again," the same voice said, patient, the way you're patient with someone you don't want to discourage. "No rush."

I tried again. And again after that. By the fifth attempt I could hear the patience in that voice starting to cost the man something to maintain, and I knew it, because it was costing me a great deal more.

"I don't get it," I said, out loud, mostly to myself. "I know what it felt like. I could describe it to you down to the second. I just can't make it happen again."

"That's actually useful information," someone else said, not unkindly. "It tells us something about the mechanism."

It didn't feel useful. It felt like standing in front of a locked door with the key sitting visibly on the other side of the glass, close enough to see every detail of it, no way through.

I didn't tell anyone how bad it was getting to sit with. Frustration isn't really the right word for it, though it's the word I used out loud. It was closer to grief, if I'm honest, grief for a version of myself that had, for about four seconds during the worst afternoon of my life, been exactly the person Cent needed. I wanted to be that person on purpose. Turned out wanting it badly enough wasn't the mechanism at all.

Davy found me after one of those sessions, sitting in the hallway outside the chair room, not saying much of anything.

"Rough one?"

"Rough five, actually. I know what it felt like, Davy. That's the part that's killing me. I'm not starting from nothing. I'm starting from knowing what I'm failing to do again."

He didn't say much. He nodded, and something in his face I didn't think much of at the time, and went on his way.

Two days later, he came to find me on the observation deck, casual about it in a way I should probably have questioned more than I did.

"Hey, come check this railing with me. I think there's a crack in the composite."

I followed him without thinking twice about it, because why would I. We were maybe ten feet from the rail when his boot caught on something, or seemed to, and he went down hard and fast, straight toward the edge, straight toward a two-hundred-foot drop with nothing at the bottom of it but the kind of landing a body doesn't walk away from.

I didn't decide anything. There wasn't a decision in it at all. One second I was watching him fall and the next I was standing next to him on solid ground, both of us on our feet, my heart going so hard I could feel it in my teeth.

"Davy, are you..."

He was already standing up, brushing himself off, completely unhurt, and something about the way he did it, too smooth, too unbothered for a man who'd just nearly died, made the relief in my chest curdle into something else before I'd even fully finished feeling it.

"You're fine," I said slowly. "You were never actually falling."

"I was never actually falling."

"You staged that." My voice came out flat, then not flat at all. "You staged that to test whether I'd catch you."

"I did."

"HOW COULD YOU!!"

He didn't flinch from it, didn't try to talk over me or soften what I'd just accused him of. He just answered it straight, and that was somehow worse than if he'd argued.

"That's not how I learned it," he said. "Not Cent either, or her mother. All three of us, real danger, no warning, no choice in it. I was escaping a group of men who meant to rape me. Yours was different. I made a choice. I manufactured it."

I stood there with all my anger and nowhere left to actually put it, because he wasn't defending what he'd done. He was just telling me the truth about it, straight, without hiding behind family tradition or good intentions.

"Why," I said, quieter now.

"Because I didn't know another way to get you there, and I was out of time to wait for one." He looked at me steady, no apology in his face, just the weight of the choice he'd made sitting there in the open where I could see all of it. "It worked. I'm not going to pretend I feel great about how."

I didn't have anywhere else to take the anger. It had nowhere left to go once I knew what it actually was: not cruelty, not a family habit passed down without thinking, just one costly decision a man had made because he'd run out of gentler ones.

"Don't do it again," I said finally.

"I won't need to." He held my eyes a second longer. "You've got it now. That's the whole reason it mattered."

I looked past him at the rail he'd never actually been in danger of going over, and felt something settle into place that had been loose in me since the mission-control attack.
