I hadn't imagined what I'd felt that day. It had a name now, a shape I could find again on purpose, even if it had taken someone else's manufactured fall to prove it to me.

Reaching for someone. That was the whole mechanism, as it turned out. Not a place. A person. I'd spent my whole life, without knowing it, building the one thing that could find her from any distance at all, and it had taken almost losing him, even fake, to finally teach me how to hold onto it on purpose.

✳

Chapter 11: Cent: The invitation came addressed to all of us, in language that actually read like an invitation. I noticed that before I noticed anything else about it, and I noticed how much it mattered to me that I'd noticed it at all.

Family, Truman, Sterling, Washington, a date. Nothing in the wording that even faintly resembled an order, and I read it twice just to be sure, because I'd learned not to take that particular kindness for granted anymore.

The private room, before any cameras, held just the four of us who actually needed to be there: me, the President, Truman, Sterling. I'd already given Truman and Sterling my answer, days ago, in my own kitchen, in about four words. This wasn't about changing it. I think I knew, walking in, that I needed to say it again anyway, somewhere it would actually be heard by the people who'd have to carry the weight of it alongside me.

"I already gave Truman and Sterling my answer," I said, once the formal part of the briefing had run its course and the room had gone quiet enough for me to actually say something real into it.

I let that sit a second.

"But it's been sitting with me since. And there's a song stuck in my head that has no business being stuck in my head. Not really my kind of thing. Caught it in some diner in the middle of nowhere, one line just wouldn't let go." I glanced toward Truman without quite meaning to. "Toby Keith. Any of you know him?"

Nobody answered. I hadn't really expected them to.

"It's not even about me, really. Guy's singing about soldiers, not whatever I am. But take all that away and it's just somebody saying he's not doing it for the money, and he's not doing it for anybody clapping. He's doing it because it landed on him and somebody has to. He doesn't want to die for it. But if it's actually asked of him, for real, he'll carry it without making a thing of it, because whatever's on the other side of that doesn't come free."

I looked at Truman when I said the last part. I don't think I could have said it to anyone else in that room and had it land the way I needed it to.

"Three men gave their lives reaching for the Moon," I said. "I don't see how I do any less."

Nobody said anything after that. The meeting closed the way meetings do, chairs scraping, papers gathering, the ordinary sounds of a room deciding it was finished. I was halfway to standing when Truman, still seated, said something meant only for me.

"...I didn't expect to hear that story again so soon."

Rough, when he said it. He didn't hide that it was.

"Figured it earned a second use," I said, dry, giving him room not to have to make more of it than he wanted to.

He nodded, short. "...Yeah."

That was all either of us needed to say. He'd told me that story once, in a room full of officials who needed to hear it to understand him, and I'd kept it since without either of us discussing that I would. Handing it back to him now, on purpose, felt like the truest thing I'd said all day, truer than the speech that came after, even though nobody but the two of us would ever know it happened.

The cameras came on after that, and the part the whole world actually saw was shorter, plainer, built for an audience that hadn't earned the diner and the song and Truman's rough voice saying he hadn't expected to hear his own story again so soon. I stood at a podium with the President beside me and said the only version of yes that mattered to the people watching.

"I'm going to be the one in the chair, on the first gate, on the date they've set." I kept it short on purpose. "That's my answer. That's the actual commitment, not the abstract one. I wanted you to hear it from me, directly, not through a press release."

It landed the way I'd hoped it would: clean, real, nothing dressed up about it.

Afterward, once the cameras were off and the reporters had gone wherever reporters go when the story's done for the day, we found a quiet corner and Dad pulled me in for the kind of hug that doesn't ask permission first. Mom's eyes were wet, and she didn't pretend otherwise. Joe just held my hand, quiet, the two of us not needing to say much of anything.

"Two yeses in one day," Dad said finally, a little rough himself. "That's a personal record, even for you."

"I only needed the one that counted."

"Which one was that?"

I thought about the diner, the song, Truman's rough voice in an empty room. I thought about the podium, the cameras, the clean short version everyone else got to keep.

"Both of them," I said. "For different reasons."

He didn't ask me to explain it further, and I loved him a little more for that, standing there in the quiet after a day that had asked more of all of us than any single day really should.

✳

Chapter 12: Osei: For two months I built Project Reach in full view of the entire world, which is a strange way to work, once you actually sit with it. Every weld, every mounted sensor, every EVA out at Kristen happened live, narrated for an audience that mostly already understood what they were watching, and I got used to hearing my own name on a broadcast feed faster than I ever expected to.

Heavy-lift launches, one after another, cargo manifests scrolling past a ticker that made the whole operation look like a company shipping stock instead of ballast frames and pressure-section mockups. I ran EVA crews out at Kristen myself, floodlit against the dark, maneuvering hardware into position while a commentary track explained my own work back to me in real time, half a beat behind whatever I'd actually just said over the comm loop.

That two months ended the day the first instrumented ballast article, the one everyone had started calling Alpha without any ceremony attached to the name, sat locked into the fixture cradle at Kristen, floodlit, waiting.

I ran through frame status out loud for that one, load cells, thermal, position sensors, nav solution, each one confirmed and logged before I moved to the next. Truman stood off to one side, present in person, unasked, the way I was starting to understand he'd always be for the milestones that mattered to him personally rather than the ones that just needed a signature. Cent stood near the fixture, quiet, watching the checklist the way she always did before a first: absorbing it, not rushing me through it.

"Go/no-go," Truman said, when I finished. "Engineering."

"Go."

"Nav."

"Go."

"Propulsion."

"Go," she said, and I watched something in her settle when she said it, the particular kind of steadiness she carried into a first that hadn't earned its own routine yet.

The test itself went the way a well-designed test is supposed to go, which is to say almost boring to actually watch. She took Alpha from Kristen out to the marked holding point first, the same deep-space nothing NASA had cleared for exactly this, and only kicked it up through the increment once both of them were well clear of anything either could hit. I watched the telemetry instead of the sky, coupling data clean, no wobble, no resistance beyond the honest mass of the article itself. She jumped clear at the top of the profile and let it coast, and I spent thirty minutes watching numbers instead of breathing normally, until independent nav confirmed her return position to the meter and she braked it back down, easy, controlled. My own post-test inspection came back clean across every line on the clipboard.

"Good run," Truman said, and meant it, no flourish attached.

I stood there afterward, running my own two months back through my head, all of it lived from the inside this time instead of narrated over a broadcast feed by somebody who understood the machine less than I did. It was strange, realizing how much of what the world had watched, I'd actually built with my own hands, one weld and one sensor mount at a time, long before any of it ever showed up on a screen as a finished thing.

I was still looking at my own data when I said the thing that mattered more than anything else that happened that day, though none of us knew it yet.

"We're going to have a problem eventually," I said, not looking up, in exactly the tone of an engineer noting a load path before it becomes a crisis rather than warning anyone about one. "Two-axis chair's fine for what we're doing today. It's not going to hold up once we need real off-axis correction. Lateral compensation, insertion burns, anything that isn't a straight line." I made a note on the tablet. "Something to think about before it's a problem instead of an observation."

Nobody made much of it in the moment. I filed it away myself, the way you file away a sentence that sounds like it's going to matter later, without knowing yet how.

The gate closed clean. First real step past Momentum's ceiling, boring by design, exactly the way a test is supposed to go before anyone lets it get interesting.

✳

Chapter 13: Cent: We had a rule by the end of the mass campaign, and it came from somewhere none of us expected, and once it existed nobody in the entire program ever pushed past it again.

The methodology itself was simple enough to explain and hard enough to actually live inside. Every new mass tier got two questions instead of one. First: can you push it at all, a conservative first coupling, nothing fancy. Second, once the first question had a clean answer: how hard. That was where the real climbing happened, kick magnitude escalating right alongside the mass itself instead of holding steady while only the number in front of it grew.

Both redlines ran in parallel through every single "how hard" tier. My own body, watched close, telemetry on everything that could be watched. The article and the fixture, watched just as close. Whichever one flagged trouble first governed that tier, full stop, no averaging the two together and hoping for the best. We stopped the instant either line showed warning signs, projected the actual redline from the trend data rather than confirming it by breaking something, or breaking me, and derated down from there before we ever moved to the next step.

It worked, mostly, which is its own kind of dangerous, because mostly is exactly the condition under which people start trusting a process a little more than the process has actually earned.

Four hundred eighty metric tons. Not a special number going in, just the next ordinary step in a climb that had gone clean for a good long while by then.

"Full effort," I said, before we started the push-magnitude escalation for that tier, dry, not shaking at all when I said it. "Let's see what full effort actually does."

"It kills me," I said, answering my own question, because nobody else in the room was going to say it out loud for me. "That's what it does."

Nobody laughed. Nobody stopped, either. We went into the escalation exactly the way we'd planned it, and the failure hit sooner than the modeling had predicted, a load path nobody had flagged as marginal giving out clean and sudden on the ballast article itself. Not my fixture. Not my chair. The article's own structure gave before my body ever came close to its own limit, which was the whole reason nobody got hurt: I was well inside my own bounds the entire time. The structural redline failed. Mine never did.

Osei took it the way I'd come to expect from her by then. I watched the gut-punch of it cross her face for about half a second, an engineer realizing something got missed, and then watched discipline overtake it almost as fast. She wasn't discouraged by the number. She was clarified by it.

Truman wasn't there for that one. He heard about it by report instead, which felt worth noting after how present he'd been for the boring first gate. I got it, though. Some milestones he needed to witness in person. This one, he trusted the process enough to take secondhand, which was its own kind of vote of confidence, even if nobody said so out loud.

We redesigned, tested again, moved on. The montage of it compressed fast in my own head even while I was living through it, tier after tier climbing clean.

But the real conversation didn't happen until later, once the redesign had checked out and everyone was ready to call the incident closed.

"We fixed the frame," someone said, in the debrief, and I let that sentence sit for exactly as long as it took me to decide I wasn't going to let it stand unchallenged.

"We fixed the frame," I said. "That's the easy part. Here's the actual question. A load path nobody flagged just failed, without warning, on a structure we'd tested and trusted. What makes anyone in this room confident my own body's warning signs are any more reliable than that frame was?"

Nobody had a good answer ready. I watched them not have one, and pressed the point while I still had it.

"I've been telling you when I'm close to my own limit. That's what the whole methodology's been built on, my own self-report plus the telemetry. But the frame didn't know it was close to its limit either, right up until it wasn't. I don't actually know that I'd catch my own version of that in time. I don't think any of us know that."

The room went to work on it properly after that, the real fix, not just a patched frame. Modeling and extrapolation from structural and biomechanical data before we ever tested live again, instead of finding the line empirically on my own body. Automated abort thresholds, independent of my own reflexes or self-report, telemetry-triggered, set well below anything resembling real danger. The derating margin itself stopped being generic engineering caution and became something specific: margin that existed because of this exact conversation, not despite it.

The President had been listening to the whole debrief, patched in rather than physically present, which by then was just how things worked. Continual review, ambient, not a rule anyone announced so much as a habit that had settled in since Momentum and never left. He came on the line once the room had worked through the fix.

"Remind me to put 'agreed with a kid over breakfast and rewrote federal test policy' on the schedule for next time," he said, and I heard the humor in it, real but light. Then it dropped out of his voice entirely, flat and serious in a way that changed the whole room's posture.

"I already watched people decide her body was an acceptable place to run an experiment," he said. "I am not doing that again. Not accidentally, not procedurally, not even with her own say-so. Nobody comes close to hurting her to prove a number. Not ever again."

A beat.

"Not on our dime."

Nobody said anything after that for a while. Sitting there, I saw what he'd drawn a line around. My own right to take on operational risk was mine, everywhere else, and always would be. This was different. A test built to approach my own injury threshold wasn't an operational risk I was choosing to accept. It was a program design decision, and my consent didn't make a bad design acceptable. He'd just made that the actual policy, not a sentiment, and I don't think anyone in that room ever forgot it again.

The rest of the campaign moved faster from there, the discipline holding all the way through, the ritual phrase recurring at every "how hard" tier from that point forward, said straight now instead of dry, because everyone understood by then that it was literally true and we proceeded anyway, eyes open.

We landed, eventually, on the numbers that would define the whole rest of the program. Seven hundred metric tons, repeatedly demonstrated, clean every time. Roughly seven hundred sixty projected as the real redline, never once deliberately reached. Six hundred certified after derating, the number that would actually govern flight. The mass campaign closed there, and closed clean, and I carried the President's line with me longer than I carried most things anyone said to me that whole year.

Not on our dime.

✳

Chapter 14: Quincy: Four hundred eighty metric tons, and I want it noted for the record that I flagged the load path in question three weeks before it failed.

Not as a prediction. I want to be precise about that too, since precision is the entire point of what I do and I will not have this story told any other way afterward. I flagged it as a gap in the data: a joint in the ballast article's frame that had never actually been tested to failure, only ever tested to comfortably below it, with the rest extrapolated from a model that assumed a linear relationship the underlying material had never been shown to actually have. I said, in a memo that as far as I can tell three people ever read, that we did not know where that joint failed. We only knew where it hadn't, yet.

Nobody acted on it, and I understood why at the time, even if I didn't care for it. The program was climbing clean, tier after tier, and a memo about an untested joint reads, in the middle of a clean climb, like an academic being an academic. I've been that particular kind of unwelcome before. I generally write the memo anyway.

The joint gave exactly where I'd said it might, exactly the way I'd said it might, sudden and clean, with nothing in anyone's instrumentation built to catch it coming. Nobody got hurt. That was margin and good fortune working together, not evidence that the gap in the data hadn't been real the entire time.

I watched Osei's face in the half second right after it happened, the particular sequence I'd come to recognize in good engineers: the gut-punch of realizing something got missed, then discipline overtaking it almost as fast. She wasn't discouraged by the number. She was clarified by it. I made a note of that too, separate from the memo, because I've found it's worth remembering exactly who in a given room turns a failure into information instead of into blame, and who does the reverse.

I sat in on the debrief. I watched her ask the question that actually mattered before anyone else in the room had worked out how to ask it: what makes anyone confident her own body's warning signs are any more reliable than the frame's had just proven to be. It was, without qualification, the correct question, and I watched a room full of very good engineers discover, in real time, that they didn't have an answer ready that would survive being said out loud twice.

I did, or close enough to one. I'd been building it for three weeks, ever since the memo nobody read. Modeling and extrapolation from structural and biomechanical data, run forward before any live test, instead of found empirically on a human body that only gets to fail once. I said as much once the room had finished sitting with the question properly, and for once in my career, nobody in it treated the word extrapolation like a hedge instead of a method.

The derating margin that came out of that conversation wasn't generic caution dressed up as policy. It was, specifically, the gap my memo had described, closed properly this time, with real work behind it instead of an assumption nobody had gotten around to testing. I don't say any of this to claim credit that isn't owed me. I say it because I think it matters, going forward, that the fix existed in draft form three weeks before the failure that finally made anyone read it.

One more note, unrelated to the failure itself, that I put in writing that same week and have not raised since, because nobody had a use for it yet: every redline we were deriving assumed a single point of force, hers, alone. Nothing in the underlying physics actually requires that. Two or more synchronized kicks, timed and vectored together, would not simply add their individual ceilings. Properly coupled, the combined structural tolerance scales faster than the sum of its parts, the same way two hands, or four, can carry a weight no single hand could manage alone without straining as hard as it would solo. I have no one to test that theory with. I filed it anyway, the way I file everything I believe will matter to somebody, someday, whether or not I'm the one who ends up proving it.

✳

Chapter 15: Cent: The third axis didn't move like a boost. It moved like heavy machinery finding an alignment, slow and mechanical, and I stood outside the fixture the first time I watched it happen and knew I was looking at something new.

Osei's team had built the ground rig fast, once her observation from the first ballast push turned into an actual line item. Full articulation, lock, position-sensing, all of it run clean before anyone strapped into anything. I watched the gimbal walk itself through its own range, mechanical locks engaging one after another with a solid, satisfying finality, load-path data streaming across a screen I only half understood the units of.

Truman was there, same as always by then. Not because this was a first, I'd learned, not because he'd assigned himself to milestones specifically. He was just around, the way he was around for a lot of things that had nothing to do with ceremony.

"Dude," I said, at one point, mostly teasing, "do you ever get time to do your own job?"

"You are my job," he said, not looking up from the readout. "Since the President got involved, this stopped competing with mission control for my hours. This is mission control now."

I didn't have a good answer for that, so I let it sit.

Green, when it finally came, meant something new too. Both the scheduler's release and a clean sensor vote, together, before the light would show anything but yellow. First time we'd actually needed both conditions at once instead of one standing in for the other, and I watched the board hold at yellow a beat longer than I expected while the second condition caught up.

Then I strapped in myself, for the first manual non-axial boost, and that was where the real newness actually lived.

Two axes, in Momentum, had meant a magnitude and not much else. Point it and push. This was different. I had to choose a vector now, an actual direction through three-dimensional space instead of a straight line I already understood in my body, and the first attempt felt like relearning how to walk after someone quietly rearranged my legs.

"Oh, I know this feeling!" I said, out loud, surprising myself with how much I meant it. "I used to love those three-ring things they'd strap you into at the carnival when I was a kid! Spin you around on three axes at once until you couldn't tell up from down!"

Nobody in the room had expected that particular reference, and I watched a couple of people fight not to laugh. It felt good, honestly, finding something ordinary and human in the middle of all that hardware, a piece of my own childhood showing up uninvited in a room full of engineers.

The amber light tripped mid-session, small and organic, nothing planned about it. Not the deliberate fault test we had scheduled for later. Just a real disagreement somewhere in the sensor chain, minor, caught exactly the way it was supposed to be caught.

And with it, for the first time, I felt the tingler.

It startled me more than the light ever had. I'd spent years learning to read a display without flinching, learning to trust a green or distrust a red with nothing more than my eyes doing the work. This was different, a physical thing happening directly to my own body, impossible to look away from the way you can look away from a screen. I held instead of forcing anything, waited for a legitimate green instead of talking myself into one, and understood, small and important, how much I was going to have to trust this new layer of the system going forward.

The session closed clean after that, logged without drama, one more gate behind us. But I sat with the tingler a while after we'd wrapped, turning the feeling of it over in my mind, understanding it was going to become as familiar to me, eventually, as the light itself. I just didn't know yet how familiar, or how much I'd come to need it.

Osei found me still sitting there, unstrapped but not yet moving, and didn't ask what was wrong, just sat down across from me, patient, understanding that some things need a minute before they turn into words.

"First new sense," she said, eventually. "There's not really a manual for what that does to a person."

"Feels like my own body just started talking to me in a language I didn't know I already understood."

"That's about the best description of it I've heard." She stood, offered a hand up. "You'll get fluent. Give it time."

I let her pull me to my feet, and thought, walking out of that fixture bay, that fluent felt like a very long way off, and also, somehow, already closer than I expected.

Several sessions after that ran together in my memory into one long stretch of calibration, telemetry-driven alignment checked and rechecked against a center of mass that kept sliding as consumables burned down. Unglamorous work. Necessary work. The kind that doesn't make it into anyone's highlight reel but has to happen anyway before the interesting part can happen safely.

✳

Chapter 16: Quincy POV: The first time they fed a fault into a live test, I watched her miss it. Not by much, and not in any way that mattered operationally. But I watched the hesitation sit there in the telemetry a beat longer than the scripted timeline called for, and I made a note of it, because a beat is exactly the kind of thing that matters to someone whose entire job is noticing what a room full of specialists has just agreed to call negligible.

The engineers had built the scenario carefully: a deliberate sensor disagreement, live, meant to prove she could actually catch and handle a real amber-or-red decision under pressure instead of just complying with whatever the board told her. She'd have said, going in, that she was ready for it. She wasn't, not the first time. The scripted fault tripped and she sat with it a full beat too long working out what she was actually looking at, and by the time she'd sorted it, the test window had already logged the hesitation for the record.

"No shame in it," Osei told her afterward, and meant it. She was right, though nobody had asked me. That's what practice runs are for.

They ran a great many practice runs after that, and I watched the recognition sharpen each time, the hesitation shrinking down toward nothing, the kind of curve I'd normally expect from an instrument calibrating itself rather than a person. By the time we reached the run that actually mattered, I'd stopped expecting to learn anything new from it. I was wrong about that, same as everyone else in the room.

The inserted fault tripped exactly on schedule, same as every practice run before it, and needed nobody's attention at all. Underneath it, unscripted, unplanned, an actual second fault fired at almost the same instant, real hardware disagreeing with itself for reasons that had nothing to do with anyone's test plan. That was the one I caught, in the raw feed, a half second before anyone else in the room said a word, and for that half second I genuinely did not know whether we were about to watch a very expensive, very public failure.

She held. I watched her do it on the telemetry, nothing in her own biometrics that would have told a lesser observer she was under any particular strain at all. She didn't force either signal toward green just because green was the answer everyone in that room wanted. She let both alarms sit, and then she resolved them, correctly, in the correct order, using something that had nothing to do with the instrument panel in front of her.

"You held," Osei said afterward, reading back through the log with an expression I didn't often see on her, something close to relief mixed with something close to pride. "Both of them. In the right order."

"I didn't do it with my eyes," Cent said. "I don't think I could have."

"That's the whole reason we built the redundant channels the way we did," Osei said.

I found that answer incomplete, though I kept the observation to myself at the time. The redundant channels explained why a correct answer had been possible. They didn't explain how she'd arrived at it faster than any instrument in that room had managed to flag the second fault as real. I have spent a fair amount of my career around people who call something instinct when what they actually mean is a data channel nobody has built the instrumentation for yet. I made a note to raise that distinction properly, later, once the room had finished congratulating itself long enough to hear it.

Truman found her afterward, in the corridor outside the test bay, and I happened to be walking the same direction. He didn't say much beyond a single question. "How close was it?"

"Close enough that if I'd trusted my eyes over my hands, I don't think I'd have caught it in time."

He nodded slowly, filing that away the way I was coming to understand he filed everything. I filed it away too, differently. Fault insertion became standing procedure for the rest of the program after that day, written into every subsequent test plan without anyone needing to argue for it, and I made sure, quietly, that the case for treating her own reported sensation as a real instrument channel, not an anecdote, got written in right alongside it.

✳

Chapter 17: Osei POV: Kristen Station's water-and-fiber jacket had kept people alive for years. It was three feet of proven, boring, reliable engineering, and it was completely useless to me, because no hull built for 0.8c could afford to carry that much mass and still go anywhere.

I needed something else. Hydrogen-rich, low atomic number, something that could stand up to the modeled particle spectrum at a real fraction of light speed without adding the kind of weight that would eat the whole mission's margin before it ever left the ground. I pulled Quincy onto the team before I pulled anyone else, first call I made once the mass problem actually had a shape to it, because I wanted someone in the room from day one who didn't care whose department the answer came from, only whether it was correct.

My first instinct was the obvious one. A rigid, dense, hydrogen-rich composite, hard-plated, straightforward. We tested it against every stressor we could model, one at a time. Vacuum. Radiation. Thermal cycling. Clean numbers, every single test, every single time.

It failed anyway.

Not under any one condition. Only when we finally ran all three together, matching real flight conditions instead of testing them in isolation the way our schedule had quietly encouraged us to, did the material start embrittling in a way none of our individual tests had ever shown us. By the time a routine impact check found it, the panel didn't just crack. It spalled, shrapnel peeling off a surface that had passed every test we'd given it, and I stood there looking at the debris field on the test bench thinking about a cylinder from Momentum that had done almost exactly the same thing, in almost exactly the same way, for almost exactly the same reason.

We'd had every individual number we needed. We'd tested the material to death, in every single condition it would ever face, and still missed the failure, because nobody had thought to ask what all three conditions would do to it at once.

The diagnosis, once we found it, stalled us completely. The chemistry wasn't wrong. Low atomic number, hydrogen-rich, all the right radiation behavior. The mechanical philosophy underneath it was wrong: we'd built something that resisted force locally, right at the point of impact, instead of spreading that force out across a wider area where the material could actually absorb it without cracking. I knew the diagnosis. I didn't have the fix. Neither did anyone else on my team, Quincy included, and I didn't hold that against him, because being smarter than almost anyone alive was never the same thing as having personally handled body armor in a former life. Reasoning your way to an answer only gets you as far as the facts already sitting somewhere in your own head. I watched real frustration settle over a room full of very smart people who'd run out of obvious next moves anyway.

Iverson was in the room for something else entirely, some unrelated business with a nav lead, when he overheard us circling the same problem for the third time that afternoon.

"You're building armor," he said, not looking up from whatever he was actually there for.

"We're building shielding."

"Same problem, mostly." He glanced over. "Modern ballistic protection stopped using hard plates a long time ago, for exactly this reason. Rigid material resists locally, fails locally. You want something that's soft under ordinary handling and locks rigid the instant it's actually struck, spreading the hit across a much wider surface instead of taking it all in one spot. Shear-thickening gel. It's the whole reason body armor doesn't look like a knight's breastplate anymore."

He said it almost offhand, the way you mention something you never expected to need again in this particular life, training from a career I hadn't thought to consult at all when I put this team together.

It was, obviously, the answer.

We built the second idea from the ground up around that principle: a shear-thickening, hydrogen-rich gel-polymer composite, keeping the radiation chemistry that had already worked while finally giving it the force-distributing mechanical behavior the first idea never had. It passed the full combined-stressor retest clean, every condition running together this time, nothing hiding in the gaps between individual tests.

Someone started calling it not-water before the ink on the actual materials designation had even dried, and the real name barely got used again after that.

I felt the thing I'd learned to feel by then whenever a failure taught me something instead of just costing me something: not discouraged, clarified. But there was something new in it this time too, something I sat with a while after the retest came back clean. The fix hadn't come from inside my own discipline at all. It had come from a career soldier who happened to be in the room for an unrelated reason, carrying training none of us had thought to ask him about. Watching him go back to whatever he'd actually come in for, I finally saw why a mixed crew mattered for reasons that went well past anyone's individual job title.

✳

Chapter 18: Truman POV: Not-water solved the chemistry. It didn't solve the actual problem, which was that nobody on Earth could build a rig capable of proving the physics at the speeds that would actually matter.

A grain of dust at a real fraction of light speed isn't dust anymore. It's an impact event, and no two-stage light-gas gun in existence could throw anything close to that fast. Ours topped out around ten kilometers a second, which sounds respectable until you set it next to 0.8c and watch the number turn into a rounding error.

"It's enough to validate the sacrificial-screen physics," Osei said, in the review. "Stand-off distance, absorption-layer behavior, all of it checks out against the model at the speeds we can actually generate. It's not enough on its own. I want to be honest about that."

Nobody argued with her. I appreciated that more than I said out loud. We'd built a program, by then, where people said the honest thing even when it slowed them down, and I didn't take that for granted.

The President's policy from the mass campaign sat over the whole conversation without anyone needing to restate it. Not on our dime. We weren't going to find out the hard way, on a crewed flight, whether the extrapolation held. But I wasn't willing to just trust the model blindly either, not with something this important, not with this much distance between what we could test and what we actually needed to know.

"Let's let Cent get a test platform up to speed," I said, "and just watch it for a while."

Nobody rode the article. That was the whole point of building it the way we did: a small, dedicated, fully instrumented test piece, no passengers, nothing but telemetry and Cent's own two hands doing what she already knew how to do. She pushed it herself, incrementally, the same discipline that had carried the mass campaign all the way through.

First push took it to an already-proven baseline, ordinary interplanetary speed, nothing dramatic. She returned, and we checked two things in parallel: the article's own instrumented telemetry, and her own physiological baseline, the same redline logic from the mass campaign just pointed at velocity and effort instead of mass. It came back clean both directions. She went again, incremental, building on the coast already established rather than resetting to zero each time, another return, another baseline check.

We repeated that pattern for a good long while, staged and unhurried, until two separate threads of data finally converged into one answer instead of two open questions: the shielding's real, proven survivability envelope, and the velocity Cent could safely and repeatably deliver to get it there.

I pushed this one personally, more than institutionally, and I don't think I ever explained why to anyone in that room, though I suspect most of them already understood. I've watched inadequate shielding cost people before. More than once. It's part of the same pattern that's shaped most of my career, the same one that put me in this seat in the first place. I wasn't going to let this particular gap sit open a day longer than it had to, not when there was a way to close it honestly instead of just hoping.

Osei's team built and instrumented the article. Cent ran the actual campaign, tier by tier, exactly as planned. Real velocity data started arriving after that, steady, over time, feeding confidence into the extrapolation model instead of asking anyone to simply trust it on faith. It wasn't dramatic work. It was the kind of work that mattered more than the dramatic kind usually does, and I made sure, in every review after that, that everyone in the room understood why.

I watched the final tier's confirmation come back clean, months after we'd started, and felt something ease in my chest that had been tight since a night I never talked about, back before any of this, before NASA, before I learned that the only real defense against watching something happen helplessly was making absolutely certain it had already been tested past the point where helplessness could still find a way in.

✳

Chapter 19: Sterling POV: The signal dropped on an intermediate velocity gate, the kind of ordinary mid-campaign day nobody would have flagged in advance as worth remembering.

I'd run this console, or one like it, for the better part of my career, going back to a much smaller station and a much smaller version of everything this program had since become. I still thought, sometimes, in the quiet stretches between events, about how far a career built on watching other people take the actual risks had somehow carried me.

I was working the console the way I worked most days by then, half my attention on Cent's telemetry and half on the dozen other threads a program this size always had running at once, when the primary beam went quiet.

Not gone. Quiet, and then gone, the high-gain array's signal degrading over the space of about four seconds in a way that read, once I pulled the data afterward, as the end of a slow accumulation rather than a single event. Repeated kicks, cycle after cycle, had walked the mount's alignment out of true by fractions of a degree each time, invisible on any single pass, undeniable once you added them all together.

The backup transmitter held the emergency link the instant the primary dropped, exactly the way it was designed to. The automatic communications hold caught the loss and stopped further acceleration on its own, inside the containment margin we'd planned for, before I ever had to make that call myself.

"Talk to me," I said, once I had eyes on the backup feed and knew we weren't actually blind.

"I'm here," Cent said. "What happened?"

"Working on that part." I ran the diagnostic while I said it, and the answer came back clean enough that I didn't have to sit with any real uncertainty for long. Mechanical. Vibration accumulation in the array mount, nothing structural, nothing biological, nothing that had anything to do with her at all. I made a point of saying that last part out loud, because three failures in a row, even three failures this different in kind, could start to look like a pattern if nobody said outright that this one wasn't hers to carry.

"Mount's out of alignment," I told her. "Slow accumulation over a lot of cycles. Not you. Not the mass, not the shielding. A bracket that needed to be stiffer than we built it."

"Good," she said, and I could hear that she meant it the way people mean good when they're relieved it isn't them.

I sat with the diagnostic a while longer after the call ended, the way I always did, cross-checking my own read of it against every alternative I could think to consider, because I'd built a whole career out of never once trusting the first clean answer without personally trying to break it. Forty-three appearances in her own file, by someone's count, every single one of them a phone call, never once a face in the room with her. I'd made my peace with that a long time ago. Some jobs are done from a console instead of a cockpit, and mine had simply never once needed to be closer than that to still matter.

The fix, once we had the diagnosis, was almost boring by comparison to everything else that year. A stiffer mount, some added damping, retested before we let another kick near it. Quick. Competent. I was grateful for that. A program can only carry so many big moments before people need the small, unremarkable kind too.

✳

Chapter 20: Grebenchekov POV: Not-water existed as chemistry for months before it ever touched a ship. That gap always mattered more than anyone talked about.

The panels came off the fabrication line sized and shaped, structural mounting points machined to spec, packaging built so Cent could twin a cassette in or out of a bay clean, no fumbling, no manual labor beyond her own two hands and whatever she was carrying with them. I ran my palm along the first one before we mounted it. Cold, dense, gave slightly under my thumb the way the briefing said it would and no more than that.

"Feels like nothing," I said.

"It's supposed to," Osei said. "Feeling like something is what killed the last version."

We installed the symmetric baseline first. Full even complement, every occupied volume ringed the way the model called for. The mass-property check came back clean, center of mass landing inside a tolerance so tight I had to ask Osei twice whether the instrument was actually working.

"It's working," she said. "The panels are just that good."

We didn't trust that for long. Next came the asymmetric test, deliberately loaded wrong, heavy on one side, a partial complement on purpose. The system caught the imbalance before I finished the install sequence and refused to clear boost on its own, no human hand anywhere near the decision.

"There it is," Osei said, and I heard something close to relief in it.

Fault insertion came after that, run procedural, almost boring by design. False-present. False-absent. Stale data. Sensor loss. A receiving-site mismatch I hadn't even thought to worry about until the system flagged it and inhibited the boost on its own. Every single one, caught exactly the way it was supposed to be caught. No surprises. I'd had enough surprises in my life already, and I let myself enjoy the lack of them for once.

The static cycle mattered more than it looked like it should. A bay had to prove it was actually empty, not just assumed empty, every single time, before the next module went in. I watched the system verify an empty bay four times in a row before Osei let me load the next module, and watching it, I finally saw why she insisted on that redundancy. I'd learned the hard way, once, what "probably empty" costs when it's wrong. A surgeon had told me once, standing over a Dacron graft that had nearly finished what a centrifuge started, that the body doesn't forgive an assumption just because the assumption was reasonable at the time. I'd carried that lesson into every room I'd worked in since, this one included.

We ran the whole cycle uncrewed at the end. Install, coast, remove, reinstall, boost, verify. Nobody aboard for any of it. I watched the telemetry come back clean from a console two decks away and felt something in my chest ease that I hadn't fully admitted was tight until it wasn't anymore.

I didn't say much about it afterward. There wasn't a speech in me for this one. I just stood at that console a while longer than I needed to, watching the fault log scroll past, every single flag caught by a system built to catch it, and thought about a different console, a different failure, a load that had come apart under me once with no system standing between it and my own body. Nobody had built that catch for me back then. I was glad, watching this one work exactly as designed, that nobody would have to find this particular limit the hard way again.

✳

Chapter 21: Iverson POV: Full mass, first off-axis kicks, first compound vectors. New territory, not a bigger version of anything we'd already proven.

I'd learned, over three decades in and around this business, most of it before Reach ever existed, that the worst days always followed the days somebody got comfortable. I'd watched good pilots get careless exactly once, right after a long clean streak convinced them carelessness was safe now, and I'd made a private policy out of never letting that streak feel safe to anyone under my command, not even for a sentence.

Somebody junior started an easy line before we began, the kind of thing you say when you haven't yet learned not to say it. "Smooth sailing, right?"

"Don't," I said, before he finished the sentence, quiet, not unkind. "We don't say that. Not before a first. Say it after."

He looked at me like I might be joking. I wasn't. Thirty years in this business teaches you which superstitions are worth keeping, and that one had earned its place a long time before I did, going back to an instructor who'd drilled it into me before I'd ever touched real flight hardware myself, and who I still thought about more mornings than I let on to anyone in this program.

Cent was already mid-prep, focused enough that none of it reached her. Good. That was how it should be. I watched her run her own preflight ritual, the same small sequence every time, gloves checked twice, restraints checked twice, nothing left to the version of confidence that skips a step because the step has always come back clean before. I approved of it more than I'd ever told her directly. It was, near enough, the same ritual Hollis used to run, different hands, same discipline underneath it.

The room held itself the particular kind of quiet that precedes any real first, techs at their stations not quite breathing the way they'd breathe an hour from now once this was logged history instead of live risk. I ran my own checklist a second time behind the primary board, out of habit, the redundant kind of caution nobody had asked me to build into my own role and that I'd built into it anyway, because a second set of eyes costs nothing and has saved more programs than any single genius ever has.

She ran the sequence solo, the full four-indicator stack live for the first time together at real mass: navigation, sensors, mechanical locks, center of mass, load path, every one of them voting independently before green would show at all. I watched the board hold yellow a long moment while the last vote came in, and felt, in that particular stretch of held breath, the exact shape of every first I'd ever sat through in thirty years of doing this, the same weight, the same silence, the same understanding that the next four seconds were going to either confirm three years of work or hand everyone in this room a very long, very quiet debrief instead.

Green.

The kick went lateral and axial together, a compound vector nothing in this program had ever actually tried before, and it went clean. No shudder in the readouts. No correction needed after. Just the thing working exactly the way three years of gimbal testing had built it to work.

Nobody cheered. I didn't expect them to, not with me in the room, and I don't think anyone quite trusted it yet either, not until it was logged and confirmed and sitting in the permanent record where nothing could take it back.

"Confirmed," Osei said, finally, from her own console. "Clean across every channel."

I let myself feel it then, quiet, nothing like triumph. Just the settled satisfaction of watching something built right finally do the thing it was built for. I logged my own confirmation alongside Osei's, plain procedural language, nothing in the entry that would tell a future reader how much silence had actually preceded those two words going green.

I thought, walking out afterward, that this was the version of the job I'd chased my whole career without ever quite naming it that way: not the glory of a first, but the quiet, disciplined certainty underneath one, built patiently enough in advance that the actual moment barely needed to be dramatic at all. Hollis would have approved of that, I think, more than he'd have approved of anything else about this whole strange, impossible program. He never once wanted the credit. He just wanted the next pilot to come home. I'd spent thirty years trying to be worth that particular ambition. Today, watching a board go clean across every channel, I let myself believe, just for a minute, that I actually was.

✳

Chapter 22: Iverson POV: I learned not to say smooth sailing from a man named Hollis Deane, who said it once, out loud, over the comm, on a test flight I wasn't on, four minutes before the vehicle he was flying stopped answering to anything he did to it.

Hollis was my instructor before he was anything else to me, and my friend after that, and I want to be precise about the order those things happened in, because it matters to how I've run every cockpit I've sat in since. He taught me to fly the way some men teach their sons to fish, patient, exacting, treating the whole business as something sacred enough to deserve real discipline instead of swagger. I was young enough, then, to think discipline was the boring cousin of talent. Hollis never once let that slide past him uncorrected.

"Talent gets you into the seat," he told me, more times than I can count. "Discipline is the only thing that's ever gotten anybody back out of one."

He said smooth sailing on a routine systems check, four minutes into a flight that had gone clean for the first three, the kind of thing a tired, confident pilot says without thinking twice about it because the numbers all agree with him. I've read the transcript more times than is probably healthy. There's nothing in it that explains what happened next. A structural fault nobody had modeled, a stress concentration in a weld that passed every inspection anyone had ever run on it, failing all at once instead of announcing itself first the way faults are supposed to. He didn't have four seconds to react to it, let alone four minutes.

I was twenty-six. I flew a training sortie the next morning because the schedule said to and grounding myself would have meant admitting something I wasn't ready to admit yet, and I have never once, in the thirty years since, said the words smooth sailing out loud before a flight, mine or anyone else's under my command. I don't think it's superstition, not really, whatever the junior pilots assume when I correct them for it. I think it's the only monument I've got that actually does anything useful: a rule, still working, still saving people who never met the man it came from.

I turned down a lot of career-advancing desk work over the years to keep flying instead, and I want to be honest that some of that was simple love of the work, and some of it was that I never found a version of grief that let me stop. Flying kept Hollis's own lessons alive in my hands in a way sitting behind a desk reviewing other men's flight logs never could have. Every checklist I ran was, in some small way, still his checklist. Every superstition I enforced was still his rule, running thirty years past the man who'd taught it to me.

Reach found me the way it found most of the pilots on this crew, through a record long enough to speak for itself, and I said yes for reasons that had very little to do with glory and a great deal to do with the fact that a mission this unprecedented needed someone in the left seat who'd already buried a friend to carelessness once and had no intention of doing it again, to anyone, ever, if there was a single procedure left uninvented that might prevent it.

I still think about Hollis before every first this program hands me. I thought about him the morning of the first full-mass compound-vector kick, standing there watching Cent prep, correcting a junior pilot's easy confidence before it could cost anybody anything. I'll go on thinking about him for every first still ahead of us, all the way out to a star system nobody from Hollis's own generation ever got to dream about reaching. It's the only way I know how to carry him. It has never once let me down yet.

✳

Chapter 23: Live feed. No caption, no narration, just the raw public stream running the way it always ran, the way it had run for months by then, ordinary enough that most people watching had it on in a second window while they did something else.

Cent, solo, uncrewed, pushing Wayfarer's own frame up through another stage of the climb toward 0.8c. Nothing about the setup read as different from the dozen times before it. A routine progression of kicks, off-axis, compound-vector, the same disciplined rhythm the feed's regular viewers had learned to recognize without needing to understand the physics behind it.

The telemetry ticker scrolled clean numbers, then didn't.

The camera angle held on the hull a beat too long, then swung, hunting for something the operator on the ground couldn't yet name. A not-water module, twinned in after the last boost and not yet twinned out before this one, caught mid-transition in a state nobody had built a test for.

The mount went.

What came through the feed wasn't clean. It wasn't legible. A garbled half-second of telemetry, numbers that stopped making sense against each other. A camera view that wouldn't resolve into anything a viewer could actually parse. Comms, which had been carrying her calm, ordinary voice seconds before, dropped to silence.

Mission control's reaction reached the public feed only secondhand, filtered, professional, the controlled tightness of people trained not to let a camera see them panic even while everything underneath the training was screaming. Nobody said the word crash. Nobody said anything at all, for a long, terrible stretch, that confirmed one single thing to the millions of people watching.

In Ohio, a retired machinist set down his coffee without noticing he'd done it, notebook forgotten open on the workbench beside him, and stood very still in front of his single dedicated monitor, watching a dead feed the way a man watches a phone that hasn't rung yet but should have by now. Across the country, in kitchens and break rooms and college dorms, the same stillness settled over anyone who happened to have the feed open, a whole nation's worth of ordinary Tuesday afternoons interrupted by the same silent, unresolved dread.

A late-shift diner outside Tulsa had the feed running on a mounted television nobody usually watched past the weather segment. A waitress carrying a full tray to table six stopped mid-step when the anchor's voice cut out, tray balanced, forgotten, while every head in the place turned toward the same silent screen. Nobody said anything. Nobody needed to. A grandmother in Sacramento who'd started watching every broadcast since her granddaughter wrote a school report on the program found herself gripping the arm of her recliner hard enough to leave a mark, praying in a language she hadn't used seriously since her own mother died, to whichever version of whoever might be listening that a stranger she'd never met and felt she somehow knew would be all right.

The signal didn't come back.

The feed held on empty telemetry, a dead camera angle, silence where her voice should have been, and gave the audience nothing else. Not whether she was alive. Not whether anyone was coming for her. Just the raw, unedited fact of losing her, live, in front of the whole world, with no resolution offered because none existed yet.

✳

Chapter 24: Joe POV: The alert didn't come through the broadcast. It came straight from mission control, stripped of every softening word a person might have used to make it easier to hear.

"Module failure. Cent's unresponsive. We need you now."

I didn't ask questions. There wasn't time for questions, and some part of me had been waiting three years for exactly this call without ever admitting that to myself.

I closed my eyes and reached.

My range with this had never been tested the way hers had. She had a sixty-seven-million-kilometer jump behind her, proven, repeatable, hers. Mine had fired exactly once, under terror, half a solar system compressed into a single desperate second I hadn't consciously controlled at all. I didn't know, reaching now, whether this distance was even inside whatever I actually had. Nobody knew. There'd been no way to test it safely, and now there was no time to wish there had been.

I reached anyway.

The jump took me somewhere I had no name for, not a place so much as an act of finding her, a thread pulled taut across distance I couldn't have measured if you'd asked me to. And then I was there, in the wreck of a compartment that shouldn't have still had air in it, and she was there too.

Unconscious. Not merely hurt. Looking at her, I knew why it had to be me and not her: she couldn't have jumped herself out of this even if every system aboard had been screaming at her to try. Unconsciousness was the one thing that actually shut the ability off, hers or mine, the one door neither of us could open from the wrong side of it.

I got my arms under her, felt the wrongness of an injury I didn't have time to diagnose, and reached again, carrying dead weight now, carrying an injury, nothing like the clean single-body jump I'd made a hundred easy times before this one. It cost more. I felt it cost more, some deep physical toll I'd never had to pay before, and I paid it anyway because there wasn't another option on the table.

We came out at Kristen, into light and noise and hands reaching for her before I'd even fully arrived myself. Medical had her within seconds. I stood there afterward, arms empty, and felt the whole three-year weight of not knowing whether I could do this finally crash down through me all at once, my knees going, someone's hand catching my shoulder before I quite understood I needed it.

It was Davy's hand, I realized, once my vision cleared enough to place him. He didn't say anything right away, just held on, steady, letting me shake for as long as I needed to before either of us tried to turn it into words.

"You did it," he said finally, quiet, none of the old wariness in his voice at all, none of the watchful distance he'd kept around me for three careful years. "You actually did it."

"I didn't know if I could."

"I know. I watched you not know it for three years." He let go of my shoulder once I'd found my own feet again. "You know now."

She wasn't my responsibility alone anymore. That was the only thought I had room for, standing there shaking in a hallway full of people running past me toward her. She wasn't alone anymore, and neither, for the first time in longer than I could remember, was I.

✳

Chapter 25: Sterling POV: She survived. Unconscious, but alive. I want to say that straight out, first, before anything else, because everyone in that room needed to hear it in that order.

The first near-fatal thing that had ever happened to her. Bigger than the PAM-D deorbit, bigger than the attacker fight, bigger than anything in her whole life that had come before it, and I sat in mission control watching the President on the secondary screen the entire time it unfolded, because he'd been patched in from the start, the way he always was now.

He didn't go quiet. I'd half expected him to. Instead he ran a low, dry commentary track under everything, tense but controlled, his usual register holding even now.

"Talk to me," he said, more than once. "Somebody give me a number I can actually use."

We didn't have one for a long while. Root cause took hours to nail down properly: a not-water module caught mid-transition during an unplanned kick, twinned in and not yet twinned out, a state the entire fault-insertion campaign had somehow never tested. Not a failure of discipline. A genuine gap, invisible until something found it the hard way.

"Fix it," the President said, once we had the diagnosis. Two words, flat, nothing dry left in his voice at all.

"A hard interlock," Osei said, already working it. "That transitional state inhibits any kick automatically. Not a procedural reminder. A wall nobody can talk their way past, including her."

"Do it."

I watched his commentary sharpen into something else entirely once the cause was confirmed, and understood I was watching his own policy get tested for real for the first time since he'd made it. Not on our dime. He'd said it once, over a mass-campaign debrief, mostly hypothetical at the time. This wasn't hypothetical anymore.

The President stayed on the line long after the crisis itself had resolved into something manageable, which surprised me, given how thin his schedule usually ran. He didn't say much. Mostly he just sat there, patched in, present, the same way Truman was present for things that mattered, and somewhere in that long, quiet stretch, I realized he needed the confirmation as much as any of us did, whatever his own title said he was supposed to already be sure of.

"She's going to be fine," I told him, once Reyes' first real update came through.

"I know," he said. "I just wanted to hear it confirmed by someone who was actually watching the numbers instead of reading a summary somebody wrote for me." A pause, quieter. "Thank you, Sterling. For staying on this the way you did."

I visited her once, in the days between the accident and her actually waking up, standing at the edge of a medical bay I had no real reason to be in beyond the fact that I needed, personally, to see for myself that the numbers on my own console matched the actual person they described. She looked smaller than she ever did on a screen. I didn't stay long. I didn't need to say anything to her, and I don't think, unconscious as she was, she'd have heard me anyway. I just needed to see it with my own eyes.

The actual complete-crew confirmation, when it finally came days later, was nothing like Iverson's clean success on the compound-vector test. Quieter. Harder-won. Trust rebuilt one verified system at a time instead of simply assumed because the last test had gone well. Truman sat through every hour of it without saying much of anything, present the whole time the way I'd come to expect from him, carrying whatever it cost him the way he always did, silently, and I didn't ask him about it, because by then I knew some things a man carries are his to carry.

Chapter 26: Quincy POV: Eleven weeks before it happened, in a design review nobody outside that room will ever hear about, I said a transitional twin state was exactly the kind of condition our fault-insertion campaign was structurally guaranteed to miss. We were testing states, not the transitions between them, and a transition is where a disproportionate share of everything that has ever actually gone wrong on this program has chosen to go wrong.

That observation went where observations like it tend to go, which is nowhere, at least not until something forces a room to reconsider where it should have gone the first time. I am noticing a pattern in my own career on this program specifically, and I don't love what it suggests about how an institution actually metabolizes a correct idea that arrives before the crisis that would have made it feel urgent.

I pulled the data myself, once she was stable and the room had stopped needing me for anything more useful than analysis. A not-water module, twinned in, not yet twinned out, caught by an unplanned kick during a state nobody had thought to test because nobody had thought of it as a state at all. An instant, too brief to matter, right up until it turned out to be exactly the instant that mattered most.

I laid it next to the mass-campaign frame failure from months earlier, because I had been carrying the shape of that one around since it happened and wanted to know if I was imagining the resemblance. I wasn't. Different subsystem, different failure mode, the same actual root cause underneath both of them: a gap in what we had agreed to test, invisible right up until something found it the only way an untested gap ever actually gets found.

"Twice now," I said, in the review that followed, "we have discovered the same category of gap by very nearly losing something this program could not afford to lose. I would like it formally recorded that I do not believe a third instance of this particular lesson is one we can survive learning the same way."

Osei's hard interlock closed the specific gap that had almost killed her. I didn't object to it. I said, and I still believe, that it closed the specific gap and not the actual problem, which is that our own test matrix has a blind spot for exactly the category of condition that neither the tests nor the testers think to call a state until it very nearly kills someone. I asked, formally, for a review of every remaining transitional condition in the entire program, not only the one that had already found us. I got it. It took the better part of a month, cost real schedule, and turned up four more gaps of the same shape before anyone had to learn about them the hard way.

I don't know if that makes me the most useful person on this program or simply the most exhausting one. I've stopped trying to resolve that particular question. I only ever cared about being correct, and on this specific point, three separate times now, I have been.
