Chapter 27: Childs POV: Osei called the review nearly complete. I called it not tested against the day everything actually goes wrong.

Every subsystem had passed independently. Osei stood at the head of the table with the integrated configuration up on the main display, ready to mark it frozen, and I sat there running the sequence in my head the only way that mattered to me: not as an engineer admiring good work, but as the woman who'd be flying Threshold down through it someday.

Wayfarer's arrival. Separation. Descent. Landing. Every emergency contingency I could think to run.

I found the problem on the third pass.

"Walk me through the transfer route," I said. "Cruise shielding installed, full gear, an unconscious crew member on a litter."

Osei pulled the geometry up. I watched her trace the same path I already had in my head, watched her expression change about two-thirds of the way through it.

"It doesn't clear," she said.

"No."

The model worked fine for someone walking under their own power. It didn't work for a suited casualty, a litter, a medical team, and every sealed pressure boundary that shielding installation had added since the model was first drawn. Nobody had built it wrong. Nobody had checked it against exactly this, either, not until now.

I didn't invoke Cent's accident to make the point. I didn't need to. Everyone in that room already knew why this mattered.

"I'm not certifying a route that assumes everyone stays conscious and mobile," I said. "Hold the freeze."

Osei held it. No argument, no defense of work she could easily have defended. She just marked it held and started pulling up the coupled systems that would need to change: pressure volume, local shielding geometry, structural attachment, Threshold's own interface, the whole ship's mass distribution shifting around one fixed problem that touched everything at once.

It took weeks to redesign properly. I won't pretend it didn't cost the schedule something. I'd rather it cost the schedule than cost somebody their life on a day none of us got to choose in advance.

We tested the revised route in a full-scale layout: pressure suits, a weighted casualty, real medical equipment, the lights killed on purpose, one route deliberately blocked to force the alternate. I ran it myself, in the suit, on the litter end of it, because I wasn't going to certify something I hadn't personally felt fail or succeed with my own body. I thought, strapped onto that litter, weight shifting wrong with every corner, about a river eleven years back and everything good instinct alone had nearly failed to cover for. I wasn't going to let this route be another six gaps somebody found out about only after it mattered.

The first run exposed a handling problem at the transfer collar, small, real, the kind of thing that only shows up when actual bodies move through actual hardware instead of a model moving through a screen. We fixed it. We ran it again instead of calling it close enough.

The second run passed clean.

One more requirement went into the frozen configuration alongside the transfer-route fix, mandated from the top rather than proposed up from the floor: a real chemical contingency reserve, actual reaction mass, no dependence on Cent or anyone else's ability to move it. Not on our dime meant, among everything else it had already come to mean, that this program was never again going to leave itself with exactly one working option if its own engine went down. The reserve stayed off the manifest during any boost Cent herself was providing, twinned aboard only once cruise velocity was locked, so it never once cost her a single kilogram of kickable mass getting a ship up to speed in the first place. If she was the one bringing a ship back down at the far end too, the same reserve came off again before she started, jettisoned clean, dead weight she'd never actually need to carry through a deceleration she was fully capable of flying herself. It existed for exactly one scenario, and only one: the day she couldn't finish what she'd started, and the ship needed a way home that didn't run through her at all.

Osei closed the configuration for good after that. Each of us signed our own concurrence, nothing borrowed from anyone else's authority. I signed for flight operations and for Threshold. Nothing more, nothing less.

I stood in the newly clear transfer corridor after the fabrication releases started moving, and understood, standing there, that the ship had stopped being a collection of promising systems. It had become something sixteen people could actually trust with their bodies. That was the whole job, as far as I was concerned. I was glad I'd held the freeze long enough to actually finish it.

✳

Chapter 28: Childs POV: I put a regional jet down on the Willamette River eleven years before Reach ever existed, both engines gone to a bird strike inside of four seconds, and I want to say clearly that I did it well. Everyone survived. The NTSB commendation says so in exactly those words, and I've read it enough times that I could probably recite it from memory if you asked me to.

I don't keep the commendation anywhere I have to look at it. I keep, instead, a much more private accounting of the six things that went right by instinct that day and should have gone right by procedure instead, because I hadn't built the procedure yet, because nobody had ever needed it before that exact afternoon, because I was flying on pure trained reflex and raw nerve in roughly equal measure and got lucky in the specific proportion that let good instinct cover for the gaps good process should have closed instead.

I still remember the exact texture of that river coming up too fast, gray-green and wrong-angled through the windscreen, my own hands doing things my conscious mind had no time to authorize, some deeper trained layer of me taking over completely while the part that usually did the deciding simply held on and hoped. I remember the water taking the fuselage's weight, the specific violence of it, and then, somehow, the aircraft settling instead of breaking apart, floating instead of sinking, forty-one people screaming and then, almost immediately, not screaming, because we were somehow, impossibly, still alive and still afloat.

Everyone talks about the landing itself like it was the hard part. It wasn't, not really, not in the way that mattered to me afterward. The hard part was sitting in a debrief room two weeks later, being called a hero for the fourth or fifth time, and understanding, privately, that heroism and correctness are not actually the same thing, and that I owed the people who trusted me next a version of myself that didn't need to get lucky twice.

Forty-one souls aboard. Forty-one souls off the water, alive, most of them cold and shaken and otherwise fine. I've been called a hero for that flight more times than I can count, and every single time, I've had to sit with the private, unglamorous truth underneath the headline: I got the outcome right and the process wrong, and the only reason nobody ever found that gap was that I'd gotten lucky enough not to need it exposed.

I spent the two years afterward doing something most people who walk away from an incident like that don't do. I didn't retell the story at dinner parties. I built the procedure instead, alone, on my own time, working backward through every decision I'd made that day by instinct and asking, coldly, what a checklist would have told me to do instead, and where the two answers diverged, and why. I found six real gaps. I closed all six, on paper, formally, submitted through channels that had no obligation to listen to a single pilot's unsolicited procedural amendment and, to their credit, listened anyway.

That's the actual reason I hold freezes other engineers think are overcautious. That's the reason I ran Threshold's casualty-transfer route myself, in the suit, on the litter end of it, instead of trusting a model that looked clean on a screen. I know what it costs to get an outcome right while the process underneath it stays wrong. I know because I did it once, and forty-one people are alive because I got lucky, not because I got it right, and I have spent every year since refusing to let luck do that job again if there is any procedure left uninvented that could do it instead.

Iverson understood it the first time I explained it to him, which is part of why we work as well together as we do. He'd buried a friend to a fault nobody could have modeled. I'd nearly buried forty-one strangers to a fault I could have modeled, if anyone had thought to model it before that particular Tuesday. Different roads into the same conviction: that the day everything goes right is not the same day as the day everything was actually safe, and a pilot who confuses the two is one bad afternoon away from finding out the hard way which day she was actually flying in.

✳

Chapter 29: Joe POV: I worked from a sealed annex on the ground, close enough to the program to follow everything, never quite steeped in it long enough to stop hearing what everyone else had learned not to hear anymore.

The integrated testing ran for months, one long extended sequence carrying Wayfarer through pressure, power, life support, communications, medical, surgical, shielding, gimbal, full mass, stage after stage, the seventh gimbal reconfirmation just another ordinary Tuesday by the time it came around. Oversight scaled up around all of it, quietly, nobody announcing a new policy so much as everyone simply starting to act like the stakes had changed. Two-person concurrence on every configuration change. The President showing up on review links often enough that I stopped registering it as unusual. Security tightened during predictable test windows, and I noticed that part mostly because it meant an extra badge check on my own way into the annex some mornings.

I'd started to understand, by then, that my own value to this program wasn't going to come from being the most credentialed person in any room. It was going to come from exactly this: standing close enough to catch what people too steeped in the work to see it fresh anymore had stopped noticing. I caught it during a simulated 0.8c mission profile, watching two countdown displays that should have agreed with each other and didn't.

Both were counting down twenty-four hours. Both were behaving exactly the way they'd been designed to behave. And they disagreed by a margin too large to be rounding error.

"Which clock?" I asked, over the comm, to nobody in particular.

Silence for a second. Then a lot of very smart people started talking at once.

Nobody had done anything wrong. That was the strange part, once we dug into it. One countdown was tracking ship proper time. The other was tracking Earth's frame. At 0.8c, twenty-four hours aboard Wayfarer runs to something like forty hours back home, and every single system involved understood that relationship perfectly well. What nobody had done, across an institution full of people who understood the physics cold, was make sure every procedure that used the words hour, day, elapsed, or due actually said which clock it meant.

The integrated sequence held while the program audited every single time-dependent instruction it had. Most of what they found didn't matter. Some of it did, badly: maintenance limits, medical schedules, communications windows, navigation events, automated warnings, mission-control commands, all of it quietly capable of meaning two different things depending on which side of the relativity gap you happened to be standing on.

The fix went program-wide. Every timer, command, alarm, procedure, and log entry had to carry an explicit time basis from that point forward. Displays showed both clocks, always. Anything missing a valid basis got rejected outright instead of guessed at.

They reran the simulation afterward under worse conditions on purpose: partial comms, delayed commands, deliberately malformed time references thrown into the mix. The system rejected every ambiguous input clean, and crew and mission control landed on the exact same event sequence independently, no daylight between them.

I hadn't solved anything about relativity. Every specialist in that program had already understood the physics better than I ever would. I'd just noticed that an institution full of experts had let one ordinary word carry two different meanings, and nobody had thought to ask which one until an outsider happened to be listening closely enough to hear the gap.

The revised campaign closed clean after that. Whatever came next, it was coming with better clocks than it would have had without me.

✳

Chapter 30: Iverson POV: Zero external hardware. No booster stage. Cent's own hands were the entire propulsion system on this flight, the same as they'd been on every flight since the first one, and I'd stopped finding that strange a long time before this particular morning.

I took the left-hand command seat and logged the cabin configuration the way I logged it every time, out of habit as much as procedure. The casualty-transfer corridor sat dark and empty behind my station. I noticed it register as validation rather than absence, Childs's widened egress route sitting there unused because nothing had gone wrong yet to need it.

Five hundred sixty metric tons, with minimal shielding, the baseline hull protection built into her mass from the start, full margin held in reserve. Clean. The not-water cassettes and the forward Umbrella panels were stowed, waiting to be twinned into their permanent cradles only once we'd locked cruise velocity, same as the chemical contingency reserve riding behind them, mandated cargo neither of us ever expected to actually need. A deep-system baseline past two hundred thirty astronomical units, twenty-four hours of coast in ship proper time, roughly forty on Earth's own clock.

The loop across the crew ran flat and calm and heavy all at once. Nobody had gone hesitant since Cent's extraction. If anything, we'd all gone more clinical, a team that had already found out what the worst version of this looked like and decided, collectively, without ever discussing it out loud, not to let it happen twice.

I caught myself, running my own preflight ritual, correcting a junior tech's easy confidence out of the corner of my ear the same way I always did, and thought, not for the first time, that Hollis would have approved of this crew specifically. Not because they were fearless. Because they weren't, and did the work carefully anyway, which was the only version of courage he'd ever actually respected.

"Go/no-go," Truman said, running the poll himself, department by department. "Every station declare your time basis before you answer. Ship proper time. No exceptions, no defaults."

"Pilot."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-oh-seven, go."

"Copilot."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-oh-nine, go."

"EVA."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-eleven, go."

"Engineering."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-thirteen, go."

"Medical."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-fifteen, go."

"Surgical."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-seventeen, go."

"Flight Med."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-nineteen, go."

"Geology."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-twenty-one, go."

"Science."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-twenty-three, go."

"Atmospheric."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-twenty-five, go."

"Bio."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-twenty-seven, go."

"Life Support."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-twenty-nine, go."

"Comms."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-thirty-one, go."

"Signals."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-thirty-three, go."

"Education."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-thirty-five, go."

Fifteen names, fifteen voices, every single one of them synced to the same tracking frame before answering, checking for any hidden divergence before logging consent nobody had coerced out of them.

"Command."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-thirty-seven, go," Truman said, holding himself to the same discipline he'd just held everyone else to.

Cent went last, the way she always did.

"Propulsion."

"Ship time, one-four-two-two-thirty-nine, go," she said. "Confirmed. Derated. Green across the board."

The board actually was green, clean, every independent vote agreeing at once: navigation, sensors, mechanical locks, center of mass, load path. No searching for a wall on this one. Everything ran strictly on numbers already proven safe.

"You are go for boost," Truman said.

She took the grips.

"Boosting," she said.

The first kick came clean, the gimbal absorbing a thrust vector that would have torn apart anything built with less discipline behind it. No shear. No resonance. The haptic grips stayed cold in her hands, silent, confirming a perfectly clean run through that one small slice of the climb.

It was, of course, only one slice. Reaching the target vector meant getting there the same disciplined way we'd proven everything else in this program: incrementally, kick by kick, coast by coast, checked and rechecked between each one instead of trusted to hold across a single continuous push nobody could have actually survived attempting. We ran that first cycle through a full watch, kicked, coasted, verified, kicked again, and when the watch ended we handed off clean, the way every long operation in this program eventually did.

I slept in my own bunk that first night same as any other, woke to Childs already back in the left-hand seat and the board reading exactly where I'd left it, one more increment closer than it had been the day before. We traded the chair on a fixed watch rotation the whole three days, the same standardized fatigue clock this agency had spent decades of institutional knowledge refining long before any of us was born: set watch lengths, mandated rest between them, circadian-anchored regardless of how the work itself happened to be going that hour. There was no real schedule pressure to justify cutting a corner NASA had already spent half a century learning not to cut, and nobody even considered trying. Cent rotated through the exact same clock as the rest of us, resting fully between kicks on the same fixed schedule, because a tired hand on that grip was exactly the kind of variable Not on our dime had been written to rule out before it ever became a real question.

By the third day, the increments had stopped feeling historic and started feeling like exactly what they were: work, careful and repetitive and utterly undramatic, the way the actual hard part of anything enormous eventually always does.

The last kick, when it came, landed the same as all the ones before it. Clean. No shear. No resonance.

We punched through the target vector and settled into coast out past two hundred thirty AU. There was nothing behind us on the display, nothing at all, the same ordinary emptiness we'd already been crossing to get here, and I thought, looking at it, that the emptiness itself looked like a small, wordless preview of something bigger waiting further out, though I couldn't have told you what yet.

The navigation arrays locked our return vector home. The ship-proper clock and the Earth-frame clock on the display had already been pulling apart the entire way through the boost, degree by imperceptible degree, buried too deep in the decimal places for anyone to have actually watched it happen. Settled now into steady coast, the drift finally read as something real instead of noise, quiet and undeniable in a way none of us had ever actually watched happen live before. The crew went silent, not from celebration, from something flatter and more final than that.

The testing phase was over. The envelope was closed.

We were going.

✳

Chapter 31: Grace POV: Cent found me on a Tuesday afternoon, three months before any of the rest of this started, standing at the front of a classroom explaining mitosis to twenty-six seventh graders who'd learned months ago that answering fast beat answering right, and hadn't slowed down since.

I ran a speed round at the start of every class, sixty seconds, no exceptions, a habit that had outlived every other teaching method I'd ever tried and outperformed most of them too. "Mitochondria," I said, chalk already moving.

"Powerhouse of the cell!" Half the room, in ragged unison, the answer drilled in so deep by then it barely counted as thinking anymore.

"Haploid."

"Half!" A different kid, quicker than the rest, pleased with himself for it.

"Why do we care about surface-area-to-volume ratio?"

"Because bigger cells can't get nutrients in fast enough!" Kayla, from the front row, the answer coming out in one breath like she'd been holding it ready.

"Nice. Meiosis gives you how many daughter cells?"

"Four!"

"Mitosis?"

"Two!" A chorus this time, confident, the easy ones always landing loudest.

A hand went up, slower than the others, half-raised like the kid wasn't sure he wanted the answer badly enough to admit why he was actually asking. Marcus, quiet most days, never volunteering unless called on directly.

"So cancer's cells dividing wrong, right? Like, not stopping when they're supposed to?" He was looking at his own hands instead of at me. "My grandma's doing chemo right now. The doctors keep saying stuff about her cells and I didn't want to ask her what it actually meant."

I stopped the clock. Some questions cost more than sixty seconds and are worth every bit of the difference. I came around from the front of the room and crouched down by his desk instead of answering from behind the podium, the rest of the round on hold and nobody complaining about it. "Yeah," I said. "That's exactly right. Normal cells know when to stop dividing. Cancer cells lose that instruction somewhere along the way and just keep going. That's the disease, underneath everything else it gets called. It's also exactly what her doctors are working to shut back off, and they've gotten a lot better at that than they used to be." I didn't promise him anything I didn't actually know to be true. I gave him the real shape of it instead, plain, because kids can tell the difference between an honest answer and a comforting lie, and only one of them actually helps. "Don't ever apologize for stopping the clock on something like that."

Clock back on. "Last one. Why does it actually matter that meiosis shuffles the chromosomes instead of just splitting them clean?"

Silence, for exactly as long as I'd expected. That one was never supposed to be easy.

"Because," I said, letting it sit a second before I gave it to them, "if it didn't, every single one of you would look like nothing but your own two parents mashed together the exact same way, every sibling, forever, no variation at all. Genetic shuffling's the entire reason any of you look like yourselves instead of a copy of somebody else. Sixty seconds up. Chairs around, diagrams out."

I'd noticed her in the doorway sometime around the third question, a young woman in an ordinary jacket, unremarkable except for how still she stood, none of the fidgeting visitors usually did in a room full of adolescents. I'd assumed, the way you assume things about a stranger who looks vaguely familiar and out of place, that she was a parent, or possibly lost, and I'd filed the thought away and kept teaching, because twenty-six seventh graders don't pause for a distraction just because their teacher has one.

"So if the chromosomes just split evenly," said a kid named Devon, from the back row, sounding like he'd just found a fatal flaw in the whole system, "how come identical twins aren't, like, exactly identical? Isn't the DNA supposed to be the same?"

It was a good question, better than most of what I got on a Tuesday, and I gave it the actual answer instead of the simplified one, epigenetics, environmental expression, the messy reality that identical code doesn't guarantee identical outcomes, watching him work to follow it and mostly succeed. I noticed, out of the corner of my eye, that the woman in the doorway had shifted her weight, leaning in slightly, the way you lean in when something's actually caught your attention rather than merely occupying it.

A girl in the front row, Kayla, who I happened to know was repeating the unit because she'd been out sick during the first pass, raised her hand slowly, not wanting to admit she was lost again.

"I don't get why it matters that they split evenly. Like, what happens if they don't?"

"Great question. What do you think happens?"

"I don't know, that's why I asked."

"Take a guess. You won't get it wrong in a way that matters."

She thought about it, visibly uncomfortable with the uncertainty, and worked her way slowly toward an answer that wasn't quite right and wasn't quite wrong either, and I built the rest of the explanation off her own guess instead of discarding it, because a wrong guess reasoned through carefully teaches more than a right answer handed over for free. I caught the visitor watching that exchange specifically, something attentive in her face that had nothing to do with mitosis at all.

She waited through the rest of the period without saying anything else, through two more questions and one entirely unrelated debate about whether a hot dog counted as a sandwich that I let run longer than I probably should have, because sometimes thirty seconds of nonsense is what keeps a room actually willing to come back to the real material. When the bell rang and the last student filed out, still arguing about the hot dog, she came in and set a worn paper printout on my desk. It took me a moment to recognize it.

My own paper. The one that had ended my research career, folded and refolded so many times the creases had gone soft, sections underlined in a handwriting that wasn't mine.

"You actually read the whole thing," I said, which wasn't the most dignified opening line I could have chosen, but it was the only thought in my head.

"Three times. I wanted to make sure I understood the actual argument, not just the part everyone quoted to make fun of it." She pulled a chair around and sat, uninvited, entirely comfortable doing it. "You were right, weren't you. About the carbon assumption."

"Nobody in my field thinks that."

"I didn't ask what your field thinks. I asked what you think."

I told her the truth, because there didn't seem to be much point in doing otherwise, not with someone who'd clearly already read past whatever polite fiction I usually offered people who asked. Yes, I still believed it. Yes, I still thought about it more than was probably healthy, ten years and a demoted career later, standing in front of a whiteboard covered in diagrams for students who'd forget them by Friday.

"I have a mission that might encounter anything," she said. "Not might find carbon-based life somewhere convenient. Might encounter anything at all, and need someone who can build a working model out of almost nothing and isn't afraid of being wrong in front of a room full of experts. That's not most scientists. That's apparently you."

"You're serious."

"Completely."

I said yes before she'd finished the sentence explaining what the position would require of me, which I knew, even in the moment, was probably not the most careful way to make a decision of that size. It didn't feel careless. It felt, instead, like something I'd been waiting ten years for somebody to actually offer me.

What I didn't know yet, standing in that empty classroom with my own discredited paper in my hands, was how much of a fight she still had ahead of her to actually make that offer stick.

✳

Chapter 32: Cent POV: I'd told him yes before I'd cleared it with anyone. That part I left out of the classroom conversation, mostly because I didn't think it would help, and partly because I already knew, walking out of that school, how hard I was about to have to fight for it.

I walked into Truman's office the way I usually did, no appointment, no warning, and got straight to it before he'd even fully looked up from his desk.

"I've got a crew addition you're not gonna like."

"I'm listening."

"But I want him anyway." I sat down across from him without being asked. "His name's Ryland Grace. Middle school science teacher. Former molecular biologist. No flight background, no astronaut training, no relevant certifications for anything we actually do up there."

Truman set his pen down, which I'd learned over the years meant I now had his full attention instead of the half he usually spared for anyone who walked in unannounced.

"So why am I hearing about him."

"Because he wrote a paper ten years ago arguing we shouldn't assume alien life has to be carbon-based, and it cost him his career, and he was right, and I need someone on this crew who can build a working model out of almost nothing when we hit something none of our specialists have a framework for. That's not a credential. That's a way of thinking. He has it."

Truman didn't say no outright, which I'd learned, over years of doing this with him, meant he was actually considering it rather than performing consideration before landing on no anyway.

"You know what the selection committee's going to say."

"I know what they're going to say. I want to be in the room when they say it."

The selection committee said, more or less, what I expected. A room full of people who'd spent careers building the most rigorously credentialed astronaut corps in the history of the species looked at a printout of Ryland Grace's employment history and did not immediately see seventeen years of accumulated NASA discipline reflected back at them.

"No flight physical on record," one of them said. "No G-force tolerance testing. No survival training, no zero-g familiarization, nothing beyond a standard civilian background check."

"Grebenchekov didn't have any of that either, the first time," I said. "He had Exo behind him and my own insistence that he be included if he could meet medical standards. We built the rest of what he needed after we already knew he belonged."

"That's a different situation."

"It's the exact same situation. You're just less comfortable with this version of it because I'm the one making the case again and none of you get to pretend it's really NASA's own idea this time."

Nobody had a fast answer for that. I watched the room work through it the way rooms full of careful people always eventually work through something they don't love but can't actually refute. Watching them, I already knew I was going to win this one the same way I usually won things: not through charm, and not by backing down, just by refusing to leave until the math actually worked out in my favor.

"Fine," the committee chair said, finally, clearly conceding a point he still privately disagreed with. "We'll build a compressed readiness track. But he clears every physical standard the rest of the crew clears, no exceptions made for the circumstances of his selection."

"Wouldn't have asked for any."

Chapter 33: Osei POV: Nobody had ever tried to get a civilian schoolteacher mission-ready in under three months, and I told Cent as much the day the assignment landed on my desk.

"He's got three months and no relevant background at all," I said. "Compressed physical conditioning, compressed zero-g familiarization, compressed everything, and none of it compresses as cleanly as anyone in that selection meeting probably promised him it would."

"I know. That's why I'm not asking you to make it easy. I'm asking you to make it real."

I built the track the same way I built everything else in this program: honestly, with the actual risks stated straight instead of smoothed over for anyone's comfort. Physical conditioning first, because a body that couldn't handle sustained micro-gravity wasn't going to be saved by any amount of scientific brilliance. Zero-g familiarization in the pool, then in the drop tower, watching him fail at the most basic orientation tasks the same way every civilian failed them the first dozen times, and watching him keep coming back the next morning anyway, which told me more about whether he'd actually survive this than any single physical benchmark could have.

The drop tower broke him a little, the second week. Not physically, nothing an ordinary rest cycle couldn't fix, but I watched something go out of his face after his ninth consecutive disoriented, useless drop. He was doing the math on how many weeks he had left against how far behind that math currently put him. I could see it happening behind his eyes.

"I taught myself organic chemistry out of library books when the college I wanted wouldn't have me," he said, sitting on the tower's edge afterward, not quite looking at me. "I don't fail at learning things. I'm not built for it."

"You're not failing at learning it. You're failing at doing it while your inner ear tries to convince you the room is lying to you. Those are different problems, and only one of them is actually about you."

"Doesn't feel different from up here."

"It will. Come back tomorrow."

He came back tomorrow. And the morning after that. By the third week the disorientation still hit him, same as it hit everyone, but he'd stopped treating each drop as a referendum on whether he belonged in the program at all, which was, as far as I was concerned, the actual milestone worth logging, whatever the official readiness chart said about it.

"You're doing better than most first-timers," I told him, maybe six weeks in, meaning it.

"I don't feel like I'm doing better than most first-timers."

"That's usually a good sign. The ones who feel confident this early are the ones I worry about."

We built his specific curriculum around the actual job rather than a generic astronaut checklist: less orbital mechanics, more structured problem-solving under uncertainty, sessions where I deliberately gave him incomplete data and watched him build a working hypothesis out of the gaps instead of demanding he wait for better numbers that weren't coming. It was, I told Truman privately, unlike training anyone else on this crew had gone through, and it was, by the same measure, exactly the training the actual mission needed someone like him to have.

He cleared every physical standard by the end of it, same as promised, no exceptions made for how he'd gotten there. I signed off on his readiness certification myself, and felt, doing it, the specific satisfaction of watching an unconventional bet actually pay out before it had even been properly tested yet.

✳

Chapter 34: Grace POV: Three months later, in the staging room, none of that made the plastic bag in my hand any less alarming.

"Two of these," Cent said, pressing them into my palm, brisk about it in a way that told me she'd done this exact briefing before. "Body straight. Neck stiff as a post, head bolted to your spine, no sudden movements. If any of it ends up on the consoles, Truman will personally hand you a toothbrush and watch you clean it."

"You're serious."

"Completely."

My flight suit didn't fit right, too tight through the shoulders, and I gripped my notebook like it might anchor me to something solid. It wasn't going to. Nothing here was solid in the way I understood the word.

"Ready?" she asked.

I wasn't. I said yes anyway, because saying anything else felt like admitting something I wasn't prepared to admit out loud.

She took my forearm.

Earth didn't fade. It didn't blur, didn't count down, didn't do any of the things my body had apparently expected it to do. One moment I stood in a sterile room on the ground. The next, the deep industrial rumble of a ship in deep space surrounded me, recycled air thick in my throat, my body suddenly, catastrophically weightless.

The briefing hadn't taken. I knew that with total clarity about half a second before my own head snapped hard against my locked neck anyway, vertigo slamming through me despite every single instruction I'd just been given. I went down into the plastic bag exactly the way she'd warned me I would, boots scrambling for a floor that wasn't cooperating, and some distant part of my brain registered, with real horror, that failing this badly after being warned this specifically felt worse than failing cold ever could have.

Someone got a hand under my arm. Truman, I realized, once my vision cleared enough to place him, hauling me upright into a molded jump seat with Iverson's help, neither of them commenting on the mess at all. Clearly not their first time doing this.

I noticed, blinking through the aftermath, that my notebook's ink hadn't smudged. Small, absurd relief.

The crew moved around me, unbothered, going about ordinary business in micro-gravity and recycled air and the vast dark just outside the hull. Watching them, it hit me that I'd arrived somewhere that had already stopped being extraordinary to everyone in the room except me. Grebenchekov floated past at one point, upside down relative to me, reading a tablet as unbothered as a man doing his taxes, and offered me an encouraging thumbs up without looking up from whatever he was actually working on, which somehow did more to steady me than any of the actual briefing had.

"You get used to it," he said, still not looking up. "Give it a week. Maybe two, for someone who reads as thoroughly unathletic as you do."

"Thank you for that specific vote of confidence."

"You are welcome. I am famous for my bedside manner."

The push, when it came, caught me completely off guard a second time. Wayfarer executed some routine correction, nothing to do with anything I'd been prepared for, and I felt the ship's mass shift under me with no engine roar to explain it, no sound at all beyond the physical fact of moving. My stomach lurched again, differently this time, a wrongness I didn't have a word for yet.

I made a note in my book afterward, hand shaking too badly to write cleanly. I was already framing it the way I framed everything, an anecdote for a classroom I'd probably never get to tell the true version of, because the true version didn't fit inside anything my students would believe.

Chapter 35: Truman POV: I read six hundred surgical files in a single week once we confirmed we needed a second physician, and only one of them stopped me cold enough to get me on a plane instead of just picking up a phone.

It wasn't the credentials, though the credentials were real enough on their own: board-certified in trauma and general surgery, a fellowship record longer than most people's whole careers, outcomes that already sat well above her hospital's own strong baseline. It was one line, buried on the fourth page of a peer review nobody else had bothered flagging, describing a night with four incoming traumas and a single working surgeon on the floor who hadn't lost one of them.

I'd learned, over years of building rosters for people headed into space, none of it ever this far before now, not to trust a line in a report enough to build a seat around it. So I got on a plane instead of picking up the phone, and told the hospital's administrator I wanted to observe, nothing more, no introductions, no disruption to anyone's day.

They put me in the gallery above the trauma bay, which was as close as anyone was willing to let a stranger get who wasn't scrubbed in. A multi-vehicle collision came in that afternoon, three patients rolling through the doors inside of four minutes, more noise and motion than I could track cleanly through the glass. I watched the room around her start to come apart a little at the edges almost immediately: a resident's voice climbing half an octave, a nurse calling for a blood unit that wasn't where it was supposed to be, the particular controlled chaos of people who were good at their jobs and still, understandably, scared.

Fraiser didn't climb with them. If anything, she got quieter, which I'd come to understand later was the actual tell, the thing that separated her file from every other file in that stack. Her voice dropped instead of rising. She called the room back to order in about four words, flat and unhurried, and the room came back to order because she'd asked it to like there was no other option worth considering.

I watched her work a bleed for eleven straight minutes without once raising her voice, without once looking, from where I stood, like the outcome was in any real doubt to her personally, even though the numbers scrolling on the monitor behind her said otherwise the entire time.

She saved all three of them. I found that out afterward from a resident too rattled yet to filter himself properly, who told me, unprompted, that one of the three shouldn't have made it by any reasonable measure of what that kind of injury usually did to a person.

I didn't introduce myself. I went back to my hotel room instead and sat a long while with what I'd actually watched: not a resume anymore, a person, calm in a room where calm wasn't the natural state of anything, holding three lives together with her own two hands because she'd apparently decided, somewhere back in her career, that panic was simply a choice other people made and she'd stopped making it herself.

We already had one physician committed to the mission, and a good one. But a single physician is a single point of failure on a ship four years from the nearest hospital in any direction, and I'd just watched a woman treat a mass-casualty night like it was an unremarkable Tuesday.

I called Cent from the parking lot before I'd even reached my car.

✳

Chapter 36: Truman POV: I walked onto Wayfarer's command deck without calling ahead first, which told Cent something was coming before I'd said a single word, since I never did that.

She looked up from her console, already half-guarded. "You've got a face on."

"I've got a crew addition you're not going to like."

"You say that like it's not how you introduced Grace to me."

"This one's different. This one already has the resume. She just doesn't have us yet." I sat down across from her, uninvited, the way she usually did to me. "Her name's Janet Fraiser. Trauma and general surgeon. Board-certified, fellowship-trained, outcomes better than anyone else in the pool we've actually got access to. I watched her hold three lives together in one room for eleven minutes without raising her voice once."

"So what's the part I'm not going to like."

"She's never flown. Never touched a zero-g platform, never been anywhere near our selection pipeline. And she's not from the eleven-thousand-person internal pool the committee already vetted. I'm asking them to open an outside search for a seat we haven't had to open outside for since Grebenchekov."

Cent didn't answer right away, which I'd learned, over years of doing this with her, meant she was actually weighing it instead of just working her way toward a no.

"We already have Park."

"Park's good. Park's also one person, on a ship that just spent an entire session proving how far it is from the nearest hospital in any direction. Two physicians isn't luxury. It's the minimum for continuous coverage if something ever happens to the one we've already got."

"You know what they're going to say."

"I know what they're going to say. I want to be in the room when they say it."

That earned me something close to a smile, faint, recognizing the line for what it was.

She brought me to the committee that same afternoon.

The room was the same room, more or less the same faces, and I watched them do to Fraiser's file what they'd done to Grace's, six months earlier, except this time the problem ran the opposite direction: too little space experience attached to too much everything else.

"Excellent surgeon," one of them said, not unkindly, turning a page. "No flight physical on record. No G-tolerance testing, no zero-g familiarization, nothing beyond what any civilian surgeon would have. And she's outside the pool. We built that pool specifically so we wouldn't be having conversations like this one."

"You built that pool to guarantee competence," I said. "I'm not asking you to lower the bar. I watched her clear a bar most of the people in this room have never personally faced, with three lives on the table and no time to second-guess herself. If she can pass the same physical and zero-g standards Grace is already working through, it's the exact same math we already ran on him."

"Grace filled a gap nobody else on the roster could fill."

"So does she. Park covers one physician's worth of medicine. She covers the difference between one and two, which on a four-year transit is the difference between a bad day and a dead crew member." I let that sit a moment before I finished it. "You already opened this door once this year. I'm asking you to walk through it a second time, for the same reason you walked through it the first: because the person on the other side of it is worth it."

Nobody had a fast answer for that, and I recognized the silence, because I'd sat on the other side of exactly this kind of silence myself, watching Cent win the same argument six months earlier.

"Fine," the committee chair said, in the tone of a man conceding a point he still privately disagreed with. "Compressed track. Flight physical, G-tolerance, full zero-g familiarization, same standards as anyone else on this roster. She clears every one of them, or she doesn't fly. No exceptions for how she got in this room."

"Wouldn't have asked for any."
