Chapter 37: Fraiser POV: I'd spent nineteen years learning to keep other people's bodies from doing the wrong thing at the wrong moment, and none of it prepared me even slightly for my own body doing exactly that in Wayfarer's staging room, in front of an audience.

"You'll want to keep your stomach settled before we start," Park told me, entirely too cheerful about it for a man about to watch me fail my own qualification.

"I've held a scalpel over an open abdominal cavity through a seven-hour surgery without needing to sit down once," I said. "I think I can manage a jump."

"That's not remotely the same thing, and you know it."

He was right, and I resented him for it exactly as much as the smugness in his voice deserved. Nothing in a surgical career prepares a person for weightlessness. I knew, intellectually, every mechanism involved: the vestibular system's confusion when gravity's constant input simply stops arriving, the inner ear sending contradictory signals to a brain that had spent an entire lifetime trusting it completely. Understanding a mechanism and surviving it turned out to be two very different clinical experiences, a distinction I'd apparently needed forty-one years to actually learn firsthand.

Cent took my arm without much ceremony. "Ready?"

"No," I said, honestly, the same honesty I gave every patient before a procedure they were frightened of. It seemed only fair to extend myself the same courtesy. "Proceed anyway."

The transition itself was instantaneous and strange in a way no briefing document had properly conveyed: one moment a sterile staging room under ordinary gravity, the next the low mechanical hum of a ship in deep space, recycled air thinner than I expected against my throat, and my own body suddenly, catastrophically without any sense of which way was down.

My inner ear did not handle the transition with anything like the composure I'd have demanded from a first-year resident. I went down hard against the safety tether, vision swimming, and understood with total clinical clarity, even mid-episode, which structures were misfiring and precisely why, which turned out to be no comfort at all.

Park caught me before Cent had to, which told me he'd expected exactly this and positioned himself accordingly, the same way I'd have positioned a second surgeon before a case I suspected might go sideways.

"Vitals?" I managed, once the worst of it passed, because some instincts run deeper than the immediate embarrassment of losing your own inner ear in front of Wayfarer's entire senior staff.

"Yours or the ship's?"

"Either. Both."

"You're fine, Doctor. Everyone goes down the first time. Cent went down the first time. Iverson went down the first time." He said it kindly, not the way people say things to soften a blow but the way they say things that are just true.

I found my footing eventually, one hand braced against the console, and looked out at the dark through the nearest viewport for the first time as something other than a photograph or a simulation. Four years from the nearest hospital in any direction, I thought, the exact fact I'd walked into this with eyes open, and found, oddly, that it didn't frighten me nearly as much now as the eleven minutes I'd just spent unable to control my own vestibular system in front of witnesses.

I'd spent my whole career being the calm one in the room. It was going to take some getting used to, being the newest, least steady person in this one instead.

✳

Chapter 38: Reyes POV: My father flew commercial for thirty-one years and used to say the sky doesn't care how good your instincts are, only how good your instruments are, and I built my entire career trying to prove him wrong about exactly that.

I was twenty-nine, an Air Force flight surgeon two years out of residency, the morning I signed off on a young captain named Doyle, cleared for a routine high-G training sortie. Textbook physical. Textbook numbers. Nothing in any instrument I had access to that morning suggested anything other than a healthy pilot fit to fly.

He lost consciousness at just over eight G, a G-LOC event so fast the recovery window closed before his own hands could do anything about it, and the instruments I'd trusted that morning had never once measured the thing that actually mattered: how a specific human body, on a specific day, with a specific night of bad sleep behind it that hadn't shown up on any chart, would actually respond to a stress no chart fully captures anyway.

He survived. I want that said first, because it matters, and because I spent long enough afterward telling the story in a way that buried it under everything else I felt. He survived, badly shaken, grounded for a season, alive. But I remember standing in that debrief room understanding, with total clarity, that I had cleared a healthy set of numbers instead of an actual person, and that the gap between those two things was where my whole field kept quietly failing people.

I didn't leave flight medicine. I went further into it instead, the specific stubbornness that runs in my family apparently skipping straight past my father's own version of the same lesson and landing on me in a different shape. If the instruments couldn't tell the whole story, I was going to build a practice that didn't only trust the instruments. Sleep. Cognitive load. The slow, cumulative erosion nobody's chart tracks because no single day of it looks alarming enough to flag. I spent a decade on the ISS program watching crews long enough to learn what six months of isolation actually does to a person in the parts no pre-flight physical ever screens for, and I came away from it certain of exactly one thing: human performance under sustained extremity isn't a subject you can study from a chart. You have to watch it happen, patiently, to people you already know well enough to catch the version of them that's starting to slip.

Nobody had ever designed a human-performance protocol for four years of interstellar transit, because nobody had ever needed one before this program existed. I didn't get recruited for Reach because I was the most credentialed flight surgeon NASA had access to, though I was, generally, in the running for that too. I got recruited because I was the one person in that whole search who'd already spent a career treating "the numbers look fine" as the beginning of an assessment instead of the end of one.

I think about that captain more than I let on. Every crew physical I've run since, on every mission before this one and on this one most of all, carries some version of him in it: the reminder that a chart clearing you is not the same thing as a person actually being ready, and that the distance between those two facts is where my whole job actually lives.

✳

Chapter 39: Reyes POV: I called the meeting the way I call most things that matter, straight, no dramatics, three names on a short list and a room booked for an hour we ended up needing closer to two.

"I want to settle this before we're four years from the nearest hospital instead of a hallway away from one," I said, once Fraiser and Park were both seated. "Not the org chart version. The actual version. Who does what when it's three in the morning and something's gone wrong."

"I assumed I was general coverage," Park said. "EVA medicine, backup for whatever you can't be two places for at once."

"You're more than backup," I said. "You're continuous coverage. I sleep. I take leave, theoretically, even out here. Every hour I'm not the one awake, you're not a substitute physician, you're the physician, full authority, not a placeholder waiting for me to wake up and confirm your own judgment."

Park considered that a moment, visibly weighing whether he was being handed real responsibility or a polite fiction of it. "You'd back a call I made without you."

"I'd back a call you made without me every time you made it inside your own actual competence, which is considerably wider than you give yourself credit for. If I start second-guessing my own deputy the moment my back's turned, I've built a system that only works when I'm personally in the room, and that's not a system, that's a bottleneck with a medical degree."

Fraiser, who'd been quiet through most of that exchange, leaned forward. "And me."

"You're the thing neither of us is," I said. "Evan and I keep people alive and functioning. You're what happens when keeping someone alive requires opening them up, and neither of us has your hands for that, and we both know it. You don't report into the general rotation. You're a standing resource the two of us call the moment either of us hits the edge of what flight medicine alone can manage."

"That's not a small carve-out," Fraiser said. "That's most of my actual value to this mission, structurally speaking, sitting outside your own chain of command."

"It's supposed to be. I didn't fight NASA for a dedicated surgical seat on this crew so I could bury her inside my own rotation and waste the reason I fought for it." I looked between the two of them, both good, both what four years and four light years demanded of a medical team that couldn't call anyone else for a second opinion. "I need to know, honestly, both of you, where your own limits actually sit. Not where you'd like them to sit. Where they actually do."

Park answered first, direct, no hedging. "Trauma beyond stabilization. Anything requiring more than a steady hand and a protocol I've already trained against."

"Anything outside general and trauma surgery," Fraiser said. "Cardiac beyond basic intervention, neuro beyond basic intervention. I'll do what the situation demands regardless, because refusing isn't actually an option out here, but I want it on record now, calm, instead of discovered live, where my own training stops being enough."

I wrote it down, plain language, no euphemism, exactly the way I wanted it available to all three of us at three in the morning someday when euphemism would only cost time we wouldn't have.

"That's the whole point of doing this now," I said, closing the folder. "Not because I expect to need it. Because the day I do need it is the worst possible day to be figuring out, for the first time, who's actually supposed to do what."

Nobody argued the premise. Nobody ever does, with that one. It's the reason the three of us understood each other, I think, faster and more completely than most of this crew understood each other at all: we'd all three already stood, at some point in our separate careers, in the specific room where a plan hadn't existed yet and should have.

✳

Chapter 40: Park POV: I got into EVA medicine because of a dead man named Callum Reyes, no relation, and I want to say up front that I have made that joke to our own Dr. Reyes exactly once and will not be making it again, on account of the look she gave me.

Callum was a saturation diver, forty-one years old, six weeks into a North Sea platform contract, and he died of a decompression event that three separate physicians before me had signed off as survivable risk on his intake physical. I was the fourth physician, the one who actually pulled his chart afterward and understood, reading it cold, that the risk had never been survivable at all, just quietly under-described by people who wanted the platform staffed more than they wanted to tell an experienced diver his own numbers didn't support another rotation.

I was twenty-eight. I decided, standing in that debrief room, that I was never again going to be a physician who signed off on a number instead of a person, and that whatever field let me actually be in the room, in the suit, in the water or the vacuum or wherever the actual risk lived, was the field I was going to spend my career in instead of a comfortable one somewhere safer.

Diving medicine taught me most of what EVA medicine turned out to need. Decompression physiology. Isolation psychology. The specific, patient art of monitoring someone whose suit is the only thing standing between their body and an environment that will kill them in under a minute if it fails, without letting your own voice ever once tell them you're worried, because a diver who hears worry in his surface support's voice makes worse decisions in the water, and an astronaut who hears it in mine makes worse decisions outside the hull. I got very good, over the years, at sounding entirely too cheerful about things that were not, strictly speaking, cheerful at all.

"You'll die of that face someday," a dive supervisor told me once, watching me crack jokes over the comm during a properly hairy ascent. "Somebody's going to flatline and you'll still be doing the weather-guy voice."

"The weather-guy voice is the reason nobody's flatlined yet," I told him, and I believed it, and I still believe it, all these years and one very different kind of vacuum later.

NASA found me through the diving-medicine literature, the same way they found half this crew's more unconventional seats, apparently. Reyes interviewed me herself, and I want it on the record that she asked exactly one question that actually mattered, buried in the middle of forty perfectly reasonable ones.

"What do you do," she asked, "when you're scared and the patient needs you not to be."

"I get cheerful. Loudly. On purpose. It's not denial, if that's what you're asking. It's a tool. I use it deliberately, the same way you'd use a specific drug for a specific presentation, and I turn it off the second the room actually needs something else from me instead."

She hired me on the spot, more or less, which I later learned was not how she usually ran interviews at all.

I think about Callum most when I'm suiting someone up for a first EVA, checking seals with my own hands the way nobody checked his, narrating every step out loud in a voice that sounds, I've been told more than once, like I'm hosting a mildly enthusiastic cooking show instead of clearing a human being for a walk in vacuum. It works. It has always worked. I intend to keep doing it exactly this way for as long as this crew needs someone cheerful standing between them and the numbers nobody wants to say out loud.

✳

Chapter 41: Cent POV: Pathfinder's launch didn't need an audience. It barely needed me, except that I happened to be the only engine it had.

I sat in the chair alone that morning, no crew, no hard dock, nobody watching but a handful of telemetry techs half a light-minute away who wouldn't even see the confirmation until well after the fact. It struck me, buckling in, that this was probably the least attended moment in the entire program's history, and also, by a wide margin, one of the most important. The first vehicle of any kind, built by anyone, ever actually leaving for another star. No fanfare. No cameras. Just me, a chair, and a hundred forty tons of hull, with minimal shielding, waiting for me to tell it where to go.

I took the increments the way I always did, one at a time, patient, the specific rhythm my whole body had learned over years of doing exactly this. Routine by then, honestly, closer in feeling to an ordinary ballast push than to anything I'd have called a launch if you'd described it to me cold. I remembered, pushing through the middle stages, the very first ballast push back at Kristen, how huge that had felt at the time, five hundred kilometers a second reading like the edge of the known universe. This wasn't even a fraction of the effort that first gate had cost me. I'd just gotten so much better at the thing itself that the enormous had quietly become the mundane, the way it always eventually does if you do something often enough.

Once we hit cruise, I unbuckled and got to work twinning the not-water cassettes and the forward Umbrella panels into their cradles, the same mechanic we'd proven a hundred times over during the ship-integration campaign, alone this time, with nobody to check my work but the telemetry itself. My hands knew the sequence without my head needing to supervise them closely, which left room for my mind to wander somewhere it usually didn't get to during work like this: all the way out to a star I couldn't see yet, four years and change away by light, closer now by one small probe's worth of momentum than it had been an hour ago.

I thought, working through the sequence, about how strange it was that the actual historic part of my job so rarely looked historic while it was happening. Nobody would ever make a documentary about cradle-seating checklists. Nobody would put this particular afternoon in a highlight reel. And yet here I was, alone, doing exactly the thing every schoolchild would eventually learn had started it all, and the only witnesses to it were a handful of telemetry techs half a light-minute away and my own two hands moving through a sequence they'd run so many times they barely needed me anymore.

Cradles seated. Locked. I logged Pathfinder as configured and coasting, gave the hull one last look, quiet and small against all that dark, and jumped back to Wayfarer. Mission accomplished. Nothing more dramatic, from where I sat, than a very long, very quiet afternoon of ordinary work, the kind of afternoon that would end up, I suspected, in a great many history books someday, without a single person alive that day having actually seen it happen.

✳

Chapter 42: Public tracking dashboard, NASA feed, open in a browser tab on however many million screens across the country, one of them belonging to a retired machinist in Ohio who'd watched every single Reach broadcast since the very first announcement and kept a private notebook, spiral-bound, cataloguing every velocity milestone the program had ever posted.

Telemetry scrolled by at a compressed, relativistic rate, matching the frame differential of Pathfinder's own 0.8c coast, numbers ticking past faster than a human eye could easily track. A toggle sat in the corner of the interface, offering to slow it down, decompressing the high-speed packets back into standard Earth time for anyone who wanted to actually read them instead of just watch them run. He always used the toggle. He liked to actually read the numbers, not just watch them blur past like weather.

The log stayed strictly clinical. No personal messages. No video journals. Machine parameters only: sensor readings, trajectory updates, localized diagnostics scrolling past in a language built for engineers, not an audience, though the audience watched anyway, in living rooms and break rooms and, in his case, a converted garage workshop with a single dedicated monitor bolted to the wall for exactly this purpose.

An ingest icon flared green on the dashboard. A Cent Express stack event, logged and timestamped, the air-gapped ground annex working through a physical bundle of read-only, checksummed drives that had bypassed the whole problem of interstellar radio lag entirely, carried home instead in someone's own two hands across a distance no signal could cross any faster.

The files cleared their sandbox integrity checks one after another. Radiation profiles. Micrometeoroid collision counts, real data pulled straight off the actual route instead of modeled from a desk. The dashboard's global parameters updated, and somewhere in a NASA engineering bay, Osei's team pulled that same data down to fine-tune the margins on shielding that hadn't even flown yet, while a machinist in Ohio wrote a new number down in his notebook, closed it, and went back to whatever he'd been doing before the icon flared, satisfied, the way a man is satisfied watching something enormous continue to work exactly the way it was supposed to.

He'd started the notebook the night of the very first announcement, years back, mostly on a whim, never expecting it to become the thing it had become: a private, careful record of a species learning to reach farther than it ever had, kept by a man who'd never once left his own state but who understood, in his own quiet way, that he was watching something worth writing down.

His name was Walt Hendricks, and he'd worked the same machine shop for thirty-four years before it closed, and he showed the notebook to exactly one person, a granddaughter studying aerospace engineering at a state school two hours away who called him every Sunday partly out of love and partly, he suspected, to hear the week's numbers read back to her in his own gravel-flat voice before she'd trust them from anywhere else. She'd told him once that his notebook had better data hygiene than half her actual coursework. He'd printed that compliment out and taped it inside the notebook's own cover, where it stayed, a small private proof that an old man in a converted garage in Ohio had turned out to matter to something enormous, in his own small, careful, unglamorous way.

✳

Chapter 43: Cent POV: I noticed the wear pattern because I was actually there, in the chair, close enough to see what the telemetry hadn't caught at all.

Routine stack run, nothing scheduled beyond a data pickup. I slid into Pathfinder's own seat and let my eyes do what the sensors apparently hadn't: a localized alignment shift, a wear pattern along the chassis, under its minimal shielding, that hadn't shown up in a single readout.

I wanted to fix it right there. I felt the itch of it, a problem sitting directly in front of me with my own hands free to solve it. I left the data stack behind instead, and jumped straight back to Wayfarer's command deck, because wanting to fix something myself has never once been a good enough reason to actually do it.

"Physical variance on the chassis," I told Truman. "Alignment shift, exterior wear, nothing the telemetry caught."

He didn't hesitate. "No remote patch. No unvetted bypass."

"I could probably..."

"No." He looked up from the console, steady, unmoved. "This is an interstellar vanguard probe. It gets direct, specialist hands-on inspection, not a guess pulled off a data feed. Not on our dime."

I went and got the specialists myself, a multi-passenger carry straight from Wayfarer into Pathfinder's cramped cockpit, working in situ while the probe kept coasting under us at speed. Two materials engineers, Okafor and a younger tech named Villareal who I'd only ever seen before in a hallway, folded themselves into a space built for exactly one occupant, tools braced against their own bodies since there was nowhere else to brace them, and worked the actual fault by touch as much as by instrument.

"Careful with your elbow," Okafor told Villareal, sharp but not unkind, "unless you'd like to be the second thing I have to explain to Truman today."

"Noted," Villareal said, and went very still for a moment, recalibrating whatever motion he'd been about to make.

I hovered near the hatch, technically useless to the actual repair and unwilling to leave anyway, watching two people I barely knew work a problem with more calm precision than I usually managed on things I'd trained on for years.

"There," Okafor said, after a long, careful ten minutes, running a gloved thumb along a seam that had, apparently, needed nothing more than a trained hand and a direct look to diagnose. "Micro-fracture propagation. The telemetry couldn't have caught this. It needed eyes."

They found the actual fault fast, once they had eyes on it directly, and fixed it clean, the whole repair over almost before I'd finished being nervous about how little room there was to work in. I made a note, watching them close the panel back up, tools passed hand to hand in a space too tight for either of them to see what the other was doing half the time, that I wanted their names on record somewhere that actually mattered, not just in a maintenance log nobody outside this program would ever read.

"Dude," I said to Truman afterward, mostly teasing, "do you ever get time to do your own job?"

"You are my job," he said, not even looking up.

I shunted the repair crew home and logged the fix, and thought, not for the first time, that I knew why he never seemed to be anywhere else.

✳

Chapter 44: Truman POV: Eighteen months of Pathfinder's own flight data sat cross-checked against Wayfarer's completed configuration by the time I called the room to order, and I knew what I was actually about to ask them to sign.

"Before anyone reaches for a pen," I said, "I want everyone in this room to understand what we're actually voting on. Not a probe this time."

Silence settled over the table.

"Pathfinder proved the route. What we're clearing today is human beings, following it. Real people, leaving the solar system, for the first time in the history of our species." I let that sit. "I want that understood before a single signature goes on anything."

Nobody moved to sign right away after that. Good. I hadn't wanted them to.

The executive oversight teams did their own work once the weight of it had actually landed: health metrics, planetary-protection compliance, interstellar abort margins, all of it routed down to the Situation Room in the next scheduled data dump, verified against every platform limit the administration had on the books. Real scrutiny, not a formality dressed up as one.

One of the newer oversight members, a health-policy attorney I didn't know well yet, raised her hand partway through. "What happens if someone on this list changes their mind between today and launch."

"They come off the list, no penalty, no record of it beyond a private note in a personnel file nobody outside this room will ever read," I said. "This isn't a contract that traps anyone into a decision made under pressure. Every single name on it gets one more private conversation with me, alone, no cameras, no committee, before we're done here today. If any of them want out, I'd rather lose them now than find out four light years from home that the yes I got wasn't really theirs to give."

Nobody left the room that day. I want that on the record too, since it mattered more to me than almost anything else in the whole certification process: sixteen separate, private, unwitnessed conversations, and sixteen separate people who looked me in the eye and said the same word again, on their own, with every real chance in the world to say something else instead.

Then I locked the manifest.

Sixteen names. Cent, our engine, and the two pilots I trusted with my own life without a second thought, Iverson and Childs. Osei, who'd built half the ship herself and understood every remaining system on it better than the people who'd designed them individually. Grebenchekov, back for a second impossible mission after nearly losing his life to the first one. Grace's slot, built around biology and a gift for teaching people things they didn't know they needed to understand yet. Fraiser's, verified now for zero-g trauma surgery on top of everything she already brought, alongside Park, Reyes, Rahal, Cho, Alvarez, Ochoa, Nasser, Harding, Carter. Every seat locked against the same five-hundred-sixty-metric-ton budget, with minimal shielding, we'd spent Part II proving, down to the last kilogram, no margin left unaccounted for.

I read through the list twice before I let myself sign it, picturing each face against each name, aware, in a way I rarely let myself dwell on, that I was about to formally commit every one of them to something no human being had ever actually survived attempting.

I signed last, the way I usually did, and sat with the manifest a moment before I closed the file. Eighteen months of watching a probe do the road-finding alone had ended in this: sixteen actual people, committed, real, about to follow it. I'd carried a lot of weight into rooms in my career. This one settled differently than most.

✳

Chapter 45: Cent POV: Tara and Jade showed up two days before launch with a bottle of wine they weren't going to be allowed past the outer checkpoint, and a level of good-natured indignation about that fact that told me how long they'd actually been planning the gesture.

"Confiscated it at the gate," Jade said, dropping into the chair across from me like she owned it, which, by that point in our friendship, she basically did. "Some very polite man in a NASA windbreaker gave me a receipt. A receipt, Cent. For wine."

"You'll get it back. Eventually. Probably."

"I want that in writing."

I'd known both of them since the same lunch table, first day at a new school, newly moved and not yet sure how to be a person in public. Tara had slid in across from me without asking. Jade had cut straight through the whole awkward silence with a joke about the cafeteria vegetables looking like something alive in a tide pool. I'd never once had to explain the actual chaos of my own life to either of them from scratch. By the time it needed explaining, they'd already decided, on their own, that they were staying anyway.

They'd gotten married two years back, quietly, a courthouse and four witnesses and a cake Tara had insisted on baking herself despite every warning that courthouse weddings and homemade three-tier cakes didn't mix logistically. It had, in fact, not mixed logistically. It had also been one of the best days of my life.

"So," Tara said, once the wine had been properly mourned. "You're really doing this."

"I'm really doing this."

"Four years. One way, unless the math works out different than everybody keeps telling us."

"One way, functionally. I'll visit. Sort of. It's complicated."

"Everything about you is complicated," Jade said, not unkindly, reaching over to squeeze my hand. "It has been since the day I asked if your name was like a smell or like a penny, and you didn't even blink answering."

"You wanted it fixed."

"I did. Doesn't mean you asked."

"You were there for the ones that actually mattered, though," I said. "Every single one. The night Grebenchekov's graft nearly went, I called Sterling first because that was the job, and then I called you, and you stayed on the phone with me until three in the morning saying absolutely nothing useful and exactly the right amount of nothing. When Nasser's appendix nearly killed her a hundred and forty million kilometers from the nearest real hospital, the two of you were the ones who showed up at my actual front door with soup I didn't ask for and never once ate, and just sat with me anyway until I stopped shaking."

"We showed up because that's what you do," Tara said, "when someone you love is quietly falling apart while insisting very loudly that she isn't."

"I wasn't falling apart."

"You alphabetized your own spice rack at one in the morning, Cent. I've known you a long time. That's not a woman who's fine."

We sat with that a minute, the comfortable quiet of people who'd already said the hard things a long time ago and didn't need to keep saying them to still mean them. Tara reached over eventually and took my other hand, the same unhesitating way she'd taken it a hundred times before, across a coffee shop table, at her own wedding, in exactly this configuration more times than either of us could probably count anymore.

"We'll be here," she said. "Whenever there's a here to be at. I don't fully understand the physics of any of it, and I don't need to. I just need you to come back."

"I'll come back."

"You'd better. Jade's already planning the vow renewal, and she wants you there for that one too, not just the courthouse version."

"I absolutely am," Jade said. "I've got a Pinterest board."

I laughed, the real, unguarded kind I didn't let myself do often enough, and understood, sitting there with both of them, how much of what carried me through everything else I did ran straight back to a coffee shop in a town I hadn't lived in for years, and a girl who'd once quoted my own words back at me to make me laugh when I badly needed it.

✳

Chapter 46: Cent POV: I'd met plenty of people's families by then, the same quarantine gauntlet running for all of them, mothers and fathers and siblings cleared through the same barrier I cleared myself through every time I crossed it. I'd never once met anyone's old gang before Harding's showed up in the visitor's lot three days before launch, six trucks that had clearly seen better decades parked in a row like they owned the place.

I only meant to walk through on my way to something else. I ended up staying the better part of an hour, because I'd never once watched six people talk over each other with that much obvious, easy love and wanted to just keep walking past it.

Aunt Meg had brought food, of course she had, a cooler the size of a footlocker that she wouldn't let anybody else carry, and she looked Harding over the exact way I imagine she'd looked him over a hundred times before, checking for damage the way the rest of us checked telemetry.

"You're too thin," she told him. "NASA doesn't feed you like I feed you."

"Nobody feeds anybody like you feed them, Aunt Meg. That's not NASA's failing. That's just math."

Laurence had a whole different energy than whatever Harding had once described to me, offhand, about a kid who'd built a relay rig out of his mother's garage. Some junior propulsion job had agreed with him. He kept trying to pry technical details out of Harding that Harding kept deflecting, cheerfully, citing classification levels I'm fairly sure didn't all actually apply.

"Come on," Laurence said. "Just the gimbal tolerances. I won't tell anyone."

"You'll tell everyone. You've never once kept a secret in your life."

"That's slander. Also completely accurate."

Mara didn't say much, which Harding had told me once was just how she was, but she stood close to him the whole visit in the specific way people stand close to something they're afraid won't be there the next time they look. Dusty, by contrast, had opinions about literally everything involving the mission, loud, immediate, several of them scientifically indefensible and delivered with total, joyful conviction anyway.

"You're gonna see an actual alien sky," he told Harding, for maybe the fourth time in an hour. "You're gonna read weather nobody's ever read before. That's not a job, man, that's basically a superpower with a badge attached."

"It's mostly spreadsheets," Harding said.

"It's spreadsheets on an alien planet. Completely different. Don't undersell it." Dusty clapped him on the shoulder, hard enough to rock him sideways. "You know what they're gonna call you in the history books someday? Extreme. Same as us."

"I have asked you, repeatedly, not to call me that in front of NASA people."

"And I have repeatedly not listened. It's tradition at this point."

Even Preacher had come, his hand still not fully right by the look of it, standing a little apart from the rest, content, a man who'd made his own peace with a smaller life and didn't envy anyone else's bigger one.

Rabbit had a printed checklist, actual paper, folded into careful quarters and worked through with a pen, and I watched him run it past Harding item by item, half of it real and half of it clearly invented on the spot for the occasion. "Emergency contacts updated?"

"You have my emergency contacts, Rabbit. You've had them for eleven years."

"Updated is different from had. Radiation exposure limits, you're tracking those yourself or is NASA tracking those for you?"

"NASA has slightly more experience with this than you do."

"I'm aware. I'm still asking." He checked something off anyway, satisfied by the tone if not the actual content of the answer, and folded the checklist back into its careful quarters. "Somebody in this parking lot has to keep asking the boring questions. It's never once been anybody but me, and I don't see why that changes just because the distance got bigger."

Dusty caught me watching, at some point in the middle of it, and when he spoke there was none of the usual noise behind it. "You know," he said, "we've been calling Bill the Extreme all these years. But you've got all of us beat, hands down."

I didn't have a good answer for that, not one that fit into a parking lot with six people determined to keep the goodbye a happy one, so I just bumped his shoulder with mine and let it stand as its own answer.

I watched all six of them orbit around Harding the way people orbit around someone they've loved long enough that the love doesn't need announcing anymore, teasing him, feeding him, needling him about gimbal tolerances, standing a little too close. Watching it, what I felt was recognition, not envy. I had people who'd known me that long. Tara and Jade, who'd forgiven me a lifetime ago for the specific chaos of knowing me in high school and kept choosing to anyway. A whole crew's worth of people from the mission that made this one possible, half of whom I still called at odd hours for no reason beyond wanting to hear their voices. Joe, longer than almost anyone. Watching Harding's gang love him loudly in a parking lot three days before he left the solar system for good didn't teach me I was missing something. It just reminded me of what I already had waiting on the other side of this same goodbye, in my own version of that same parking lot, whenever I let myself think about it.

Aunt Meg caught me watching, at some point, and gave me a long, assessing look I recognized immediately, because I'd been on the receiving end of exactly that look from my own mother more than once.

"Take care of him," she said. Not a question.

"I will," I said. "He mostly handles that part himself. But I will."

She nodded once, satisfied, the same nod I imagine she gives anyone she's already decided to trust, and went back to making sure everyone within reach had eaten something before the actual goodbyes started.

✳

Chapter 47: Osei / Halstead / Davy: Two sides of the same glass, and the man in the middle of it, watching the same fifteen seconds and understanding almost nothing the same way.

Osei POV: I ran the heating coils zone by zone, warming Davy's depth garment against the brine he'd be dropping into, flat confirmatory checks, no drama in any of it. Davy centered over the retention throat, stepped down, and twinned to the abyss the way he'd done it a dozen times before.

Containment throat camera, interior, wide-angle, mounted low against the curve of the wall. A man in a depth-rated suit standing at center frame, motionless, and then, between one recorded frame and the next, water instead, filling the same volume his body had occupied a frame earlier, a sphere with an edge the lens could actually resolve as an edge, not a splash, not a pour. The frame rate does not slow down for what it cannot otherwise explain.

Davy POV: The abyss doesn't announce itself, not even after however many times I've gone looking for it by now. One second I'm standing in a fabrication bay listening to Osei run through her own checklist in my ear, and the next the cold finds every seam in my suit at once, the water black and total and older than anything I have any real language for, pressing in from every direction like it's been waiting specifically for me to show up.

I don't stay long. I never do. Long enough to find the shape of it, hold the volume steady in my own head, and let go before the cold gets anywhere close to costing me something I can't get back. I surfaced back in the fabrication bay still half expecting to feel wet, and never once actually was.

Halstead POV: I'd spent eighteen years as chief engineer for a regional water authority, and not one day of that career had prepared me for a briefing packet that used the word teleportation without a hint of irony anywhere in it. I stood back from the observation glass with my binder held against my chest like it could stop something. My town's entire twenty-four-hour grid demand was about to clear this one room in fifteen seconds, and I'd run that arithmetic four times already that morning without it getting any easier to hold in my head.

Osei POV: The link triggered clean on my console. Position lock. Endpoint reassignment. Routine data, nothing more dramatic than a line item clearing green.

Halstead POV: On my side of the glass, a perfect, impossible sphere of dark alien water simply existed inside the containment throat, there without ever having arrived, and I felt my whole understanding of how the physical world worked take a step sideways it never quite recovered from.

Osei POV: The slam hit structure-borne, exactly as modeled. I watched the hydraulic accumulator pistons, each one scaled like a utility pole, drive into their nitrogen cushions and bleed the oscillation down through clean, predictable decay cycles until the whole system settled onto a flat plateau. Twenty-five thousand gallons a second, steady, unchanging.

Halstead POV: The shockwave came up through the concrete slab and into my heels before I heard it, and my binder actually buzzed against my ribs from the vibration. I flinched. I'm not ashamed of that. I'd have judged any engineer who didn't.

Osei POV: The roar held steady for minutes, no degradation, nothing the model hadn't already told us to expect.

Halstead POV: It kept going. And going. And somewhere around the two-minute mark I looked over and found two NASA technicians eating pastries by the console rows, utterly unbothered, and understood that my own panic had officially outlasted its welcome. Nothing was going to change. Nothing was going wrong.

I signed the tie-in clearance. Not because I felt confident. Because the evidence had simply worn me down past the point of arguing with it, and I've learned, over a long career, to recognize when that's actually the correct response.

Osei walked me out afterward, offered me a coffee I badly needed, and asked, conversationally, like she hadn't just upended eighteen years of my professional understanding of the physical world, whether I had any questions before I drove home.

"One," I said. "Does it get easier. Watching this."

"No," she said, without hesitating even slightly, which I appreciated more than a comforting lie would have earned from her. "You just get better at standing next to it while it happens."

I drove home with the binder still clutched against my chest out of habit, long after there was anything left in that room to actually be afraid of, and thought, somewhere past the county line, that I was going to need a considerably bigger binder for whatever came next.

✳

Chapter 48: McGarrity POV: I'd run election nights out of a war room for other people my entire career. This was the first one where the machinery was running for me instead of through me, and I didn't much care for how that felt.

Holcomb had invited me to watch it with him, in the Situation Room, not my own campaign headquarters, a quiet gesture that told me what I needed to know about where I actually stood with him after all these years. I sat at the central table while staffers murmured into secure lines around us, advisors leaning over chairs trading precinct numbers, the same institutional static I'd run myself for most of my career, low light and late-night stillness pressing in on all of it.

I tapped my pen against my knuckles, an old habit I'd never managed to break, and rolled my shoulders against a tension that had been sitting there since the polls closed.

The feed ran split, the way these things always did now. On the right, ordinary broadcast footage, a small town's mayor and a knot of residents standing on cracked ground, looking up at a municipal water tower that read, even compressed through a news feed, as unmistakably empty. On the left, Davy's own helmet camera, a tighter, stranger register, HUD readouts where the news side ran a chyron. I watched my own attention move between the two halves the way anyone reads a split screen, except I already knew, in a way that audience didn't yet, exactly what the left side was about to do to the right.

Davy stepped into the primary distribution conduit at one of NASA's own holding tanks, water already pressure-regulated, already tamed, waiting. The instant his body occupied that space, the twin opened, straight through to the tower's own internal riser pipe, no visible strain on the feed, no drama in it at all. Both ends of this one sat on Earth, under engineered hydraulics somebody had already spent months taming properly. It read, watching it, as mechanical. A heavy, deliberate siphon opening. Not a rupture.

Reservoir camera, interior, wide fisheye, timestamp running in the corner nobody watching live ever actually looked at. A man in a depth-rated suit, waist-deep, motionless for four-tenths of a second longer than the frame before it, and then simply not there. The water behind him did not ripple outward the way displaced water should. It went still all at once, the way a held breath goes still.

Water tower camera, interior riser pipe, infrared only, no ambient light this far inside a sealed structure. Empty pipe, dry corrosion staining visible on the walls, and then, in the same four-tenths of a second the first feed lost him, full. Water occupying space it had not occupied one frame earlier, rising, no point of entry either camera's own optics could actually resolve.

Davy POV: The tank went quiet around my knees the way it always did right before, water settling into that specific stillness I'd learned to read as ready rather than calm. I found the riser's own signature in my head, the exact shape of a pipe I'd never once physically stood in, memorized off a schematic and a single practice run months back, and let go.

There's no transition to describe, whatever the feeds end up making of it. Tank, then pipe. My own boots on a different floor, water already moving through the space my body had just finished occupying, the job already three-quarters done before my own head finished registering I'd actually moved. I held the link anyway, felt the exact texture of a system moving water at a rate that would have terrified me the first year I ever tried anything like this, and held it steady until pressure on both ends finally agreed with each other and let me go.

I don't get to see the tower fill. I never do. I get the moment before, and the moment after, and I made my peace, a long time ago, with everything in between being something other people get to watch instead of me.

McGarrity POV: Back on the right side, the field lines hummed audibly under the load, water flooding the town's own grid under hydraulics behaving exactly as engineered, no improvisation anywhere in it. Davy held the twin open, continuous, an invisible pipeline instead of a discrete event. I watched the room's reaction instead of the tower's own gauge for a moment: the Secretary of State leaning back, gripping the table's edge; the Defense Secretary muttering something I didn't quite catch and didn't need to; an advisor's hand flying up to cover her own mouth.

Holcomb just watched. His shoulders dropped, some weight I hadn't realized he was still carrying finally easing loose.

"We actually did it," he said, quiet, almost disbelieving, the words meant for no one else in the room, the exact tone of a man who'd spent years working toward this exact moment without ever quite letting himself believe it would arrive.

Then something in him gave. A short, surprised laugh broke loose before he could stop it, and he dropped back into his chair like his legs had gone out from under him, one hand coming up over his mouth, as if he could still catch the sound and pull it back in before anyone else in the room heard it.

"Sorry," he said, once he'd found his breath again, not sounding sorry at all. "My granddaughter used to make that exact sound. Some movie she watched a hundred times, girl practically falls out of her chair over it." He shook his head, still half laughing. "Took me until right now to understand why."

For a moment none of it had anything to do with the election at all.

It was only after that settled that my own eye drifted to the secondary displays, where the precinct maps had started moving in earnest, real contested ground from earlier in the night giving way, state by state, to a margin wider and more comfortable than anyone in the room had predicted going in. A landslide, bigger even than Holcomb's own blowout of a debut win, decisive, unambiguous, nothing left in it for anyone to argue with.

The room broke into a roar around us.

I understand now, in a way none of us said out loud in that room, exactly what that split screen actually did to the numbers coming in behind it. Nobody let a humanitarian miracle land in the middle of election night by accident. Somebody on his own staff had scheduled that test window with exquisite care, and everyone who mattered enough to know it kept the knowing to themselves, then and for years afterward. But it landed live, undeniable, precisely when the whole country was already watching the other screen, and I have never once been able to find the actual line between the man who delivered that water and the margin that came in an hour later. Holcomb never claimed the connection out loud, not that night, not after. He didn't need to. He was still watching Davy's own shoulders ease on the feed, relief on his face that had everything to do with the mission succeeding, and, I have no doubt at all, something quieter underneath it too, already counting the same numbers I was. He earned me that landslide deliberately, and never once said so where anyone could quote him on it.

Holcomb turned to me before anyone else had fully registered what the numbers meant. He didn't say anything. He just gave me a firm, heavy nod, private, the two of us alone inside all that noise, and I knew what he'd just handed me without either of us needing a single word for it.

The mission. The mandate. Everything that came with both.

I thought, sitting there with the room still roaring around me, about every war room I'd ever run for somebody else, every victory I'd helped build and then quietly handed off to the person whose name was actually on the ballot. This one had my own name on it, finally, after a career spent standing one careful step behind the chair instead of in it. I found I didn't feel the triumph I'd always assumed I'd feel, imagining this exact moment on the nights it seemed impossibly far away. I felt something heavier instead, and steadier, and sitting there, I knew the heavier feeling was probably the more honest one, and the one I was going to need to actually do the job well.

Bartlett POV: I was in that same room that night, off to one side with McGarrity's own skeleton campaign staff, close enough to watch it happen without ever being mistaken for someone who belonged at the center of it.

The sitting Chief of Staff introduced himself to me sometime after the numbers had settled and the roar had died down into something closer to exhausted celebration, offering his hand with the specific weary graciousness of a man watching someone else's future arrive while his own quietly wound down.

"You're the one who's been running his campaigns since the school board days," he said. Not a question.

"Something like that."

"He's going to need you in a real office soon, not just a war room." No bitterness in it at all, which surprised me more than the observation itself did. "Get some sleep first, if you can manage it. I never could, the one time it was my own name on the ballot."

I didn't know yet that he meant that literally, that this decent, tired man had run for something himself once, long before he became anyone's Chief of Staff, and lost. I found that out later, the way I found out most things about him: secondhand, offered without complaint, long after it might have changed how I treated him.

I caught it happening across the room before the handshake, before the introduction: Holcomb's composure cracking clean open, that short surprised laugh, one hand flying up over his mouth like he could still stuff the sound back in.

Something surfaced first, half-formed, faster than actual thought: two girls, shrieking with glee, superimposed for one disorienting second directly over the room in front of me, the Situation Room's own hard edges bleeding through some carpeted floor a world and a decade away. Gone before I could even ask myself where it had come from. Then it landed, whole. I knew exactly what that sound was before I'd finished consciously placing it. Some movie Chloe had made me sit through more times than either of us could count, some girl practically sliding out of her own chair with a joy too big to stay inside her body. I'd watched my own daughter do exactly what Holcomb had just done, hand over her mouth and everything, more times than I could count on one hand.

The memory hit before I could stop it, and some undignified sound of my own escaped right along with his, laughing at a joke nobody else in that room had any way of understanding, over an election that had nothing to do with either of us.

✳

Chapter 49: The drone held station six miles above a stretch of ground that hadn't seen real rain in going on four years, the operator's own console painting the terrain in the same cracked, colorless read it had carried for every pass that month, thermal and optical both agreeing on the same flat, parched nothing.

A secondary feed carried the other half of it: Davy, on the open concrete of the NASA hangar floor, finishing a life-support check that looked, next to what the primary screen was about to show, almost aggressively ordinary.

This deployment carried no containment hardware in the loop at all, first time in the whole sequence, because there was no vessel built on Earth that could actually absorb what was coming without becoming the story itself. His suit ran the heating-wire network doing double duty this time, fighting two temperature extremes at once instead of one: sixty below at the delivery altitude, twenty-eight degrees at the other end of the link, water kept liquid after however many million years by pressure and tidal heat alone, sitting right at the edge of its own salted freezing point rather than any comfortable distance above it. Reading the numbers off the console, the operator understood, professionally, that this was the mission profile with the least margin in the entire sequence, precisely because nothing mechanical stood between the physics and open sky anymore.

He jumped straight up first, target altitude, tracked on the telemetry as an instantaneous position change rather than anything resembling an ascent. He didn't fall. The twin held him locked against empty air, logged with the same flat professional notation as any other stable value, even though the actual fact underneath it, a man standing on nothing six miles up, wasn't ordinary at all. The same fraction of a second his body registered the thin upper air, he twinned again, straight down, from altitude to the Europan seafloor, miles under the ice, on the other side of an entirely different sky.

Helmet camera, exterior mount, timestamp continuous across both position changes with no gap the recording itself could actually account for. Frame one: thin blue-black upper atmosphere, curvature just visible at the frame's own edge, no ground, no reference plane at all. Frame two, same timestamp, following digit: absolute black, a single beam of the suit's own work light catching nothing but suspended sediment and the immediate texture of moving water. No transition recorded between the two frames. The camera does not explain gaps. It only reports what falls inside its own shutter.

Davy POV: I felt the air thin out before my body had properly understood what thin actually meant up there, and held, locked against nothing, no ground under me and none needed, the whole coordinate matrix doing the one job I'd asked of it. Then water. All at once, all directions, black and total, the kind of cold that doesn't announce itself gradually the way cold usually does. It just arrives, complete, and the coils in my own suit went to work answering it before I'd even finished registering the shift.

I couldn't hear anything but my own breathing and the ocean itself, a sound big enough it stopped being sound after the first few seconds and just became pressure instead, felt through the whole surface of my body rather than through my ears specifically. I held the shape of it in my head, the exact volume I'd promised to deliver and not one gallon more, and reached back across a distance I'd stopped trying to actually understand a long time ago.

Drone camera, optical, sixty-eight thousand feet, image stabilization compensating for wind shear that would otherwise have blurred the frame past use. Nothing in frame for eleven consecutive seconds but empty parched terrain and a clean horizon line. Then, entering from no visible direction, occupying space the previous frame had confirmed was simply open sky, a sphere. The camera's own exposure metering struggled through two full frames before it found a setting that could hold both the bright, sun-struck water and the dark ground beneath it in the same image.

Then, on the primary monitor, with no transition anyone could actually point to afterward, the globe existed. Whole. Massive. Suspended six miles over ground that hadn't held water in years, cohesive, solid-edged, spherical, refusing every single thing thin air should have done to it. The numbers still had to be read anyway, because that's the job: volume estimate, position hold, thermal bleed at the boundary layer where impossible ocean met ordinary cold sky. The numbers held. The image in front of them didn't care what the numbers thought about it.

The frame stayed up on the operator's own screen longer than the job strictly required, because nobody watching particularly wanted to look away from it yet.

The release, when it came, was instant. Every reading on his own suit slackened at once, tracked on the hangar feed as an immediate, visible easing through his own shoulders, and he twinned home, the anchor withdrawn from the sky in the same breath. Without him holding it, the globe didn't fall. It came apart instead, flash-atomizing into a rolling cloud deck that spread across the whole horizon on the optical feed, and out of that cloud, calibrated, steady, gentle in a way nothing else that day had been, rain actually began to fall.

The ground stayed on-screen a long time after the job was technically finished, rainfall rate and coverage area both holding exactly where they were supposed to hold, the operator's own attention somewhere past monitoring by then, closer, if anyone had asked, to simple witness.

✳

Chapter 50: Holcomb POV: McGarrity invited me back to the same room I'd once invited him into, and I noticed, walking in, that he'd changed almost nothing about how it was arranged, out of respect or habit or some mixture of the two I never quite worked out which of us should ask about first.

"Sit anywhere," he said. "This isn't a briefing."

His own Chief of Staff had a chair already pulled out for me before I'd finished crossing the room, a small kindness I recognized immediately, the same quiet instinct I'd watched him run for years before either of us ever ended up in a room like this one.

The main screen carried the same kind of split feed I remembered from my own turns in this chair, though the numbers scrolling underneath it this time belonged to weather models and drought indices instead of precinct returns. No election riding on tonight. Nothing on the ballot at all. Just the thing itself, allowed, for once, to simply be the thing itself.

I watched the globe of ocean water exist against an empty sky the same way I had the first time, only a few months removed from being the one sitting in this chair myself, and found the wonder hadn't worn down at all in that short a stretch, which surprised me more than it probably should have.

"You didn't have to do this," I told him, once the rain had started falling in calibrated sheets over ground that hadn't held real water in years.

"No," McGarrity agreed. "I wanted to." He didn't elaborate further than that, and I understood, sitting there, exactly why not. Some debts are better repaid than explained.

I thought about the night I'd handed him an evening exactly like this one: the firm, private nod I'd given him across a roaring room, expecting nothing back for it, not even a word. I'd told myself, at the time, that it was a small thing. Sitting on the other side of it now, for the first time in my life, I understood exactly how wrong I'd been. It wasn't small at all. It was the sudden, disorienting weight of being handed something enormous by a man who would never once ask you to say thank you for it. Some gestures only make sense once you've actually been on both sides of them.

✳

Chapter 51: Bartlett POV: McGarrity offered me the job three days after his own election, in a hotel suite that still smelled like the celebration nobody had fully cleaned up from the night before, and he did it with a directness that surprised exactly no one who'd actually known him as long as I had.

"I need someone who tells me the truth before I ask for it," he said. "Not after. Before. I've watched what happens to administrations that surround themselves with people who wait to be asked."

"I've been telling you the truth before you asked for it since before either of us had gray hair, Mr. President-elect. I don't imagine that's about to change now."

Something in his face eased, the same look he'd worn the day I talked a reluctant, overqualified professor into running for a school board seat neither of us had any business caring about, thirty-one years and a great many harder rooms ago. "I'm counting on that," he said. He didn't elaborate further than that, and even then, I knew this was simply how the man operated: decisions arrived at through some internal process he rarely showed anyone, then delivered whole, already final, rarely revisited once made.

I said yes before I'd finished thinking through everything the job would actually cost me, which felt, in retrospect, like an appropriate way to begin working for a man whose entire career I'd talked him into in the first place.

The months between his election and his inauguration passed the way I imagine most transitions pass, equal parts ceremony and genuine labor, briefing books thick enough to double as furniture, a security clearance process that made me feel, more than once, like a man being slowly disassembled and reassembled by people who'd never once smiled at me. I met the sitting Chief of Staff during that stretch, a decent, tired man near the end of a long, honorable run, who took it upon himself, without anyone asking him to, to make sure I inherited an office in better order than the one he'd been handed.

"I want this to go well," he told me, more than once, in exactly those words, and I believed him every time.

The meeting I actually want to describe happened in that same stretch, before the oath, before the actual chair changed hands, a meeting that was, technically, still someone else's to run, which struck me as an entirely appropriate way to begin learning a job I hadn't started yet.

The outgoing Chief of Staff ran the room by long habit, easy in it, and I knew I was there at McGarrity's own request, present to absorb the thing firsthand rather than receive it secondhand after the fact, the way a man learns a trade by watching someone competent do it in front of him rather than reading about it later. There was real warmth between the two staffs in that room, not rivalry. The outgoing side wanted this handoff to go well. I could feel it in the way they included me at all.

The findings came one at a time, and none of them got the bureaucratic-list treatment I half expected. Vehicle safety. Earth-departure geometry. Mission duration. Crew consent. International notification. Planetary protection. Physical security. Operational security. Abort limits. Holcomb worked through each one with the same forceful, adamant register I'd already heard described to me by people who'd sat in rooms with him before. I watched more than I listened, if I'm honest, cataloguing not just what he said but how he carried himself saying it, a manner I suspected I'd either need to inherit or deliberately depart from before too long.

Shipboard audio played low under everything, Truman's team somewhere far off running through ordinary pre-launch chatter, muted enough that Holcomb's own voice carried the room without competition.

He signed at the end of it. Cent's own approval, and his approval of the post-boost shielding protocol, both recorded separately, distinct signatures for distinct consents.

I studied the page a moment too long after he set the pen down, long enough that the outgoing Chief of Staff glanced over, clearly expecting a question about countersignatures or filing procedure.

"You know the word authorize comes from the Latin augere," I said. "To make something grow. Everyone treats authorization like permission. It isn't, really. It's cultivation."

I closed the folder before anyone could decide whether that had actually been a question, already reaching for my coat, already thinking about Wednesday. Nobody in the room quite knew what to do with that, which was, I was beginning to suspect, going to be my whole method going forward.

✳

Chapter 52: Bartlett POV: The oath took twenty-two seconds. Everything after it took considerably longer, and none of it slowed down for the rest of the day.

"Chief," McGarrity said, already moving, already three strides down the hall before I caught up with him, the Marine Band still packing up somewhere behind us, "walk with me."

"That's generally the idea, sir."

"Don't call me sir yet, it still sounds like you're talking to my father." He didn't break stride. "What's on fire?"

"Nothing's on fire. Several things are smoking."

"Smoking's fine. Smoking I can work with. What's the worst one?"

"Depends whether you mean worst for the country or worst for the next forty-eight hours of your life."

"Both. Quickly."

I fell into step beside him, and a staffer I didn't yet know by name fell in on his other side with a folder, and somewhere behind both of us someone else was already talking into a phone about motorcade logistics for an event none of us had confirmed he was attending yet.

"Confirmation hearings for two Cabinet seats are stalled in committee," I said. "Agriculture and Commerce. Neither's actually contested, both are just sitting there because the outgoing committee chair wanted a scheduling win before he handed off the gavel."

"Call him. Personally, not through staff. Tell him I remember every favor owed and every one still open, and I'd like both seats moving by Friday."

"That's a threat."

"It's a fact delivered with excellent posture. There's a difference." He took the folder from the staffer without breaking stride, flipped it open, closed it again. "What else."

"Reach needs your signature on the final launch authorization inside the week. Truman's people say the crew's already through their own consent process. They're waiting on you now, not the other way around."

That landed differently than the committee business had. He actually slowed, half a step, before catching himself and speeding back up.

"I've read the file. All of it, not the summary." He glanced at me. "You've read it too."

"I sat in the room when Holcomb signed off on the destination review. I've read everything since."

"Good." A beat. "Tell me the truth. Not the diplomatic version. Are we actually ready to send sixteen people four light-years from home?"

"I think the honest answer is nobody's ever been ready for something like this, and the program's spent four years making sure that not being ready doesn't matter as much as it normally would."

He almost smiled at that. "That's a very Bartlett answer."

"I've only had the job six hours."

"You'll notice I already knew that was the answer before you gave it. That's why you have the job."

We turned a corner into a knot of people that included, as far as I could tell in the three seconds I had to assess it, the National Security Advisor, someone from the press office already looking harried, and a Navy officer carrying something in a leather satchel that I decided not to ask about on my first day.

"Mr. President." The NSA fell in alongside without preamble. "Five minutes on the overnight cables before your first Oval meeting."

"Walking meeting. Bartlett, stay."

"I wasn't planning on leaving."

The NSA gave me a look that suggested six hours wasn't nearly long enough to have earned staying, and I gave him a look back that suggested I'd noticed the look and filed it for later, and neither of us said anything about it because the President was already three sentences into a briefing on something involving a shipping lane I made a private note to actually understand before tomorrow.

"...and the second item," the NSA was saying, "concerns Project Reach's own security posture, now that command authority formally transfers to you at noon."

"It transferred at noon." McGarrity checked a watch I hadn't seen him put on. "It's currently twelve nineteen. Catch up."

"Protective intelligence wants your personal sign-off reaffirming the existing doctrine. Verbatim, they were specific about that. They don't want a new policy. They want you to say the old one still holds, in your own voice, on the record."

"It holds." He didn't slow down to say it, didn't need to. "Protect, not possess. Same as it's been since before I had this job. Draft the statement, I'll have it back to you by end of day, and it will say what the last one said, because nothing about that changed just because the chair did."

"Yes, sir."

"And somebody find out who scheduled my first full Cabinet meeting for the exact hour Reach's own launch window opens, because I refuse to believe that was an accident, and I want to know if I should be flattered or annoyed that my own scheduling office thinks that highly of my multitasking."

Nobody answered that directly, which I was beginning to understand was itself a kind of answer.

We reached the Oval sometime in the middle of a sentence about the shipping lane, and McGarrity stopped, finally, one hand on the door, and looked back at the small crowd that had accumulated behind him over four hundred feet of hallway.

"Everybody who isn't Bartlett, give us the room for ten minutes."

They went. It was, I noticed, the first time all day anyone had actually stopped moving.

"You held up," he said, once the door closed.

"It's been six hours."

"I know. That's usually long enough to tell." He sat down behind the desk, the first time, I realized, as its actual occupant, and something in his shoulders changed, some weight settling into a shape it hadn't held an hour before. "Reach launches under my authority now. Not his anymore. Mine."

"How does that sit with you?"

He didn't answer right away, which told me more than an answer would have.

"Like every other thing today," he said finally. "Too big to feel properly yet. Ask me again once it's actually happened."

"I intend to."

He almost smiled again, the second time that day, and reached for the first folder of what I already knew, correctly, was going to be several thousand more just like it.

✳

Chapter 53: Carter POV: I was rejected for a flight seat four times before Reach, and I want to be precise about that number, because I've heard it rounded up and down by people trying to be kind to me in one direction or the other, and the actual number is the only version of the story that ever taught me anything useful.

The first rejection made sense. I was young, thin on hours, a physicist who'd only just finished cross-training into engineering and hadn't yet built the flight record to back up either credential properly. I took the note, built the hours, requalified.

The second rejection didn't make sense, not for a long time, until a review board member who liked me enough to be honest told me the actual reason over a drink I hadn't expected to get. "You're too broad," she said. "Every mission wants a specialist who's the best in the world at one specific thing. You keep showing up as very good at six things instead, and it makes selection committees nervous, because they don't know which box to put you in."

I could have narrowed myself down. Plenty of people advised me to, kindly, over the years, the same advice delivered a dozen different ways: pick a lane, become unmistakably the best in it, stop making committees do the extra work of figuring out where you fit. I got the advice. I never once took it, because narrowing myself down felt, every time I considered it seriously, like agreeing that the things I was curious about were a liability instead of the actual shape of who I was.

The third rejection cost me the most, privately, because I'd let myself believe going in that it was finally going to break my way. I didn't talk about that one for almost a year. I requalified anyway, all of it, flight physical, zero-g familiarization, every certification with an expiration date on it renewed on schedule like clockwork, because stopping would have meant admitting the fourth rejection, whenever it came, might actually be the last word instead of just the next chapter.

The fourth rejection was the one that finally taught me something true instead of just something painful. I sat in my car afterward, in a parking garage I still remember with more clarity than I'd like, and asked myself honestly, for the first time, whether I actually wanted a flight seat or whether I'd just spent so many years wanting one that stopping felt like a bigger loss than continuing did. I decided, sitting there, that the answer was yes, I actually wanted it, cleanly, without the sunk cost doing the deciding for me. So I requalified a fourth time. I didn't know yet that I was requalifying for a program that hadn't been announced.

Reach needed exactly the thing every previous committee had found inconvenient about me: someone who could own communications, deep-space navigation, Pathfinder's own telemetry, relativistic pointing, signal analysis, and enough raw physics to cross-cover half the ship's other specialists in a genuine emergency, all in the same seat, because a four-year interstellar transit doesn't get the luxury of six separate narrow specialists when one broad one will actually do the job better. I read the mission profile the first time and understood, with a clarity I'd never quite gotten from any rejection letter, what every one of those four denials had actually been protecting me for, whether anyone involved had meant it that way or not.

Truman didn't reject me. I want that on the record too, since it matters as much as the four times that came before it. He read my file, called me himself instead of routing it through a form letter, and asked exactly one question.

"Why do you think four committees passed on you?"

"Because I never fit cleanly into what any one of them was actually looking for."

"I'm not looking for clean," he said. "I'm looking for someone who can hold four different jobs at once without dropping any of them, on a ship that won't have the luxury of a specialist for every single thing that could conceivably go wrong four light years from anyone who could help. You understand that's what I'm actually offering you."

"I understand what you're offering me. I've been training for it since before either of us knew it existed."

I signed my flight certification for Reach the same week I turned in my fourth renewed qualification packet, the one I'd built for a program that hadn't announced itself yet, on the theory that stopping was never actually going to be the thing that got me a seat. I was right. It only took thirteen years and four closed doors to prove it.

✳

Chapter 54: Carter POV: Station readiness. Systems verification. The four-indicator gimbal board. I ran the checklist the way I'd run every checklist in my life, precise, procedural, refusing myself the luxury of feeling anything about it until the actual work was done.

I'd been passed over for flight seats four times before this one. This was the mission that finally needed what I'd spent a career building myself into, and I wasn't going to let myself feel that yet either, not with department call-outs still coming in around me. There would be time for feeling it later, once the board actually confirmed we'd left, and not one second before.

Cent could leave this ship any time she wanted to. The rest of us couldn't. I let that thought surface once, brief, unbidden, and put it back down where it belonged, because there was a checklist in front of me and feelings didn't belong on it.

"Wayfarer, you are go for launch."

The President's own voice, clean and short, over ship's comms. Not Truman's line to give, not Sterling's. His, personally. I clocked it as the small, unstated marker it was and didn't let it break my rhythm at all.

A screen near my station carried the same split feed the public was watching, closed captions running on both sides. From where I sat, though, the mix ran backward from what the world outside was hearing. My own checklist stayed at full volume around me, real and immediate. The President's address played low on that one screen, muted, background noise I half-caught the way anyone half-catches a television left on during actual work.

"With these first steps," his voice said, distant under everything else I was doing, "we do not merely leave our own solar system. We begin the journey to another star."

I kept working.

"Tonight, sixteen people sit aboard the ship that road was built for, running through the same checklist any of us would recognize, waiting on a word that is mine to give."

Mine, I thought, briefly, and went back to my board.

"Godspeed, Wayfarer," I heard, faint under my own console chatter. "Go find out how far this really goes."

Truman called the poll station by station, the same callback he always used. "I need a go/no-go for boost."

One by one, the departments answered plain and immediate, no more ceremony attached to it than any other checklist item, and answering my own in turn, I knew this exact discipline was what would let everything that came after actually mean something instead of just feeling big in the moment.

"Navigation," Truman said.

"Go," I said, and heard, in the flatness of my own single word, thirteen years of requalification packets, four closed doors, and a review board member's honest drink-fueled confession about being too broad for anyone's comfort, all of it compressed down into one syllable nobody in that room had any reason to know the actual weight of.

Cent answered last.

"Go."

The instant every station cleared, the sequence began, and the last thing I registered before the boost took over everything was the ordinary, disciplined shape of my own hands still moving through the checklist even as the ship itself started to change under me.

I let myself feel it, once, exactly the way I'd promised myself I would: four rejections, thirteen years, a whole career spent being told I fit nowhere cleanly, all of it arriving, finally, at a board that had just gone green because it needed every single thing about me that six separate committees had once found inconvenient. I didn't say any of that out loud. I didn't need to. I just kept working, the same discipline that had gotten me here doing what it had always done, quietly, competently, without asking for anyone's applause.
