Chapter 55: Live feed, same as it always ran, except every network that still bothered with scheduled programming had already cleared the whole morning for it instead of a segment.

"Four years ago," the President said, live from a podium banked with more microphones than any single speech of his had ever actually needed, "a young woman stood beside me and told the world she wanted to know how far this could go. In a few minutes, we are going to find out."

In a diner outside Amarillo, a waitress who hadn't missed a single broadcast since the program's own first press conference propped her phone against the napkin dispenser and refilled coffee nobody was drinking anymore, every booth in the place turned the same direction at once.

Mission control's own voice ran underneath the President's the whole time, faint, procedural, easy to miss if you weren't listening for it specifically: Truman, confirming stage after stage in the same flat cadence he used for everything, a sound most of the country never consciously registered even though it was the actual thing making any of the rest of this possible.

"Sixty-some years ago, this agency lost three men on a launch pad before any of them ever actually got the chance to leave it," the President said. "We did not stop. We flew suborbital. We reached orbit. We walked on the Moon. We went to Mars, and then to Europa, and every single step of that entire history has been a step into something untouched. We never once pretended otherwise, to ourselves or to any of you. What we did, every time, was close as much of the unknown as honest preparation could close, and tell you the truth about whatever was left over. Today is no different in kind. Only in degree. Sixteen people are about to cross a threshold no human being has ever even been close enough to consider crossing before, carrying whatever honest unknown four years of work, and sixty-some years before that, couldn't remove. I would like the record to show that every single one of them volunteered as soon as they were given the opportunity."

A retired Air Force sergeant in Tucson, who'd tracked every launch since before the President himself was old enough to vote, stood at attention in his own living room without quite deciding to, the way an old habit sometimes decides for you instead.

Somewhere underneath the address, mixed so quiet most feeds never bothered bringing it up any louder, Truman's voice said, "Go for boost," and sixteen people the whole country had spent four years learning the names of went.

"Godspeed," the President said, and for once in a career built on careful language, didn't try to improve on the plainest word available to him.

The feed held on the telemetry plot long after the President had already stepped back from the podium, a single climbing line, patient, unhurried, headed somewhere no camera on Earth would ever actually be able to follow it.

✳

Chapter 56: Cent POV: I'd run this exact profile before, kick for kick, board for board, out to a deep-system baseline with nothing riding on it but a spent booster frame and my own nerve. This time it wasn't a test, and that difference sat in my chest the entire way up, quiet, undramatic, impossible to ignore.

Long, controlled, nothing like a single dramatic shove. Incremental kicks, the four-indicator board holding green through every single one, haptic grips steady in my hands the same disciplined rhythm I'd run dozens of times by then. Except every increment now carried fifteen other people who couldn't leave if I got any of it wrong, fifteen people who trusted their whole lives to whatever my hands did next, and I felt that weight settle into the rhythm of the kicks themselves, a fraction heavier than muscle memory alone accounted for.

I caught Grace's eye once, early in the sequence, strapped in a few seats back, his knuckles pale on the armrests, his notebook forgotten in his lap for once. He'd been through his own back-to-back firsts only months before. This was mine now, the actual thing all of it had been building toward, and I gave him a small nod that I hoped read as more confident than I entirely felt.

I wasn't afraid of the mechanics. Those were proven, solid, mine down to the bone by now, four years of testing behind every single increment I fed the ship. What sat with me instead was something quieter, something closer to the weight of a promise finally coming due: doing something irreversible, for real, with real stakes, instead of a qualification flight with a clean path home waiting behind me the whole time. There was no clean path home from this one. There was only forward, kick after kick, until we reached the number we'd built this entire program to reach.

I felt the cabin around me the way I hadn't let myself feel it during any of the testing runs: Osei's steady breathing two rows back, Fraiser's hand resting flat against her own restraint strap like she was checking a patient's pulse, Harding with his eyes closed, lips moving slightly, running some private ritual of his own I didn't have a name for and didn't need one to respect. Sixteen separate people, sixteen separate ways of holding the same enormous fact steady in their own chests, and all of it riding, ultimately, on the same pair of hands.

The board held. Kick after kick, clean, Truman's voice steady over the loop confirming each stage before I moved to the next, the same disciplined call-and-response we'd run a hundred times in testing, except every single person answering it now was actually going somewhere none of them could take back.

We settled into cruise behind Pathfinder, 0.8c confirmed across every station, and I let myself feel exactly one thing about it before I went back to work, before the checklist demanded my full attention again, before there was time for anything but the job in front of me.

We were actually going.

✳

Chapter 57: Truman POV: A clock on my own display ticked down toward full velocity, undramatic, just a number closing on zero the same way it had through every qualification test I'd sat through before this one. Except this time, there was no braking boost waiting on the other side of it.

Zero.

0.8c, confirmed across every board.

I didn't say anything. That's not how I run. Something quieter moved through me instead, close to the same pattern that's shaped most of my career, watching danger I couldn't stop and carrying what it cost. Except this time there was nothing to be helpless about. This time the thing I'd built my whole life around actually worked, clean, exactly as designed, and I sat with that in a register I didn't have a name for.

A second clock started mattering the instant we hit velocity. Ship proper time. Earth's own frame. Two numbers that had read the same since launch, starting now, quietly, to pull apart from each other for real.

I logged the milestone the way I logged everything, plain procedural language, official record, nothing in the entry itself hinting at how enormous the thing it described actually was. Cruise velocity 0.8c confirmed, all stations nominal, mission proceeding as planned. Nine words, for the first time in the history of the species that a crewed vessel had reached a real fraction of the speed of light and settled into it clean. That gap, between the flatness of the words and the size of what they held, was the whole feeling, as far as I was concerned. I didn't need more than that.

I thought, briefly, of a nineteen-year-old standing too far from a test stand to do anything but watch, and of every catastrophe since that had asked the same helpless thing of me. This was the first time the watching had ended somewhere good.

I noticed, sitting there, that I hadn't reached for the mantra. First time since Apollo 1 that the moment hadn't needed it. Suck it up, man. You got this. Neither one came, because for once there was nothing here to endure.

I didn't say that out loud either. I just sat with the absence of it a moment, understanding, quietly, that this was what the whole pattern had always been building toward, arrived at last.

Sterling's voice came through the loop a moment later, quieter than his usual register, meant for me alone rather than the record. "You good?"

"I'm good." I found I meant it, which surprised me more than the milestone itself had. "I keep waiting to feel the thing I've felt every other time something this size actually worked. It's not coming."

"Maybe it doesn't have to, every time. Maybe some of them are just allowed to be good, clean, what they look like on paper."

"Since when did you get philosophical."

"Since I watched a species leave its own solar system for the first time in its entire history and didn't have anything smarter to say about it than good job." A pause, and something almost like a laugh underneath it. "Log the milestone, Truman. I'll be here when you're done."

The ship had left the solar system. Alpha Centauri wasn't an aspiration on a flight plan anymore. It was where we were actually going.

✳

Chapter 58: Cent POV: The stack runs started feeling ordinary faster than I expected. Everything does, eventually, even four light years of distance, once you've done it often enough.

Twice a week I twinned a package of hardened drives straight into a receiving cradle aboard Wayfarer, the same mechanic we'd proven for shielding modules, no full decon required for what had become a mundane chore. Somewhere along the way, without anyone deciding it officially, the whole operation picked up a name nobody had ever tried to make official either.

"Cent Express," Grebenchekov said one day, grinning, and it stuck immediately, the way the good nicknames always do.

I watched the two clocks start their long, slow divergence on every display I passed. Ship proper time. Earth's frame. The gap between them widened a little more with every cruise week that went by, visible now in a way it had only been a number before launch.

The family visits followed their own strict rhythm. Mom and Dad had to clear quarantine same as any returning crew member, the barrier protecting both directions, not just one, no exceptions carved out for being my parents. I suited up and decontaminated every time I actually crossed into Wayfarer's living spaces from a real visit home, the same discipline I demanded of everyone else, applied to myself without exception, because a rule that bent for me wasn't actually a rule at all.

I remembered one visit in particular, maybe a year and a half into the cruise by ship time, Mom sitting across from me at the kitchen table back home, both of us on the good side of the barrier for once, an ordinary afternoon with ordinary tea going cold between us.

"You look tired," she said, the same thing she'd said to me over a comm line years before any of this, back when tired meant something so much smaller than it did now.

"I am tired. Good tired, though."

"Is there a bad kind, in your line of work?"

"Constantly." I wrapped my hands around the mug for the warmth of it, something I never got out on Wayfarer, where the air always ran a fraction too climate-controlled to feel like anything at all. "But this isn't that kind."

She studied me a long moment, the specific way she'd studied me my whole life, reading things off my face I hadn't said out loud yet.

"You're happy," she said, like she was confirming a hypothesis rather than asking a question.

"I think I am. I don't know if I expected to be, given what this actually is. But I am."

Dad came in from the workshop then, sawdust on his shirt, and the conversation moved on to smaller things, the way conversations in that kitchen always eventually did, and I let it, grateful for the ordinary weight of it, an afternoon that could have belonged to any family anywhere, except that in a few hours I'd be twinning back into a ship traveling faster than anything humanity had ever built before, carrying that ordinary warmth with me the whole way.

Tara and Jade got their own slot in the rotation, same as my parents, and guarded it just as jealously. I'd show up on their porch some ferrying afternoon with exactly enough time for one cup of coffee before the next scheduled Cent Express run, and they'd already have a mug poured and waiting, timed off my own last text, like I'd never actually left in the first place. Jade kept a running, deeply unscientific tally of how many times I'd shown up mid-sentence, mid-meal, mid-anything, and read it back to me with real ceremony every time it hit a new round number. Tara mostly just made room at the table without asking, the exact same way she'd made room at a coffee shop table years before either of us had any idea what either of our lives were actually about to become.

"You look thinner in space," Tara told me once, squinting at me over her mug like she was running an actual clinical assessment.

"It's an optical illusion. The uniform's slimming."

"It is not. I've seen the uniform. The uniform is a garbage bag with delusions."

I laughed hard enough that Jade had to thump me on the back, and thought, not for the first time, that whatever else four light years cost me, it had never once managed to touch this particular hour of my week.

It became, eventually, just how our life worked. Strange, if I stopped to actually think about it. Ordinary, if I didn't.

✳

Chapter 59: Grebenchekov POV: Somewhere around month eight of a cruise that had already started feeling permanent, I knew the mission had a problem nobody in public affairs had gotten around to naming yet, and that I was, apparently, going to be the one who solved it.

NASA's own feed did excellent work with the facts: velocity, trajectory, system status, all of it accurate, all of it necessary, none of it anything a normal person could actually feel while reading it. I had watched, from four light years out, the official channel's engagement numbers decline steadily for months, the specific slow fade of an audience losing its grip on something too large and too quiet to hold onto without help.

I raised it with Truman first, the way I raised anything I thought worth doing properly.

"People are losing interest."

"People are watching a ship travel in a straight line for four years. I'm not sure what you expect me to do about that."

"Nothing official. Let me do it instead."

"Do what, exactly."

"Show them what it is actually like. Not the numbers. The living."

He considered it longer than I expected, which I'd learned by then meant he was taking it seriously rather than dismissing it politely. "Cleared through public affairs. Nothing classified, nothing that reads as an official mission statement, and nothing," he added, with the look I recognized from exactly one other conversation in my life, "that makes NASA regret trusting a cosmonaut with a camera."

I did not disappoint him, mostly. I posted the coffee machine, which Alvarez had rebuilt twice by then and still made noises no coffee machine should make. I posted Earth, shrinking, then gone, then nothing but a bright point among other bright points, and underneath it a single line: home is a direction now, not a place. That one did better than anything NASA's official feed had managed in a year. I posted Grace teaching Rahal a mnemonic for stellar classification using a system he'd apparently built for actual twelve-year-olds, and Rahal's real, delighted laugh at how well it worked on her too. I posted the Cent Express manifest logs, dressed up as a delivery tracking app, four years' worth of Cent's own quiet heroics reframed as the most reliable shipping service in the solar system.

I found, doing it, that I had a talent for the small thing that made the large thing bearable. I had not expected that about myself. I had expected, if anything, to be terrible at it, a man who had spent his career being precise finding himself suddenly asked to be charming instead. It turned out the two weren't as far apart as I'd assumed. Precision, applied to a joke instead of a gimbal tolerance, is still precision.

The phrase itself started small, an accident, the way the good ones always do. Cent had threaded a return approach through a debris field Osei's models had called borderline, clean, unhurried, the kind of flying that looked easy specifically because it wasn't. I filmed the tail end of it without thinking much about the caption, typed three words while my hands were still half full of adrenaline on her behalf, and posted it before I'd properly considered whether it was worth posting at all.

Like a boss.

It wasn't even my own phrase, not originally. I'd grown up on the same handful of decades-old sketch clips everyone still passed around without remembering where they'd first found them, the same three words already a fossil by the time I typed them, some comedian in sunglasses walking away from an explosion in a video older than I was. I didn't expect anyone to catch the reference. Enough of them did anyway, which told me something about exactly how long a good bit can stay funny if nobody ever actually lets it die.

It should not have worked as well as it did. I have turned that over more than once, wondering what made two ordinary words catch the way they caught, and never landed on an answer better than: people needed something short enough to say back, and something old enough to already feel like theirs. Within a week, half the replies under anything Cent Express related used it unprompted. Within a month, it had become, without my planning any of it, the closest thing this program had to a mascot phrase, mine by accident and hers by association, and neither of us minded that especially.

I went looking for the right format myself before the month was out, deep enough into the same half-forgotten internet by then that I already knew exactly where to find it: black background, white block text, sincerity dialed up past the point of parody until it curdled into something funnier than sincerity ever actually was. I built Cent's own version myself, her silhouette dropped into the exact pose from that same old clip, sunglasses added digitally since she'd never once actually worn a pair on camera in her life. I made it my own header within the hour, and have never once regretted the afternoon it cost me.

I did not tell anyone, filming any of it, why I'd wanted this job in the first place. It was not complicated enough to need explaining, and I am not, by nature, a man who explains himself unprompted. But I will say it here, once, the way I say most true things, straight and only when asked: I had already been given one unearned extra year of my own life once, back on Earth, in a hospital bed I nearly didn't leave. I knew, better than most people on this ship, how easily none of this happens at all. Posting a coffee machine, a shrinking star, a girl flying through debris like it was nothing, was the smallest, most honest way I knew to keep saying the same thing over and over, to a few million strangers who had no idea that was what I meant by it.

We are here. Still. Somehow. Like a boss.

✳

Chapter 60: Bartlett POV: The broadcast was already underway when it happened, live, mid-sentence, in front of an audience I couldn't begin to estimate the size of. I'd been Chief of Staff long enough by then to have stopped flinching at the size of these audiences, or so I'd told myself. I was wrong about that, as it turned out, within the next ninety seconds.

Cent's face sat on the split feed the way it always did during these joint updates, mid-address alongside the President, her voice carrying across four light years of delay compressed down into something that felt, on screen, almost conversational. I'd watched a dozen of these broadcasts by then and had, I'll admit, begun to find them almost routine, the specific danger of working close enough to something extraordinary that it starts, quietly, to stop registering as extraordinary at all.

She didn't pause to do it. That was the detail that got me, afterward, turning it over in my head for days. She placed the Earth end of the twin directly into the room and stepped through mid-breath, the same sentence carrying across the transition without a single hitch in it, screen to in-person, as though changing rooms across four light years cost her no more attention than changing rooms down a hallway would cost anyone else.

The whole country watched her do it. Live.

"I feel compelled to note," I said, into the stunned quiet that followed, "that the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations has really quite a lot to say about who's allowed to simply walk into a secure federal room, and none of it, as far as I'm aware, anticipated four light years of relevant travel time."

Nobody quite knew what to do with that. I hadn't necessarily expected them to.

A junior staffer near the back of the room, young enough that I don't think she'd fully absorbed yet how normal we were all supposed to be pretending this was, actually applauded, one single startled clap before she caught herself and went scarlet. Nobody told her to stop. Half the room looked like they'd wanted to do the same thing and simply had better professional discipline than she did.

Somewhere aboard Wayfarer, I gathered later, someone kept talking to her for a full three seconds after she'd already left, the live link dying with the door the instant she closed the twin behind her, before anyone up there worked out she simply wasn't in the room anymore rather than experiencing some kind of signal fault.

Down here, she finished the address standing in the room instead of on the screen, and I watched the format quietly escalate itself one small step further without ever technically breaking its own established shape.

Business resumed after, differently. She'd need to open a new twin eventually to get back, treated by everyone who knew her as the most mundane fact in the world. To me it hadn't gotten anywhere near routine yet. I don't think it ever quite did.

McGarrity found me afterward, in the hallway outside the briefing room, working his jaw the specific way he did when he was turning something over before deciding whether to say it out loud.

"You know," he said, "I used to think I understood, intellectually, what it meant to work this close to something unprecedented. I don't think I actually understood it until just now, watching a twenty-three-year-old walk out of a television screen mid-sentence like it was nothing."

"She's not twenty-three anymore, sir."

"No. I suppose none of us are who we were when this program started." He almost smiled. "Draft me a statement anyway, would you. Something dignified. The Vienna Convention line was excellent, but I suspect the actual record's going to need something a bit more official standing next to it."

"Already working on it."

"Of course you are." He shook his head once, more admiration in it than exasperation, and went back into the room to actually run the country, leaving me alone in the hallway with the particular, quiet astonishment of a man who got to witness history from close enough to smell the coffee going cold on the credenza while it happened.

✳

Chapter 61: Joe POV: Reaching Cent once, under terror, half a solar system apart, had taught me the reflex existed. It hadn't taught me a single thing about doing it on purpose, calmly, against a target that wouldn't sit still.

Wayfarer was moving now, cruising at a real fraction of light speed, and my proven range had never been tested against anything but a fixed point. I needed a different kind of practice than fear could give me, and I already knew where to start.

Davy had been there too, that day. My ability had locked onto both of them at once, him and Cent, real, unstaged, under genuine threat. That gave me a partner close enough to actually work with, instead of only having Cent herself, four light-years off, as my one proven target.

"Same thing you already know how to do," Davy said, the first time we tried it properly. "Just without someone trying to kill us this time."

I laughed, mostly because the alternative was not laughing.

The technique wasn't about the reflex. That already worked, proven three times over by then. It was about holding a clear lock on him deliberately, on purpose, calm, the way you'd hold a name in your memory rather than have it shocked into you. Voice. Presence. The specific felt sense of who he actually was, built consciously instead of forced out of me by terror.

We ran it small at first. He'd move somewhere nearby, controlled, safe, and I'd reach for him without any danger attached to the reaching at all, just the discipline of doing it on purpose.

It didn't work the first several times. I kept expecting the fear to be part of the mechanism, and without it, I had nothing to grab onto, my own hands closing around empty air where an anchor should have been.

"You're waiting to be scared," Davy said, after one more failed attempt. "Stop waiting. It doesn't need the fear. It just needs you."

Something in that landed differently than anything else he'd said. I tried again, holding him in my mind the calm way instead of the desperate way, and felt the lock take hold, clean, quiet, nothing like the chaos of that first real time.

I reached, alone, deliberate, and found him where he was standing.

"There you are," he said, when I arrived, grinning at me like I'd just proven something he'd never actually doubted.

The real test came later, once the technique held steady on its own. I had to reach Cent herself, aboard a ship actually cruising now, moving in a way nothing I'd ever locked onto before had moved. No crisis. No terror. Just a deliberate, calm, ordinary visit, using a technique I'd built on purpose instead of one that had once been handed to me by the worst afternoon of my life.

I closed my eyes. I found her voice in my memory, her presence, the specific felt shape of who she was, and reached.

I arrived aboard Wayfarer, calm, upright, entirely undramatic, and found her mid-conversation with Truman, both of them turning at the ordinary sound of someone simply arriving.

"Hey," she said, like it was nothing at all. Which, by then, it almost was.

I stood there a second longer than the moment strictly needed, understanding what I'd just done. Not a rescue. Not an accident. A choice, made calmly, and it had worked exactly the way I'd asked it to.

Cent caught something in my face, the way she always did, and set down whatever she'd been holding. "You did the thing."

"I did the thing."

"On purpose. Not because something was on fire."

"Nothing's on fire. I just wanted to see you." I heard how strange that sounded, said out loud, after three years of only ever arriving in the middle of a catastrophe. "I've been able to do that this whole time, apparently. I just needed to stop waiting for a reason bad enough to earn it."

She crossed the deck and hugged me, plain and unhurried, the kind of hug that isn't checking for injuries first. "Took you long enough."

"I had a lot of terror to unlearn first."

"Fair," she said, into my shoulder. "Welcome to the ordinary version of this. I think you're going to like it a great deal more than the other one."

✳

Chapter 62: Truman POV: It was an ordinary stretch of coast, closer to Centauri than to home by then, nothing marking the day as different from any other day in a long, monotonous transit.

The automatic systems caught it incidentally, the way they caught everything, sensors logging fragments of something at a fixed location that clearly wasn't natural. Geometry too regular. Faint waste heat where nothing should be generating any. Tiny station-keeping corrections, small and precise, the unmistakable signature of something manufactured, ancient, still quietly operating out there in the dark.

We were already past it before any of us understood what we'd seen.

That's not restraint. I want to be clear about that, because I know how it reads afterward, in the calm of a debrief, like a disciplined choice not to react. It wasn't a choice at all. At 0.8c, the ship covers so much ground in the time light takes to carry a detection back to our own instruments that by the time anything resolved clearly enough to recognize, we were already well past its position. We weren't watching something happen. We were reading history that had already finished happening before we ever knew to look.

Cent floated the idea out loud once, half a sentence into it, and her own crewmates shut it down before I had to say a single word.

"Not on our dime," Iverson said, flat, immediate, and that was the end of that conversation before it had properly started.

I never got a moment to order every instrument onto it, the way I might have imagined I would if you'd asked me in advance how I'd handle a discovery like this. There wasn't time for a command like that. We were already gone. What we actually did, once someone realized what had happened, was review whatever the automatic systems had incidentally caught, after the fact, reconstructing an encounter none of us had actually witnessed in real time.

The reconstructed data resolved into something angular, deliberate, a shape that read as built rather than grown, sitting in a fixed position that owed nothing to natural orbital mechanics. It gave nothing else away. No signal. No visible response to our passage. Just geometry, patient and old, where it had apparently always been.

I sat with that fact longer than I let anyone in the room see. Something had built that shape. Something had maintained it, actively, quietly, for however long it had sat there, station-keeping corrections firing on their own schedule regardless of whether anyone was ever going to fly past to notice them. We hadn't found a ruin. We'd found something still working, still tending itself, in a stretch of dark nobody from Earth had ever had reason to look at closely before. Whatever built it hadn't necessarily left. It had simply never needed to announce itself to anyone passing through at 0.8c with barely enough warning to register the fact of it before it was already behind us.

I put a finger on the dot representing it in the reviewed data, later, in the quiet of the review room.

"I'll be back," I said, dry, doing my best impression of a certain iconic delivery and landing, by my own admission, nowhere close to it.

"That," Cent said, from across the room, without missing a beat, "was the most terrible Arnie impression in the history of ever."

The room actually laughed, and I let them, because she wasn't wrong.

✳

Chapter 63: Independent verification took the better part of three days before anyone on Earth heard a single public word about it. Two separate analysis teams, working from the same raw sensor data pulled off Wayfarer's own instruments, reached the same conclusion independently before the President agreed to let the finding leave the building. Quincy ran one of the two teams himself, borrowed again the moment somebody remembered he existed, and by all accounts spent the better part of a day telling a room full of career astronomers they were being too conservative with their own confidence interval. Manufactured. Not natural. Ancient, and, by every measurement available, still faintly, impossibly operating. Quincy's team had reached that conclusion a full six hours before the second one did, and reportedly never once let anyone forget it.

The joint broadcast opened the way these broadcasts always opened by then, a format the public had watched evolve since a single announcement years before had first promised them exactly this kind of transparency. Wayfarer, mission control, and the White House sharing the same real-time feed through a scheduled twin, cameras live in three places at once, verified data confirmed before anyone said a public word about any of it.

"An object was detected," the President said, plain, no embellishment attached, the same register he'd used for every hard truth this program had ever handed the country. "Manufactured. Its approximate location and observed vector have been confirmed independently. Its origin, its occupants, its purpose, and its destination remain unknown."

He let that sit a moment before continuing.

"I want to be clear about what we are and are not telling you tonight. We are telling you what the evidence proves. We are not speculating about what it means, because speculation isn't something this program has ever traded in, and I don't intend to start now, with something this significant."

Truman took the next part, laying out why Wayfarer hadn't diverted to pursue it: no safe destination the sensors could resolve even if the mission's own doctrine had allowed the attempt, no real window inside the physics of the encounter to react at all, a certified flight plan that had never actually faced a genuine decision point to abandon, because the choice had already been made for them by the simple mathematics of traveling at 0.8c.

Somewhere in the background of the feed, off-mic but not quite off-camera, Grebenchekov's voice carried through anyway, light, pitched just loud enough to catch, in a deliberately thickened Russian pronunciation.

"Truman's Schwartzeneger impression, on the other hand, is very much confirmed. And very much terrible."

A ripple of laughter moved through the room, caught live, unedited, humanizing an announcement that could easily have stayed purely technical, purely frightening, purely the kind of news that sends people to their windows at night looking up at a sky that suddenly feels different than it did that morning.

In a converted garage workshop in Ohio, a machinist named Walt Hendricks wrote a single new line in a notebook he'd kept since the program's first announcement, underlined it twice, and sat with it a long while before closing the cover. Manufactured. Not natural. Still operating. He'd catalogued velocity milestones for years without once needing to reach for a word bigger than impressive. He didn't have a word ready for this one yet either. He wrote the facts down instead, the same discipline the broadcast itself had modeled, and decided the word could wait until he'd had time to actually sit with what it meant.

The unanswered question stood there at the end of the broadcast, deliberately, honestly unresolved. Not a mystery this program was built to solve. Just the truth, offered the same way this whole program had always offered it, and left, deliberately, for whoever came looking for the rest of the answer someday.

✳

Chapter 64: Truman POV: I hadn't fired anything on purpose. That's the part that stayed with me longest, afterward, longer than the fear did.

Marcus Webb had come to me two years earlier, a junior flight controller with a sharp eye and a habit of catching things senior people missed, and I'd watched him grow into exactly the kind of deputy a man could actually trust with his own job. I'd handed him my ordinary mission-control duties without a second thought once Reach became my whole life instead of one piece of it, and he'd never once made me regret it. He had a wife back in Houston and a habit of bringing terrible coffee to early briefings because he liked the ritual of making it himself. My person, in every sense that mattered, the way Cent was Davy's person, the way Joe was Cent's.

Cent had opened a scheduled twin, routine, ordinary comms window between Wayfarer and NASA, and I was on the loop the way I usually was, present because I was always present for these. Something went wrong on the ground, mid-session, live, an explosion in an equipment bay at a location Cent had never physically seen, a sudden electrical fault with no warning at all, no time for anyone to weigh a single option before it happened.

Marcus Webb went down in it.

Cent tried. I watched her try, watched the horror cross her face as she understood, in real time, that her own ability had an edge she'd never actually hit before. She'd never physically stood in that room. She had no way to jump there, connected or not, watching or not, and I have never in my life seen someone want to do something as badly as she wanted, in that moment, to break a limitation she'd never once been forced to actually confront.

I didn't decide anything. There wasn't a decision in it at all, same as it never is, the same as it wasn't for Davy or Millie or Cent herself or Joe before me. I reached, unstaged, reflexive, danger real and close enough to touch, and it didn't need a place at all. Only a person.

It worked where hers couldn't.

I came back into that ruined equipment bay with no memory of the transition itself, only the fact of suddenly being there, smoke and alarms and Webb's weight already in my arms before I realized I'd moved at all. I got him clear, got him to the medical team already running toward us, and stood there afterward shaking in a way I hadn't shaken since I was nineteen years old, watching something through a window I was too far away to reach.

Standing there, I knew something in me had just done what it had apparently always been capable of doing, the whole time, waiting for this. I didn't examine it further. I didn't get to. There wasn't an arc here, just a single, real, consequential thing that had happened to me once, the way it had happened to every one of them before me, and I carried it the same quiet way I carried everything else that had ever landed on me too hard to set down again.

Webb kept the terrible coffee habit after he recovered. I noticed that before I noticed almost anything else about the weeks that followed, the stubborn ordinariness of a man returning to his own small daily ritual like nothing about that morning had changed him permanently, even though I could see, watching him, that it obviously had. He never once asked me how I'd gotten there. I think he already understood, the way people in this program had all quietly learned to understand things about each other that nobody ever needed to say out loud.

I didn't tell Cent for three days. Not out of secrecy. Out of needing to be sure, first, privately, what had actually happened to me before I handed her one more thing to carry alongside everything else she was already carrying that year.

"You too," she said, when I finally did tell her, quiet, unsurprised in the specific way that told me she'd already half-suspected it. "Welcome to the club nobody applies for."

"Some club."

"The best one I know," she said, and hearing it, I knew she meant it completely.

✳

Chapter 65: Cent POV: I'd run this profile before too, same as the outbound boost, kick for kick, board for board, except everything about it ran backward now, and backward felt entirely different in my own body than forward ever had.

The braking sequence went long, controlled, the same disciplined rhythm as the launch itself, gamma easing back down toward one as I brought us out of cruise for real, and I let myself think, for the first time in longer than I usually allowed, about everything that stretch of coast had actually held. Years of it. Cent Express runs, twice a week, until the whole routine had worn as smooth and unremarkable as brushing my teeth. Joe learning to reach me on purpose instead of by accident, arriving calm in the middle of an ordinary afternoon like it was nothing at all, when I still remembered what it had cost him to manage it the first time, under fire, not knowing if the distance was even his to cross.

The object. That brief, wordless glimpse of something ancient and patient sitting where it had always sat, gone again before any of us could do more than register it existed. I still thought about it sometimes, in the quiet parts of a shift, wondering what it had been watching for, or whether watching was even the right word for whatever it actually did out there.

Truman's own trigger. I hadn't been there for the worst of it, only for the after, watching him carry something new and unasked-for with the same silent discipline he carried everything else, no fanfare, no exploration, just a fact now true of him that hadn't been true before.

Tara and Jade, on the other end of every ferrying run, a mug already poured before I'd even finished my last text. Reyes and Fraiser and Park, quietly building a medical practice out of first principles because nobody had ever needed one for a mission shaped like this before. I thought about all of it at once, the whole accumulated weight of a coast that had felt endless while I was living inside it and now, from the far side of it, read as almost brief.

I watched the two clocks on the display start closing the gap between them, the divergence that had been quietly widening for years now visibly, actually narrowing in real time as we slowed. Years of accumulated difference didn't vanish. The rate of new divergence collapsed toward nothing right in front of me instead, a felt, visual bookend to everything the coast had opened with, back at the beginning, when Truman first watched that same gap start to grow and didn't reach for his old mantra because there was nothing left in him that needed it.

Board after board confirmed in-system speed. Wayfarer wasn't crossing interstellar distance anymore. It was in the Alpha Centauri system, actually in it, the destination finally something outside the window instead of a number on a flight plan I'd first done the arithmetic for on an ordinary Tuesday, years and years and one very long coast ago.

I looked out through the viewport, past the confirmation displays, past all the careful numbers, and found a star out there that wasn't the sun. Wasn't home. Was, unmistakably, somewhere else entirely, and close now, close enough to actually mean something.

The whole crew felt the shift in tempo with me. Years of routine coast giving way, all at once, to something sharper, more immediate, more alive, real electricity running through a ship's worth of people realizing, together, that the waiting part was finally over.

We were here.

✳

Chapter 66: Rocky POV: My people do not sleep the way yours do. When an Eridian sleeps, the whole body goes still and the mind goes with it, entirely, and nothing short of another hand physically rousing us brings either back before its own time. This is why we watch each other. Not custom first, though it became that too, eventually. Necessity first. A sleeping Eridian who is not watched is simply unprotected, in every sense that word can mean, for as long as the sleep happens to last.

It did not happen all at once, though I did not understand yet that the distinction mattered. One of them went still at the end of a cycle and did not wake at the start of the next. I watched, the way I always watched, and expected what always comes after. It did not come. Some days later, a second one failed to wake the same way. Then a third, spaced unevenly from the second, no pattern I could find at the time, each one simply taking a turn I could not predict and could not stop. I watched every single one of them through to that same ending, because watching is what we do, and I told myself, each time, that the next cycle would break the pattern. It never broke. It only ran out of people to take.

I am alone on this ship because of that: because whatever took them took them wearing sleep's own shape, one at a time, no perceptible cause, no injury, nothing my own instruments were ever able to identify, until only I remained, for a reason I have never been able to determine. That is the whole of it. I do not need a longer story than that, and I have stopped searching for one.

There were twenty-two of them. I do not perceive them the way your kind perceives, through light striking a surface and returning to an eye. I perceive through vibration, through the pattern of the world pressing back against me, and I still, some cycles later, catch myself listening for the specific patterns each of them made moving through this ship, before I remember that those patterns no longer exist anywhere except in whatever I still carry of them internally. I do not have a word in my own language for what your kind calls a ghost. I begin to understand, now, why you might need one.

One of them moved with a particular unevenness, a habit of favoring one side that I never learned the cause of and never asked about, because asking felt, at the time, like a thing there would always be later cycles to do. There were not later cycles. I have thought about that specific unevenness more than I expected to, in the time since, a pattern I will never hear again and cannot fully explain why I miss as precisely as I do. Grief, I have come to understand, attaches itself to exactly the details you never thought mattered enough to properly notice while you still had them.

I came to investigate the reflection. My own network found it through patient, accumulated listening, years of coherent pulses returned from a fixed point where nothing natural should exist. Hearing it resolve clearly for the first time, I knew I was hearing an echo of my own signal, bounced back by something ancient and still, impossibly, awake.

I continue the investigation alone because stopping is not a real option available to me. This is simply what I do now. It is, I have come to understand, easier to continue than to sit still with what continuing is actually replacing.

My instruments found something else entirely, and it was this second thing, not the first, that changed everything. A shape, manufactured, moving with a velocity and a bearing that could only mean intention. Not the old, silent object I had traveled here to study. Something arriving.

Closing the distance was not difficult. My ship's own capability makes that a small thing, hardly a decision at all, more a simple correction of my position relative to theirs. What actually occupied my attention was a different question: what to send them, so that whatever mind received it would understand, immediately, without any shared language between us, why I had come.

I considered several options. A pure mathematical sequence risked reading as sterile, informative but cold, telling them I could count without telling them why I was here at all. A direct approach, unannounced, risked reading as aggression to a species I knew nothing about, and I had no wish to begin this encounter by being mistaken for a threat.

I settled on geometry instead. A representation of the star system itself, rendered in solid form, with a clear mark showing the connection between their position and the object that had drawn us both here. Legible to any mind capable of recognizing a map, since a shape does not require translation to remain a shape, whatever hand or surface eventually found it.

I shaped it from hardened resin, quick, practiced, the material moving liquid under my limbs before it set rigid in exactly the folded, spring-tensioned form I needed: compact enough to survive the crossing, built to release itself the instant something on the far side worked the seal free. A single object, purpose-built in minutes, carrying everything I needed it to say before either of us had a single word to say it with.

I closed the last of the distance, extended the delivery limb along its own track to the nearest point of contact, and released the object into the gap between us, letting momentum carry it the rest of the way neither of us yet had words to manage.

✳

Chapter 67: Grace POV: I wrote a paper once, early in my career, questioning why we assumed all life anywhere had to be built out of carbon. It cost me my research position. It's the reason I ended up teaching middle school biology to a room full of thirteen-year-olds who, whatever else you could say about them, never once let a class go by without a fight over who got to answer first, and it's the exact reason I was standing in the observation bay when something manufactured, undeniably alien, appeared close enough to read.

None of the things that had happened to Rocky's people, whatever those things actually were, had happened to me. There'd been no crisis that put me on this ship specifically to handle this. I was just here, on the roster, for reasons that had nothing to do with first contact at all, and somehow that made this moment feel more mine, not less.

I watched it happen on the external feed before anyone said a word: something on the alien hull unfolded, jointed and improbably long, walking itself along a track worked directly into the plating until it reached the nearest point to us, and simply let go. The object tumbled free, end over end, crossing the gap on nothing but the velocity it had been given and whatever thin margin of trust that release actually represented. Grebenchekov had the retrieval arm out and tracking before I'd even finished processing what I was looking at, EVA lead by training and apparently by instinct too, and he caught it well short of the hull, gentler than the toss that had sent it, and drew it in without once needing anyone to tell him it was his to catch.

It took me longer to get the thing open than it should have, which in hindsight told me something useful before I'd even seen what was inside. I gripped the cap and turned it the ordinary way, righty-tighty, lefty-loosey, muscle memory from every jar and bottle I'd ever opened in my life, and got nothing for my trouble but a faint scrape of resistance. I tried the other direction next, more out of stubbornness than any real theory, and felt it give immediately, smooth and confident, exactly the way a lid is supposed to turn once you've actually got the direction right. There was no earthly reason his species would have settled on the same threading convention mine had, and I made a mental note, working the cap the rest of the way open, never to assume handedness again.

I got the compact little bundle free of the casing and turned it over in my palm, warmer than anything that had just crossed open vacuum had any business being, dense, otherwise giving no outward sign of being more than a solid lump of resin. I filed the warmth away without yet having a reason for it. I was still turning it over when it let go.

The whole interior assembly released at once, jointed segments snapping outward on their own spring tension hard enough that I nearly dropped it, flinching before I'd even registered what was happening, a puzzle box built to answer its own question the exact moment nobody was still bracing for it: a central sphere, larger than the rest, radiating thin connecting rods out to a scatter of smaller spheres locked at fixed points along their own length. A star. Planets, at their proper relative distances, or close enough to matter. Then one more piece, farther out than any of the planets, connected back to the rest by its own rod, shaped nothing like the others at all: irregular, faceted, deliberately not a sphere, the one part of the whole model built to look like it didn't belong among a set of tidy orbiting worlds. Not a planet. Not anything with an orbit. Something else out there, marked as carefully as everything else in the piece, and I understood, once my pulse had come back down to something reasonable, that whatever this species had actually crossed the dark to find, it wasn't us. We were just what they'd happened to run into on the way.

The object came across as a solid shape, and I was the one who worked out what it meant before anyone else in the room had finished processing that it existed at all.

"It's a map," I said. "Look, that's a star system. That's a marked path to a fixed point. He's telling us why he's here."

Osei looked at it. Rahal looked at it. Neither of them argued with me, and within about a minute the whole room had simply, quietly, started treating this as mine to run, not because anyone appointed me, just because I was visibly the only person aboard actually built for exactly this problem.

We started with numbers. Primes first, small integers, the most content-free starting point either species could offer the other, proof of pattern-recognition without any freight of meaning attached yet.

"Primes," Osei said, watching over my shoulder, already ahead of me before I'd said a word about why. "Same reason as every plaque and record we've ever flung into the dark on the chance somebody eventually found one. Math first, because math doesn't care what base you count in or what you sound like saying it. Chuck Berry can wait."

"That's the idea," I said, oddly grateful not to be the one explaining it for once.

"Bet everything I own he has math," she said. "Nothing builds a hull that shape without it."

It went both ways cleanly, faster than either of us had dared hope.

She didn't argue after that, because there was nothing left to argue about, and I noticed, distantly, underneath the actual work, how strange it felt to have someone finish my own reasoning out loud instead of just quietly tolerating it, for the first time in longer than I could easily remember.

I needed something more than counting, though. I needed a unit both of us could verify independently, not just agree on abstractly.

I built a simple, steady, evenly-spaced signal, a fixed interval I could define and hold precisely, and sent it toward Rocky's ship, uncertain whether anything on his end could even perceive it the way I meant it.

He answered in his own register, not sight, not sound the way I understood sound, some vibrational equivalent matched exactly to the interval I'd sent. My own instruments caught it and translated it back to me as a signal that matched, cleanly, unmistakably.

The breakthrough wasn't the exchange itself. It was the moment I realized he'd reproduced my own interval independently, unprompted, on his own initiative, proof that something as basic as duration actually translated between two entirely different kinds of minds.

I sat back from the console, hands shaking slightly, and felt something ease that had been tight in my chest since the day my own paper got quietly buried. Every reviewer who'd ever told me my thinking was too speculative, too willing to entertain a framework other than the one everybody already trusted, was wrong in a way I would never get to tell any of them directly, and I found, sitting there, that I didn't actually need to tell them. The proof was sitting three meters away, on the other side of a hull, answering a signal I'd built out of exactly the kind of thinking that had ended my career the first time.

Osei put a hand on my shoulder, brief, wordless. Rahal was already pulling up the next sequence to try, already treating the whole exchange as the beginning of a real methodology instead of a lucky first contact. Nobody in that room needed me to explain what this moment meant to me personally. I don't think I could have explained it properly even if they'd asked.

We understood each other. A little. It was enough to start.

Chapter 68: Grace POV: Rocky's own request came a day later, relayed through symbols scored into yet another sent object. I couldn't make sense of the shape myself and brought it to Iverson rather than guess. He had it read in about four seconds.

"That's a docking configuration," he said. "His hull and ours, joined by a connecting passage. Wide enough to matter. He wants to build a tunnel."

He needed a sample of my own hull material first, something to calibrate against, and I gave it to him the way I'd have handed over almost anything at that point: without much hesitation, chipping a section free with tools meant for EVA repair and throwing it across the gap myself, trusting a species I'd known for nine days with a literal piece of my own ship.

The tunnel went up within a day of that, his side of it assembled by a many-armed hull robot working faster than anything human-built I'd ever watched operate. The dividing wall at its midpoint arrived first as a whole panel of different material samples, a dozen distinct textures and colors set side by side, and it took me an embarrassingly long moment to understand what I was looking at: an offer. He had no way of knowing what my own atmosphere would do to any given formulation of xenonite, so he'd sent options, and was waiting for me to pick one.

I chose the clear one. Not for any material reason I could have defended to Osei or anyone else, just because every other option meant staring at an opaque wall for however long this relationship was going to last, and I wanted, badly, to actually see him.

He was smaller than the ship carrying him had led me to expect, and entirely unlike anything a human imagination would have generated on its own: five-limbed, carapaced, rock-skinned, patient in a way that read as intelligence the instant I actually saw it moving. We stood on either side of that wall a long moment, not speaking, because neither of us had anything yet to speak with beyond what a handful of cylinders had already managed to say.

I raised a hand, slow, deliberate, the single gesture I trusted enough to risk being misread. He mirrored it back with one of his own claws an instant later, close enough to the same motion that I laughed out loud before I could stop myself. I raised the other hand. He matched that too, then added a third, testing the edges of the game before I'd even finished setting its rules. Four hands up. Five. He ran out of hands to raise a full one ahead of me and simply held the last position, patient, waiting to see what I'd do next.

It wasn't language. It wasn't even really communication, not yet, not in any sense a linguist would have signed off on. But it was the first time either of us had done anything with the other that wasn't purely transactional, and I understood, watching him wait for my next move with what I already wanted to call patience, that whatever came next was going to be built on top of exactly this: two minds, badly mismatched in every way that mattered, finding out they could still play the same game.

Chapter 69: Grace POV: The first sound he ever made in my presence stopped me cold, mid-gesture, hand still raised from whatever round of mimicry we'd been running.

It wasn't a mechanical noise. It wasn't static, or a translated readout, or anything my own instruments were doing on his behalf. It came straight through the divider itself, the xenonite carrying it the way glass carries a knock, several notes at once, layered, chorded, something between a cello section and a whale a very long way underwater.

"Say that again," I said, uselessly, since he had no way yet to know what "say" or "again" meant. He said it again anyway, close enough to identical that I understood, all at once, exactly what I was actually hearing.

"You have a language," I said, and heard my own voice crack on it. "You don't have a mouth. I don't understand how you have a language."

He made the sound a third time, shorter, and paired it this time with a gesture toward the sound itself, toward me, toward the sound again, the unmistakable shape of someone trying to hand something over.

Of course they had language. Nothing builds a spacecraft without one. I'd known that intellectually since the first cylinder came across, but knowing a thing and standing in front of it, listening to a five-limbed, rock-skinned creature sing a chord at me on purpose, expecting to be understood, were not remotely the same experience.

I brought a laptop into the tunnel that same hour, because whatever came next, I wasn't going to let a single note of it go by unrecorded. Rocky went still the moment I started working the keyboard, a different stillness than anything he'd shown me yet, not even during the mimicry game, and I understood, watching him track it, that it was my own hands holding his attention, not the machine itself.

He reached one claw toward it, stopped well short of the divider, and simply held the pose, following my fingers through every strike and the small, rapid clicking that came with each one. I had no words yet to explain what any of it actually did, and no shared vocabulary sturdy enough to build that explanation out of even if I'd tried, so I didn't try. I just kept typing, let him take in the motion and the sound of it for as long as he wanted to, and left the actual meaning of the thing for some later day when we'd both earned the words for it.

Whatever he actually made of it, it held him longer than anything else had, and I filed the reaction away, certain I'd just shown him something that mattered considerably more than a recording device, with no way yet to explain why I thought so.

I set up the crudest recording rig the ship's own equipment could manage while he watched, with what I'd already started reading as curiosity rather than suspicion, and when I finished, he made the sound again, unprompted, patient, like a teacher repeating a lesson for a student who genuinely wanted to learn it and simply hadn't caught it yet.

I hadn't caught it yet. But for the first time since any of this started, I actually believed I was going to.

Chapter 70: Grace POV: Rocky built a small pass-through into the dividing wall before either of us tried exchanging anything else through it, a compact box with a door on each side and its own separate atmosphere cycle, so neither of us ever had to breach the main divider or throw anything across open space again. A small thing, mechanically. It changed everything about how fast we could actually work.

The next object came through it within the hour: a ring of beads strung together, seven of them circling a shared axis, three single beads branching off on short lengths of the same fiber. I turned it over a long moment before the shape actually resolved for me.

"It's an atom," I said. "Or a molecule. The ring's the nucleus, maybe, protons, and each branch is a bonded atom of its own."

Osei leaned in over my shoulder. "Seven and three?"

Seven protons. Nitrogen. Three single-proton branches, hydrogen, bonded to it.

"Ammonia," I said. "He's telling us what he breathes."

I built our own answer the same way, borrowing wire and loose beads from Osei's own kit: two rings of eight beads each, connected by a short bridge between them. Oxygen, paired, the way it actually exists in a breathable atmosphere rather than as some single isolated atom nobody would ever really encounter floating on its own. I sent it back through the same pass-through the first message had come from, and understood, watching it go, that we'd just told each other something more intimate than either of us had probably meant a molecule to be: here is the air my whole existence depends on, here is exactly what would kill you if you ever breathed it, one piece of whatever was going to have to stay between us, alongside the warmth I still hadn't found a reason for and a third piece neither of us had gotten to yet.

He returned mine a while later with a small addition scored into its own surface, a tally I could see was deliberate but couldn't immediately parse. I handed it off to Carter rather than fumble it myself, since pressure conversions were exactly the kind of thing she did faster and more reliably than I ever would, and she had an answer back inside a minute: a count of how many multiples of his own breathing gas would fit inside the volume mine represented. Twenty-nine, translated out. Twenty-nine atmospheres of pressure, on the other side of the wall now actually standing between our two ships.

"Twenty-nine atmospheres," she said again, like repeating it might make it sound less insane the second time. "That's not ocean pressure, Grace, that's most of the way to a submarine's crush depth. Whatever's on the other side of that wall is built not to care about being crushed."

I didn't have the vocabulary yet to ask Rocky what that meant for his own body, existing under that kind of weight every day without apparently thinking twice about it. I filed the number away and kept working, because that was the only way either of us was actually going to learn anything: one small, careful, mutually incomprehensible gift at a time, until enough of them stacked up into something that finally meant something to us both.

Carter POV: Grace mentioned the laptop to me almost as an aside, still turning it over out loud more than actually reporting it: Rocky's own attention, that whole first time, had stayed on his hands and the sound of the keys, never once on the screen those keys were actually driving. It took me a few seconds longer than it should have to understand what he'd just told me. Something capable of solving three-dimensional problems in beadwork faster than either of us could sketch our own model of the same thing could not perceive a single pixel of anything a screen had ever shown him, and it made a strange kind of sense once I actually thought it through. His own homeworld's surface never saw light at all, not once, in the entire history of his species. There had been nothing out there worth evolving an eye to catch.

That wasn't an entertainment problem. That was a bandwidth problem, and bandwidth problems are the ones I actually know how to solve. A groove in vinyl could carry a song into his perception the same way it carried one into a human ear, no contact required, since vinyl exposed to his own atmosphere for even a second wouldn't survive long enough to be read at all. There was no real reason a screen's own light couldn't be carried the same way, converted instead of simply displayed. I started sketching the converter that same night, full specs sent back to a fabrication team on the ground before I'd even finished convincing myself the physics actually closed, because nothing about the actual build, precision optics, a custom-textured readout surface, and a footprint large enough to cover most of a dividing wall, was anything Wayfarer's own shop could turn out from what we had on hand or had room to build at that scale in the first place. I flagged it priority on the way down and meant it, not because anyone's life depended on it but because I wasn't going to sit on an answer this clean a single day longer than the actual fabrication required. Cent jumped the finished unit out herself days later, well before either of us had any real use for it beyond proving it could be built at all, because I have never once in my life managed to leave a solvable problem sitting unsolved just because nobody had actually asked me to solve it yet.

Cent POV: I watched the whole exchange scroll past on a repeated feed from the command deck, and by the time Grace was laying out his plan for the next one, I'd already gone to my own quarters and come back with something of my own.

"Add this," I said, setting a battered paper sleeve on the console in front of him. Real vinyl inside it, scuffed from however many decades of actual use before it ended up packed into my own gear for a mission I'd never once expected to bring it on. My grandmother's, originally. Then my mother's. Then mine, more sentimental cargo than practical, the kind of thing you carry along on a one-way trip because leaving it behind would have felt like leaving behind a piece of the actual reason you were going.

"Here," I said. "Include this."

Grace turned the sleeve over in his hands. "Johnny B. Goode." He almost laughed. "Of course it is."

"My grandmother always said, if you ever get a real chance to send something out into the dark for someone else to find, you include some Chuck Berry."

He was already turning toward the pass-through with it, reaching for the door out of pure habit, before some instinct of mine caught up with the math faster than he had.

"Wait," I said, and put a hand on his arm before he could actually open it. Nobody had measured how hot Rocky's side ran yet. But it ran hot enough already, on everything we did know, that testing exactly how hot against a piece of irreplaceable vinyl felt like a bad way to find out. Neither the sleeve nor the label would fare any better.

"Can't send it through," I told him. "It'd melt before it even cleared the seal."

We brought it to the main divider instead, the same clear xenonite wall Rocky had been talking to us through since the tunnel first went up, and I held the record up to the glass on my own side, grooves out, facing him.

"You can't hold this one," I told him. "Your own air would ruin it in about a second. But you don't actually need to hold it."

He didn't reach for the glass at all. He went still where he already stood, several feet back on his own side, the same way he went still for anything he was actually concentrating on rather than just being polite about, and I remembered, watching him, that reaching for it had been my own instinct, not his. He didn't need contact to read a surface any more than I needed to touch something to see it. His whole species had never once needed to.

"This has structure," he said slowly, still without moving any closer. "Physical. Continuous. A single long groove, cut in one motion, never broken." A pause. "This is not decoration."

"No," I said. "It's sound. The actual shape of it, cut directly into the material. You're not looking at a picture of the song. That groove is the song."

He held there a long moment, reading a groove through a pane of xenonite and several feet of open air the same way my own eyes read it from arm's length, and I understood, watching him do it without so much as leaning in, that I was the one who'd needed the wall explained to me, not him.

"This is the only human technology I have understood immediately, with no explanation needed at all," he said finally. "Everything else you have shown me needed translation first. This did not."

I hadn't expected that reaction, out of everything either of us could have handed him, but it made a strange kind of sense once he said it. Light needed converting before it meant anything to him. A groove didn't, not even through a wall. It was already, natively, exactly his own language.

"Want to hear it the way I do?" I asked, mostly out of habit, before I actually thought through what I was offering him.

"You want. I already have." Not quite a refusal. More a simple correction of my own assumption, delivered without any of the usual games he liked to play when I got something wrong.

He was right, obviously. He'd had the whole thing the moment he read the groove. I played it anyway, off my own laptop, because I still needed the version that came in through my own ears, and he waited through the whole three minutes of it with what I'd already started reading as patience toward a much slower species, still catching up on its own terms with something he'd already finished.

"Sound with structure," he said, once it was over. "Repeated. Deliberate. Not information. Not language. Purpose, question?"

"No purpose," I said. "That's the whole point. We made it because it felt good to make, and it feels good to hear. That's it. That's the entire reason it exists."

He was quiet long enough that I thought the concept might have actually broken something.

"Amaze," he said finally, and asked me to play it again.

Then I played him five more, whatever came up next in the queue, and he asked a real question about every single one of them, and by the end of that hour I understood something about my own species no briefing document had ever quite put into words for me: we make music for no survival-relevant reason at all, and somehow, out of everything I could have shown a person from another star, a groove cut into a piece of plastic was the thing that actually seemed to floor him.

Chapter 71: Grace POV: We needed a shared unit of time before anything else we were building would actually mean anything, and Rocky solved that problem before I'd even finished explaining why we needed it.

He held up a device through the divider, a cylinder with five small windows, each showing a single symbol that clicked over to the next every couple of seconds. I watched it a long while before the pattern actually resolved: the rightmost window cycled through six unique symbols before rolling back to the first, and only then did the window beside it advance one step.

Base six, not base ten. Obvious, once I thought about it. He had three fingers on each of two working hands and apparently didn't count on the other three, which stayed planted for balance. Humans built base ten around ten fingers. He'd built base six around exactly the same kind of anatomical accident, just a different accident.

I timed a full cycle of his fastest-moving symbol against my own stopwatch, then again, then a third time to be sure, and worked out that one of his own seconds ran a little over twice the length of mine. Small thing. Enormous thing. We now had an actual number both of our own instruments could verify independently, not just agree to abstractly, and once we had that, converting anything else, distance, mass, temperature, any measurement either of us cared about, became a problem of arithmetic instead of a problem of trust.

"You are quick," Rocky said, once I'd worked it out loud enough for the translation to catch it. "Humans are unusual. Slow at many things. Quick at this."

"High praise," I said.

"Was not praise. Was observation." A pause, then, in the lower register I'd already started associating with something like humor. "Was also, somewhat, praise."

I laughed, and he threw all five hands up at once, every claw splayed wide, jazz hands, the closest thing he had to laughing right back at me, and somewhere in that exchange, small and technical and almost entirely about arithmetic, I understood we'd crossed some kind of threshold neither the star map nor the atmosphere models nor even the music had quite managed on their own. We weren't just exchanging information anymore. We were starting, however haltingly, to build a friendship.

With actual words finally worth trusting, I brought the laptop back into the tunnel and asked him, at last, the question that had been sitting with me since the first night he went still watching me type. "That's a computer," I said. "It thinks. Not the way I do, not really, but it holds information and does math with it, faster than I ever could on my own."

He was quiet a long moment. "A thinking machine," he said finally, the register dropping into the low, careful tone I'd learned meant he'd stopped performing and started actually processing. "We have no thinking machines. We have never made one. I did not know it was possible to make one."

"You do math faster than I can, though," I said. "In your own head. I've watched you do it."

"Yes. We are good at that. We never needed the machine, so we never built the machine." He reached one claw toward the laptop, stopped short of the divider, and set it back down. "Amaze," he said, quiet, and I understood, watching him, that I'd handed him something considerably bigger than a recording device, and finally had the words to tell him why it mattered.
