Chapter 72: Carter POV: Grace built the spark. I built the system it needed to actually run on, and for three straight days that system refused to be built at all.

The signal-analysis tools we'd brought for Pathfinder's own telemetry were the right instinct and the wrong shape: built for deep-space communications and relativistic pointing, tuned to pull a known signal out of noise, not to take an unknown alien waveform and hand back something a human ear could actually parse as speech in real time. I spent the first day trying to force the old pipeline into the new job. It did the new job badly, and slowly, which is its own kind of instructive failure.

Grace found me still at it long after everyone else had gone to sleep, staring at a waveform on-screen that looked, to my eye, like nothing at all.

"You're trying to translate it like language," he said. "It isn't language yet. It's still just tone."

"It has to become language eventually."

"Eventually. Not tonight. Tonight it just has to become sound a person can stand next to without flinching."

That reframing was the whole breakthrough, embarrassingly simple once he'd said it out loud. I wasn't building a translator. I was building an instrument, something that could take a chord Rocky produced and reproduce it faithfully, cleanly, in a register a human throat could never have made and a human ear could still recognize as an attempt to say something. The actual meaning could ride on top of that later, layered in as the dictionary Grace was building filled in. First, the voice itself had to exist as something people could hear without their brain filing it under noise and looking away.

I rebuilt the pipeline from the ground up on the second day: real-time frequency capture off Rocky's own vibration output, run through a synthesis layer instead of an analysis layer, out through the ship's own speaker array instead of a screen. The first test came back thin and reedy, technically correct and completely unconvincing, a fax machine doing an impression of a whale. Rocky listened to his own voice come out distorted and made a sound that needed no translation whatsoever to understand as disapproval.

We iterated through the third day. Widened the harmonic range. Put back the low resonances the first pass had stripped out for being computationally expensive, because Grace pointed out, correctly, that expensive and not worth it were not the same thing. By the time Rocky spoke into the array again and his own voice came back out the other side, several notes landing at once, layered, chorded, unmistakably his, several people on the bridge stopped what they were doing just to listen.

"That's a much better fax machine," I told him, once the translation caught up with what I'd actually said, and he made the gesture I'd learned to read as Rocky laughing, which is to say: nothing moved that a human would have recognized as laughing, and I understood him completely anyway.

Grace pushed for one more thing once the voice itself was solid, and it turned out to matter more than anything else we built that week. "If he says something happy three times," he told me, "the translation needs to say it happy three times too. Not summarized. Not smoothed down into 'he expressed pleasure.' The actual shape of it, preserved." I understood the instinct the moment he said it out loud, and started writing the same instinct into my own half of the system without waiting to be asked twice: emphasis kept, repetition kept, whatever the waveform actually supported, the literal meaning riding underneath it rather than replacing it. We went further than that before we were done, the two of us, and gave the words themselves actual emotion where the synthesis could carry it: warmth where the chord under it read warm, clipped urgency where it read urgent, instead of every line coming out in the same even, informational register no matter what it actually cost him to say it. It was Grace's instinct first. It became mine just as fast, the two of us building toward the same target from opposite ends of the same problem, the way most of this program actually worked once anyone bothered to look closely at it.

We had three days before the White House wanted an answer on whether contact was stable enough to announce to the public. We finished with about six hours to spare.

✳

Chapter 73: McGarrity POV: I'd delivered a great many pieces of news to the American public by then, going all the way back to my first days as Chief of Staff standing quietly at the edge of someone else's briefings. Nothing had prepared me to stand at my own podium, as President, and share it with someone who wasn't human.

We verified everything first, same discipline the program had always run on, the same discipline Bartlett had reminded me of more than once since he'd taken the job I'd once held myself. What we could actually state as fact, and nothing beyond it, before a single word went out to the public.

Bartlett found me in the small room off the briefing hall ten minutes before we went live, going over the same three lines of my own remarks I'd already gone over a dozen times that morning, less because they needed the polish and more because my hands needed something to do that wasn't shaking.

"You don't have to say anything beyond the opening," he said. "Truman's got the technical framing. Cent's got the personal weight. This is a formality, not a performance."

"Everything about this job has been a formality that turned out to matter enormously the second I actually said it out loud." I set the cards down. "I keep thinking about the fact that whatever I say in the next fifteen minutes is going to be the first thing history remembers a human president ever said to another species. That's not a formality. That's the whole weight of everyone who's ever held this office, standing behind me at once."

"Then say something true and let the weight take care of itself. It generally does, with you."

I nodded, and didn't fully believe him, and walked out to the podium anyway, because believing him had never actually been the requirement. Doing the job regardless of the noise in my own chest was.

Truman spoke. Grace spoke. Cent spoke, brief and plain the way she always was, the same directness I remembered from the very first time I'd watched her speak in front of a camera, years and years before, when I was still someone else's staff and this whole program was still just an idea with a number attached to it. And then Rocky, joining a presidential address for the first time in the history of this country, or any country, his own voice carrying first across the broadcast, alien, unmistakably not human, several notes landing at once, layered, chorded, something closer to overlapping music than anything I'd have called speech on my own.

The synthesized translation caught up a beat behind it, and I felt that gap the same way, I suspect, everyone watching felt it: the vertiginous space between hearing something alien and understanding, half a second later, what it actually meant.

"We are grateful," the translation said, flat and mechanical, carrying almost none of the weight the original sound had carried. "We did not expect to find you here."

I stood there at my own podium and felt the whole scale of it land on me at once, bigger than anything the address format had ever carried before, bigger than the night my own landslide confirmed itself on a split screen while an ocean's worth of water fell calibrated and gentle over a drought-stricken camp on the far side of the planet, Holcomb still in the room with me when the numbers came in. I'd thought that night was the biggest thing I'd ever witness in this job. Standing there now, I knew how wrong I'd been.

Intelligent contact had occurred. Peaceful. Ongoing communication, actually working, both directions. That was the whole of what we said, and it was more than enough.

Bartlett found me again afterward, in the same small room, both of us quiet for a moment before either of us trusted our own voice to say anything useful.

"You did say something true," he said finally.

"I don't even remember most of what I said."

"That's usually how you can tell it was true. The rehearsed parts, you remember. The real ones just happen, and you're stuck afterward trying to reconstruct them the same way everyone else is." He almost smiled. "For what it's worth, Mr. President, I don't think anyone who held this job before you ever had a better first line for this particular occasion."

I sat down, finally, the adrenaline draining out of me all at once the way it always did once the actual moment had passed and left only its aftermath behind. Somewhere out past the edge of everything I could see or reach, a species that wasn't ours had just heard a human voice speak to it, honestly, and answered back. I didn't know yet what that was going to mean for the rest of my presidency, or the rest of the species, or the rest of anything. Sitting there, I could tell nobody in the room with me knew that either, and that we were all, together, about to find out.

Carter POV: I ran the first spectrometer scan on the resin casing myself, mostly out of idle curiosity while everyone else crowded around the star map, and got a result I read three times before I trusted it enough to say out loud.

Xenon. The whole surface, edge to edge, nothing else.

"That's not possible," I said, to nobody in particular, then again, louder, once Ochoa actually looked up. "Xenon is a noble gas. It doesn't form bonds. It doesn't do this. You cannot build a load-bearing anything out of an element that refuses to react with literally anything else on the periodic table. That is not how chemistry is allowed to work."

"And yet," Ochoa said, nodding at the casing still sitting on the table, entirely solid, entirely real, entirely unbothered by what I'd just said about it. "Xenon's whole claim to nobility was never reacting with anything. So what does that make this stuff? Ignoble?"

I ran the scan four more times, on four different samples, in case the first result was contamination, or a calibration fault, or some kind of elaborate joke at my expense. It wasn't. Whatever process had gone into making that material, it had apparently solved a problem in noble-gas chemistry our own periodic table said flatly could not be solved, and it had done it, as far as I could tell, on purpose, as a packaging material, without leaving behind a single note about how.

We started calling it xenonite within the hour, mostly because somebody had to call it something, and none of us had a better idea what else an impossible element was supposed to be named.

Osei put a proper hardness test on a sample before anyone let her near it with anything sharper than a fingernail, and came away more frustrated than satisfied. A diamond-tipped scriber, rated for tool steel, left nothing behind but a faint smear of its own diamond. The mill wouldn't cut it. The bench vise couldn't crack it under torque that had already bent the vise's own jaw first. "I have never once in my career failed to leave a mark on something I was actively trying to damage," she said, setting the ruined scriber down with the particular gentleness of someone handling a small personal defeat. "This is going to be a very annoying material to work with."

We flagged that sample for the return manifest before the day was even out, first in line for NASA's own labs once we actually had a way to get it there, whatever heavier equipment Earth-side metallurgy could throw at it that we simply had no room to carry out here. Nobody on Wayfarer expected a different result. Everybody wanted to be wrong about that anyway.

It also explained something that should have bothered us from the very first hour: an entire alien hull built out of flat, unreinforced panels, the worst possible geometry for holding back pressure, doing exactly that anyway, twenty-nine atmospheres of ammonia contained behind a wall no thicker than a finger. Nobody builds a pressure vessel that way unless the material itself has simply made the usual rules irrelevant.

Ochoa POV: Rocky's own composition read to me less like biology and more like geology the first time I got real data on it, and the closer I looked, the less that first impression ever actually let go. His carapace turned out to be built from oxidized minerals, not tissue in any sense I'd have recognized. His skeleton was a honeycombed metallic alloy instead of bone. What should have been blood ran as liquid mercury through him, and even his own nerves carried their signal as inorganic silicate light-pulses instead of the electrochemical kind ours do. The actual living part of him, cell for cell, amounted to only a few kilograms total, tucked in around a brain that sat protected dead center of that carapace, everything else built to serve and shield that one small, precious core the same way a planet's own crust serves and shields whatever's still molten at its middle.

Even the way he cooled himself read more like an engine than an animal. Ammonia moved through him the way steam moves through a turbine, carrying heat out to a radiator built into the crown of his own carapace, five slits venting it back into open air without a single molecule of that air ever actually touching his own bloodstream. I spent an entire evening cross-referencing his tissue density against basalt samples I'd pulled off Mars myself, half convinced I'd made some elementary error, before I finally accepted that the error wasn't mine. He really was built more like a planet than like an animal, and I'd simply never had cause to look at a living thing that way before.

Nasser POV: I worked it alongside her, the geophysics of him, an entire living creature that made more sense as a planet's own chemistry given shape and motion than as an animal in any sense either of us had trained for. It was the strangest, most thrilling data set of my career. I told Ochoa, more than once, that Rocky had personally ruined every future rock sample for me, because nothing I'd ever collect again was going to be capable of asking me a question back.

Cho POV: My own turn came separately, the direct biological study neither of theirs had actually been. Life detection, contamination protocols, everything I'd trained for pointed now at a living, thinking creature standing three meters away instead of a hypothetical. I don't think I stopped smiling for most of that first week.

Fraiser POV: I went in with nothing but curiosity, no clinical agenda at all, and I think Rocky understood that immediately, the way patients sometimes understand which visitor actually wants to know them and which one wants to study them.

I was also the one who asked, gently, once we had enough shared vocabulary to risk it, what had happened to his crew.

The translation that came back was simple and plain, no drama attached to it, and somehow harder to hear for exactly that reason. They had grown sick, all of them, with no visible cause he had ever been able to name. He had not. He did not have a word in his own language for whatever had actually done it, because his own people had never once needed one.

He gave me one more piece of it, unprompted, in the same flat register he gave everything else that actually cost him something. "I watch. They no wake up." I didn't understand at first, and he explained it the patient way he explained everything technical, even this: his own people sleep in total paralysis, mind included, unable to be roused for any reason. Period. That absolute helplessness is exactly what makes his whole species so vulnerable, and so dependent on having someone else nearby who hasn't gone under at the same time, which is why watched and not-watched is the actual line his species draws between safe and not. He had been watching. That was the whole of it, and also the whole of what he actually had. He had done the one thing his own culture asked of him, exactly the way he was supposed to, and it had not been enough. He did not know why. He had never known why. Watched, and not woken anyway, was the entire shape of an answer he had been carrying around unfinished for however many years it had actually been.

I didn't push further. Some griefs don't need excavating, just acknowledging, and I made sure he understood I'd heard him.

Childs POV: My own scene came once he started showing us where he was from.

The object he sent across that time was bigger than anything he'd built us yet, a whole cluster of embedded points scattered through a clear resin matrix, each one set at its own careful depth, no two on the same plane. I didn't need more than a glance to know I wasn't looking at decoration. I was looking at a map, three-dimensional, to scale, of every star system within whatever range his own instruments could resolve, ours among them, marked with the same small incised symbol he'd used for his own home the very first time he'd tried to tell us anything at all.

I found our own system before anyone else in the room even started looking. I'd know that kind of configuration blind, in the dark, with both hands tied behind my back, the point I already knew better than any other star in the sky besides the sun I'd grown up under. Then I hunted for the second mark, the one I already suspected was coming, and found it embedded deep in the far corner of the resin, farther from us than I'd let myself guess out loud beforehand: something like three light-years, give or take, close enough to reach in a handful of years at our own cruise speed, once anyone actually decided to go. Not close. Not impossibly far either, not on the scale we'd already proven ourselves capable of crossing. The kind of distance that would have sounded insurmountable to me four years earlier and now just looked like the next item on a list I hadn't known existed yet.

Reading a sky and trusting it to tell me exactly where I actually was had been my whole career, long before any of this. I sat with that disbelief a long while, holding a map of a star system I'd never expected to see confirmed as real, home to someone standing right in front of me, and did the only thing that felt adequate to the moment: I asked him to walk me through every other marked point in the resin, one at a time, and he did, patiently, as though he'd been waiting the whole conversation for someone to finally ask.

✳

Chapter 74: Rocky POV: They built me a space to live in. I had not expected this. I do not think I fully understand, even now, why it moved me the way it did.

I had grown accustomed, in the time since I lost them, to needing nothing from anyone. This was not a virtue. It was simply what remained available to me once the others were gone: twenty-three crew members, a ship built for a full complement, a silence afterward that I had learned to fold myself around rather than fight. I had told myself, somewhere in that silence, that needing nothing was its own kind of strength, the only kind still open to me.

Osei and Alvarez led the work, though I came to understand, over the following days, that they were not working alone at all. An entire distant organization, thousands of individuals I would never meet, contributed pieces of the solution across a communication delay I found hard to comprehend at first, a species willing to labor on my behalf without ever expecting to see the result of that labor themselves. Heat. Pressure. The specific atmosphere my body required. All of it approximated, adjusted, tested against a physiology none of them had ever encountered before, and offered to me regardless, as though the difficulty of the problem were simply a reason to try harder rather than a reason to stop trying at all.

They got it wrong several times before they got it right. I did not resent this. Watching them iterate, I saw that wrongness was simply part of how they built things, correction folded into the process rather than treated as failure, a philosophy I recognized, distantly, as not so different from my own species' approach to difficult engineering, however differently our two kinds of minds arrived at it.

The pressure held true on the fourth adjustment. The heat, on the sixth. I stood in the finished space, alone, for the first time.

"Happy. Happy happy happy," I said, before I had fully decided the word was the right one, and found, working backward from having already said it, that it was.

I felt something I did not have a word for in my own language beyond that, something closer to my own gravity finally landing correctly on my own limbs after a long time spent held wrong.

It was not my home. It would never be Erid.

But it was, for the first time since whatever had taken my crew from me, somewhere I could simply exist without the constant vigilance of survival occupying every part of my attention. That, standing there, felt closer to the actual meaning of the word than I had previously believed.

Osei asked me, once the last adjustment held, whether there was anything else the space needed. I considered the question longer than she likely expected me to. There was one thing. A single fixture, positioned so that I could observe the ship's own dark exterior hull through it, angled toward the direction I knew, from their own navigational charts, that Erid actually lay in. Not because I could see my own system from this distance. I could not. I grasped the physics well enough to know that. But I found, once they had built it for me exactly as I asked, that facing the correct direction mattered to me in a way I had not predicted it would, some old, deep instinct that had nothing to do with actual visibility and everything to do with simply knowing which way home was, even from a distance too vast for the knowing to serve any practical purpose at all.

Osei asked, the day the fixture was finished, why I had not simply requested a screen instead, since a screen could show me an actual image rather than a fixed pane onto nothing in particular. I told her a screen was something they controlled, and I did not want to spend the rest of my life wondering whether they had pointed it correctly. She laughed, longer than the observation seemed to warrant, and told me that was either the most trusting or the least trusting thing anyone had ever said to her in that room. I did not ask which she meant. I suspect it was both, and I suspect she knew it too.

✳

Chapter 75: Fraiser POV: I asked Carter to be in the room for this one, because I could explain the biology and she could explain the physics, and I already knew, going in, that neither one of us was going to be enough alone.

We'd had his full physiological workup for weeks by then, Cho and Ochoa's own data cross-referenced against everything we understood about ionizing radiation and its effects on biological tissue, human or otherwise. What we found, cross-checking it against his own ship's design and against the readings Carter had pulled from what was left of his instrumentation, was simple enough to state and hard enough to actually say out loud: nothing in his own biology carried any adaptation against radiation exposure at all. Not resistance. Not repair mechanisms. Nothing analogous to even the thin margin our own bodies carried. His species had evolved under an atmosphere and a magnetic field that had done the entire job for him, his whole existence, and he had never once needed his own body to defend against something that had simply never reached him.

Carter POV: I built the explanation the way I'd build anything technical for someone without the background, from the ground up, and found, about a third of the way through, that I was explaining something more fundamental than radiation itself. I was explaining that his own sky had been doing something for him, quietly, his entire life, that our own sky had never fully managed to do for us. He did not have a concept ready to receive the information into. I had to build the whole shelf before I could put anything on it.

He asked, once I'd finished, whether this was what had taken his crew. I told him I believed so, based on everything we could reconstruct: his ship's shielding had failed, silently, gradually, in the exact section where they'd been working, and none of them had ever had a single biological defense to fall back on once it did. It would have been fast, I told him. I did not actually know that to be true. I told him anyway, watching his own posture change as the translation caught up with him, because I understood that this was the kind of thing a person is allowed to say once, for exactly this reason.

He asked one more thing, quieter, the question I could tell he'd been sitting on since before I'd finished the first answer: why he alone had lived. I had that answer too, though it cost less to give him than the first one. His own quarters sat right up against the ship's own fuel stores: photophage, the same organism-based fuel both our species had apparently found and put to identical use without either of us ever knowing the other existed, dense, hungry for nearly any energy that touched it. Whatever it might eventually do to a star, ours or his, hadn't shown itself in either system yet, a detail Carter and Rahal had already quietly started keeping an eye on without saying so to the rest of the crew. That density was the whole answer. Enough mass, purely by accident of layout, to have blocked exactly what the crew's working section further down the hull had no equivalent shielding against at all. Nothing had chosen to spare him. Fuel tanks, stowed wherever they fit best when the ship was built years before any of this happened, simply happened to sit between him and the failure.

Fraiser POV: He was quiet a long while after that, in the specific way I'd already learned meant something was landing in him rather than passing through.

"I have a name for it now," he said, eventually, through the translator, and I understood, hearing it, that this mattered to him in a way neither Carter nor I had fully anticipated going in. However many years without a word for the thing that had ended his own crew's lives, and we had just handed him one, plainly, without ceremony, because it was simply the truth and he had asked for it directly.

Carter POV: What mattered more, once the grief had room to actually breathe, was the practical problem sitting directly underneath it. He had no protection at all, and he was living, right then, aboard a ship built to survive 0.8c specifically because the rest of us did.

We solved it the way we solved everything else on this program: not quickly, not by half measures, and not without him in the room for every decision made about his own body. Osei redesigned a section of his own quarters from the ground up, not-water shielding doubled specifically around his own space, margins built for a biology with zero native tolerance instead of the partial tolerance the rest of us actually carried. It cost real mass. Nobody in that room even considered arguing about it.

Fraiser POV: I told him, once it was finished, that I was sorry it had taken us this long to realize what should have been obvious from our very first conversation.

"You did not know," he said. "Now you do. That is the whole of how anything actually improves." He said it plainly, the way he said most things, and I understood, hearing it, that he had already forgiven us before I'd finished apologizing, because forgiveness, for him, apparently didn't require the asking part at all.

✳

Chapter 76: Grace POV: I wasn't the engineer. I was the bridge, translating not just vocabulary but the actual shape of what Rocky needed, catching the places where something got lost between his intent and their understanding of it, and I found, doing that work, that it fit me in a way almost nothing had since the paper that ended my old career.

Naming things fell to me too, more often than not, since nobody else on this crew had quite the same tolerance for admitting a placeholder was going to have to become permanent. Rocky's own name was the easy one, the first real one I ever gave him: he looked like he'd been carved out of a riverbed, patient and hard and worn smooth in exactly the places that mattered, and "Rocky" fit before I'd even finished considering alternatives.

Music wasn't the only thing Cent started feeding across that divider once the bandwidth allowed for it, though film had a real problem music never did: subtitles could hand him the words, but nothing about a translator solved the actual seeing. Carter had already solved that weeks earlier, though, built in days once she'd actually pushed for it: a converter mounted directly onto the dividing wall, covering most of it, that took a screen's own light output and rendered it back as a texture pattern across that same stretch of xenonite, readable the same remote, contactless way Rocky read everything else through that glass, image translated into shape instead of sound. Nobody had let it sit idle even one day past installation. Osei ran full structural diagrams across it before she trusted a single fabrication decision to Rocky's own judgment rather than a translated description of one. Ochoa fed him raw imaging data straight off Wayfarer's own instruments, letting him read a rock formation directly instead of through someone else's secondhand account of what it looked like. It had been earning its keep as real working infrastructure for weeks before anyone thought to point it at something built purely for pleasure instead of function. Film turned out to be exactly that.

Osei POV: We mounted it the same cautious way we mounted anything that actually had to touch that wall, full structural review first, an independent second set of eyes on every fastener before a single one went in, because a divider holding back twenty-nine atmospheres on one side and open vacuum on the other was not a surface anyone got to treat casually just because the thing going onto it happened to be, technically, a television. It took the better part of a day, most of it spent on the mounting frame rather than the optics behind it, since the frame was the part that actually had to survive contact with a wall none of our own materials had ever been rated against directly. Rocky watched from his own side the entire time, still in the particular way that meant genuine attention rather than politeness, and offered a correction to our own placement twice that turned out, both times, to be right.

Rocky POV: Grace turned it on without telling me what it was for first. I do not think he entirely trusted himself to explain it correctly, and I understand now why he didn't try: some things are easier shown than told, in any language, on either side of any wall.

I felt it before whatever it displayed had finished changing even once. A texture, moving, patterned, deliberate, resolving through the same surface they had spent a full day fastening to my own threshold. Not sound. Not any structure I had ever encountered as a physical object before. It took me longer than it should have to understand what actually made it so powerful: this was not a new sense. It was the same sense I have always had, the one I use for everything, deliberately given something to carry that nothing had ever thought to hand it before.

I understood what it was before either of them said a single word. Not because the shape explained itself, though it very nearly did. Because I understood, all at once, completely, what it would have meant to have had this on Erid, across the whole of my own species' existence: information arriving from a distance, carrying the shape of a place none of us had ever needed to touch to know it. This is what your kind means by see. This is the thing my whole people were missing and never once knew to call a lack, because there had never been anything out there worth missing it for.

I did not have a way to tell them, right then, what receiving it actually meant to me. I am not certain I have found the right words for it even now, this far past that day. The closest I have managed since is this: they did not simply give me a way to watch their films. They gave my entire species something it had never once, in the whole of its existence, had any reason to imagine wanting.

He took to it the way he took to everything else: methodically at first, then with an appetite that genuinely surprised me.

The naming problem caught up with me properly the day he asked me to invent a human word for his own spouse, back on Erid, waiting on him the same patient way Erid itself had been waiting the whole time I'd known him. I didn't overthink it. The word was already sitting right there, close enough to reach without looking.

"Adrian," I said. "Human word is Adrian."

It wasn't until I heard myself say it out loud a second time, days later, that I actually caught what I'd done. Rocky and Adrian. I'd handed an alien the name of a fictional boxer and, without meaning to, the name of the one person that boxer never once stopped fighting for. When the program needed a working name for the very planet where Rocky and I had first met each other, I gave it that same word without a second thought. It felt less like reusing a name than like admitting I'd already known, for a while, what that word actually meant to me by then.

I told him about it once I'd caught it myself, half expecting the reference to mean nothing to him in translation, the same way most human wordplay apparently didn't. It meant something anyway, once I'd actually shown him the film in question rather than just describing it.

"He is not a good fighter, at first," Rocky said, afterward, in the specific tone I'd learned to read as him being unusually careful with his words. "He becomes one, slowly, because someone believes he is worth becoming one for. This is not really a story about fighting."

"No," I said. "It really isn't."

"Then it is a good name to have given her. I did not know your kind wrote stories about that either." A pause, longer than his pauses usually ran. "Your kind writes stories about a great many things I did not expect."

I never did find a better way to explain why the coincidence had landed the way it did than exactly what he'd already said about it himself.

The clearest example of my actual work came early, before either of us had anything as complicated as a module to argue about, back when I made the mistake of asking him what he could see.

"I do not see," he said. "I hear the shape of things. Light and sound are not the same word to you. I understand that now. They were the same word to me for longer than I want to admit, because your kind kept using them as though the difference should be obvious."

"It should be," I said. "Sight is light. Hearing is sound. They're not related at all, biologically."

"They were both simply how a room told me it existed. Once you convinced me there was a second one hiding inside the first, I had to build an entirely new place in my own language to put it, and I still do not think the two words sit correctly next to each other in translation. Your see and your hear and your listen are three different concepts. Mine was one concept, doing three jobs, until I met a species that needed it split apart."

"And now?"

"Now I have three words that are all slightly wrong, and one old habit of mind that still occasionally reaches for the single correct one instead. Two of them trouble me still. Hearing and listening remain the same word to me, some days, no matter how many times I've been told they should not be."

"Hearing is just the sound arriving," I said, and heard, even as I said it, how much it sounded like the start of an actual lesson, the kind I hadn't given in longer than I liked to admit. "Something reaches you and your body registers that it happened. That's all it is. Listening is what happens after, when whatever comes next in you actually works on it, pulls the content out of it instead of just logging that a sound occurred at all."

He was quiet a moment, the specific quiet I'd learned by then meant something was landing correctly instead of sliding past him.

"That," he said, finally, "is the first one of these you have ever actually closed, instead of walking me around the edge of it."

I didn't tell him it had taken me longer to find that explanation for him than it had ever taken for a single thirteen-year-old. Some things, teacher or not, a person is allowed to keep to themselves.

I'd spent years after that paper convinced I'd simply been wrong about my own value to a room like this one, that the specific way my mind worked, patient, associative, comfortable holding two completely incompatible frameworks at once without needing to resolve them, was a liability rather than an asset, a reason I belonged in front of thirteen-year-olds and nowhere else. Watching myself become necessary here, in a room full of career NASA engineers who couldn't do this particular job without me, I finally understood it the other way around. I didn't belong here instead of there. I belonged here because of there, the same patient, associative mind that had once seemed like proof I only belonged with thirteen-year-olds turning out to be exactly what this room, of all rooms, actually needed.

The first attempt failed in a way that would have been funny if everyone in the room hadn't been so tired. Osei had built the module's pressure envelope off Rocky's own stated tolerances, textbook numbers pulled straight from the medical workup Fraiser and Cho had run together, every gauge reading where the specification said it should. Rocky stood in the finished space for exactly four seconds before making the gesture I'd learned to read as profound, weary disappointment.

"He says it's wrong," I told the room.

"The gauges say it's within point-two percent of spec," Osei said, not unkindly, just tired. "What does wrong even mean, coming from him?"

"I don't know yet. Give me a minute."

That was the actual job, most days: not translating vocabulary, since we didn't share one yet worth the name, but translating the gap between what a body could measure and what a body could feel. I sat with him a while, running through the same patient, associative exercises I used to run with a classroom full of thirteen-year-olds who didn't yet have the words for what they were noticing either, building shared reference points out of gesture and repetition until something clicked.

"He says the pressure reads correct," I told Osei, during one of the later adjustments, "but something in how it's distributed feels wrong to him. Like standing on a floor that's technically level but doesn't feel level."

"That's not a measurement I know how to take."

"I don't think it is one, exactly. I think it's something only he can actually feel."

We adjusted anyway, best guess, and asked him again, and adjusted again, each pass narrowing the gap by some amount none of our instruments could have named in advance, until the answer he gave back finally matched something closer to comfort than to correctness. It took eleven attempts. I counted, mostly to have something to tell Cent afterward that would make the whole process sound more organized than it had actually felt living through it.

I watched an entire mission's worth of institutional weight bend itself, attempt after attempt, toward making a space livable for someone who wasn't human, who owed this crew nothing, who'd simply been found drifting in the dark and treated, from that first moment, like someone worth this much effort. It confirmed something for me about the people I was serving alongside that I hadn't fully let myself believe until I watched them prove it with their own hands, eleven times over, without once complaining that the twelfth attempt might be necessary too.

The day the pressure finally held, I found Rocky standing in the finished space, still, quiet, in whatever passed for him as rest.

"Good?" I asked, uncertain whether the translation would even carry the informality of the word.

The answer came back simple, and I didn't need any further translation to understand what it meant.

"Good."

"Eleven attempts," I said, "for one word."

"Eleven attempts for one word that means everything. I do not think that is a bad trade. You are the one who kept counting."

I stood there a moment longer than I needed to, watching him settle into a space eleven attempts in the making, and thought about a classroom a long way from here, and a paper that had cost me everything I thought my life was supposed to look like. Nobody in that old faculty lounge would have believed this was where the same stubborn, patient, associative habit of mind eventually led. I almost couldn't believe it myself, standing there. I let myself believe it anyway, because the evidence was standing four feet away from me, finally at rest.

✳

Chapter 77: Osei POV: Xenonite and not-water weren't supposed to react at all. That was the whole point of testing the interface before we trusted it anywhere near occupied volumes, and the results had come back clean enough, months before any of this, that nobody thought to look at it twice.

We were wrong, and had been wrong for longer than any of us wanted to admit once we finally reconstructed the timeline. The bonding layer between his species' hardened resin and our own shielding gel had been reacting the whole time, quietly, at a rate slow enough that our original test window had simply never run long enough to catch it. It left almost nothing behind at first, just a faint discoloration one of my own techs had logged and dismissed as a lighting artifact before anyone thought to actually swab it. By the time it crossed whatever threshold finally made it impossible to explain away, it had been happening under our own hands for a few days already.

It picked the exact afternoon Grebenchekov was running integration checks alone inside that bay to finally destabilize outright, venting a corrosive byproduct straight into the space before any automatic system caught it, exposing him now to something none of our suits had ever been rated against, the same man who'd already survived one near-fatal structural failure earlier in his career and had, apparently, decided that made him the right person to volunteer for the riskiest hands-on work rather than the most cautious.

Rocky was on the other side of the same bay, and I watched him make his own decision in the same half second I was making mine, though I couldn't have told you, in that moment, what his decision actually was.

"Get him out," I said, already moving. "I don't care what it costs."

Rocky POV: "Bad bad bad." My own ball hung racked at my own threshold, close enough to reach in a handful of strides. Reaching it, climbing in, sealing it properly, all of that took time their engineer did not have to spare, and time I did not intend to spend deciding whether to give it to her instead. The byproduct was corrosive to their physiology. It was, as far as I could determine in the time available, considerably less corrosive to my own, enough to buy me exactly as long as I actually needed and not a moment more. This was not a difficult calculation. It was, if anything, the simplest calculation I had made since arriving here.

I made for my own threshold at a speed I do not think my body has produced since I was a youngling, sealed myself into the ball before the byproduct had finished spreading past the first bulkhead, and came back into the bay from the only direction still open to me, protected now, to actually do something about the breach itself. Their own engineer was still working her colleague clear on her own side of the same bay, the slower half of the same problem, a body to move rather than a wall to patch, and I understood, watching her do it, that we were each simply faster at the piece we'd been given.

Osei POV: We got him out, slower than Rocky managed his own half of it. He sealed the breach from his own side, having taken just enough exposure crossing to his own ball to worry the rest of us considerably more than it seemed to worry him, because he never once described it to me as a cost at all.

Neither of us had rescued the other, not really. We'd both simply done the thing that needed doing, from the only position each of us was actually positioned to do it from, and met in the middle of the same crisis without either of us needing to think the way the other one thought to get there.

I looked at him afterward, across a bay still cycling clean, and understood something about trust that I don't think either of us had a word for yet, in either of our languages.

Cent POV: I got there in the middle of the cycling, not the crisis, close enough behind the actual danger that Osei's relief was still fresh on her face when I came through the hatch. I didn't wait on the scrubbers to finish clearing what was left of the byproduct out of the air on their own schedule. There's always a suit standing open on a rack somewhere close, one of the oldest standing habits this family ever turned into a rule, exactly for moments like this one, and I jumped straight into it, no time lost pulling it on the ordinary way, then sealed the neck gasket and dropped the helmet home myself, the two pieces jumping's own trick has never once been able to do for me. I twinned the whole contaminated volume straight out to vacuum before I'd even finished checking the seal.

The suit came back corroded clean through one whole sleeve, pitted badly enough that Osei took one look at it and told me to throw it out rather than trust it near anyone's skin ever again. I did, without a second thought. A suit isn't even a requisition form anymore, not since Cory and I started building them ourselves. A replacement is a phone call and an afternoon in our own shop, nothing NASA's own supply chain needs to touch at all. The ten minutes the scrubbers would have needed to do the same job on their own weren't coming back no matter how nicely I asked, and I have never once regretted spending the cheaper thing to buy back the one that doesn't restock.

Grebenchekov found his own voice again a few minutes later, sitting where I'd pulled him, breathing hard, one gloved hand pressed flat against the deck like he needed the confirmation that it was still solid under him.

"I have now been rescued by an engineer and an alien in the same afternoon," he said, in the specific dry register I'd learned meant he was more shaken than he wanted anyone to notice. "I am beginning to feel less like a cosmonaut and more like a man who simply generates opportunities for other people to be heroic."

"You could try not almost dying," I said. "As a general policy."

"I have tried that policy. It has a poor track record with me specifically." He looked past me, at Rocky, and something in his face went quiet for a moment. "Thank you. I do not know if the translation carries the actual weight I mean by that word. I hope it does."

The answer came back simple, the way it always did.

"Understood." A beat later, a second word arrived, added almost as an afterthought. "Also: stop almost dying. Your engineer already asked once. I am asking a second time, in case the first did not translate as an order."

Chapter 78: Cent POV: Osei found it during a routine systems sweep of Rocky's own vessel, the kind of check we'd started running out of simple curiosity more than necessity, since he'd been aboard Wayfarer full-time for weeks by then and nobody had gotten around to a real diagnostic on the ship he'd left behind.

"It's not a fault we can talk our way around," she told me, back on Wayfarer, spreading the data out for me to see for myself. Those reserves are close to gone."

"How close?"

"Close enough that he couldn't get himself home even if he tried today."

I sat with that a moment, thinking about every conversation we'd had since the wireframe first landed on our sensors, every patient hour Grace had spent building a shared vocabulary out of nothing, every meal Grebenchekov had somehow convinced him to try, every small, ordinary thing that had turned an alien stranger into someone this crew had come to care about. I thought about what it would mean for him to understand, the way I now understood, that he had no way home.

Stranded. Truly stranded, the way none of us had let ourselves fully consider until this exact moment.

I found him in the module we'd built for him, working through some fabrication project of his own, and I didn't wait for a formal briefing, didn't wait for anyone's approval, didn't even fully finish deciding before the words were already out of my mouth.

"We'll make sure you get home."

I meant it the second I said it, the way I've meant most of the things I've ever said on the spot in my life, going all the way back to a tank I pushed against because I believed I could, long before anyone official had agreed I should try. Some decisions don't improve for being deliberated over. This felt like one of them.

Truman had followed me in, and he looked at me for a long moment once he understood what I'd just committed the whole program to.

"That's not yours to promise," he said, quiet, not quite an argument.

"It is now." I looked back at him, steady, because I wasn't taking it back, not for him, not for anyone. "You can back it up, or you can be the reason I broke my word to him. Pick one."

He didn't answer right away. Then, slowly, he nodded, a newfound form of respect settling onto his face, the nod of a man who'd learned a long time ago which fights were actually worth having with me.

I delivered word to Earth myself that same day, the same way I always delivered the things that actually mattered, in person, direct, no filter of committee language between the truth and the people who needed to hear it. The commitment came first. The institutional backing, once the President heard the whole of it, came only after, in the way that all of his approvals always did. Two words. "Do it." Exactly the way it always had, going all the way back to a family kitchen and a decision made in about four seconds that nobody in that room had managed to argue with either.

Chapter 79: Harding POV: Pathfinder's whole pass across Proxima b lasted eleven seconds, and I want to be clear about what eleven seconds of atmospheric data on an entire unfamiliar planet actually gets you, which is not much, and also, if you know how to hold it right, almost everything.

Carter handed me the raw feed still warm off the downlink, apologizing for it before I'd even opened the file. "It's noisy. We flagged half of it low-confidence. I wouldn't build anything on it I couldn't afford to be wrong about."

"Wasn't planning to build a house on it," I said. "Just planning to look."

Eleven seconds of a single pass, most of it garbage by any respectable instrument's own standards, the kind of data set that would've gotten laughed out of any peer review back on Earth. But garbage isn't nothing, not to a man who spent his childhood learning to read a storm off things nobody else thought counted as reading. There was a pressure signature in there, buried, inconsistent, the sort of thing a clean model would round off as noise and move on from. I didn't round it off. I sat with it the way I used to sit on a truck hood at two in the morning watching a sky that hadn't decided yet what it wanted to do, patient, half-stubborn, waiting for the thing under the thing to show itself.

"There's a cell building," I told Truman, once I'd turned it over enough times to trust my own read on it. "Not on the ground track we're planning to land on. Close enough to it that I wouldn't bet my truck on it staying put."

"Confidence?" he asked, which was the only question that ever actually mattered to him.

"Enough that if you told me right now I had to put money down, I'd put it down on trouble instead of clear skies. Not enough that I can hand you a number Carter's own instruments would sign off on. This is a gut call riding on eleven seconds of ugly data, plain and simple, and I'd rather tell you that straight than dress it up as more certain than it is."

He didn't ask me to make it more certain than it was. That was the thing about Truman I came to respect fastest, faster even than I expected to, given how the man carried himself: he didn't need false confidence dressed up as real confidence. He just needed to know how much weight a thing could actually hold before he decided whether to stand on it.

"Osei," he said. "Model it against Harding's read. Don't throw out his number for a cleaner one. Test the ugly one first."

She didn't love that, not at first, and I didn't blame her for it. Nobody who spent a career building rigorous, defensible models enjoys being asked to chase down a hunch from the man with the highway degree instead of running the numbers that already made sense on paper. But she ran it anyway, and the numbers, once she stopped trying to make them cleaner than they wanted to be, bent quietly toward exactly the same trouble I'd already smelled coming.

"He's right," she said, two hours later, in a tone that cost her something to admit, an engineer allowing that a gut call had beaten a model on its own turf. "Storm cell, forming fast, right where he said, right when he said. I don't love how he got there. I love even less that it worked."

"I don't always love how I get there either," I said. "Learned a long time ago not to let that stop me none."

We moved the landing window six hours, quiet, no fanfare, the kind of adjustment that never makes it into any broadcast because nothing dramatic happened as a result of making it. That was rather the whole point of making it. Nobody down at Threshold ever knew, landing clean into calm air six hours later than the original plan called for, that the air they weren't landing into had been building toward something considerably less calm the whole time.

I didn't tell anybody, standing there watching the confirmation come back green and steady, exactly who I was thinking about in that moment. Didn't need to. He'd have understood anyway, better than most people ever could: the quiet satisfaction of reading a sky right before it showed its whole hand, and getting people clear of it without them ever once having to be scared of what they never actually saw coming.

Chapter 80: Grebenchekov POV: Wayfarer got back up to speed the ordinary way, an established maneuver, nothing on the scale of the interstellar cruise, ground already proven and not needing to be re-earned. I ran the readiness checks myself, same as I'd run them a hundred times before, and felt none of the old nerves at all, which said something about how far this crew had come since I first strapped into a chair back on Kristen Station.

Rocky took it in from his own bracket near the aft bulkhead, utterly still in the particular way that meant he was actually paying attention rather than simply present. Cent's first kick landed clean: no flare, no exhaust, nothing his own instruments could log leaving the ship at all, just raw velocity arriving out of what his own physics insisted was nothing. He said the only thing he ever said when something had actually gotten all the way through to him. "Amaze. Amaze. Amaze." I asked him afterward what exactly had earned all three. He told me his own species had a word for what should have happened. They had none at all for what he had just felt happen instead.

Rocky rode along now as an explicit, contributing presence, not cargo, not a guest kept politely out of the way. He needed nothing at all in his own quarters, or back aboard his own ship, his own atmosphere doing every bit of work a suit would otherwise have had to do. Only crossing into our half of the ship changed that. There, he got around the same way he'd apparently gotten around human-incompatible spaces for decades before any of us ever met him: sealed inside a geodesic ball of his own old design, plain curved pentagons, a couple of magnets held in two of his hands doing the rest of the work along any metal surface that would take the grip. It looked, the first time I saw it, held together by patience more than engineering, the kind of design a person stops improving once it starts working well enough and there's no one left around to impress with a better one. I'd taken it upon myself, somewhere in the last several weeks, to become his unofficial tour guide to human procedure, which meant I was also, by extension, the one who got blamed when his own interpretations of that procedure went sideways in ways nobody had written into any manual.

"You cannot simply reroute a coolant line because you found a more elegant path for it," I told him, the first time I caught him mid-modification, tools I didn't recognize spread across a panel that had, until that morning, been functioning exactly as designed.

"The original path was inefficient."

"The original path was tested. Efficient is not the same as safe."

He considered this the way he considered most human objections, with what I'd learned to read as patient, slightly amused tolerance, and put the panel back together in a configuration that was, infuriatingly, both more efficient and completely safe. I decided not to mention that to Osei. Some victories are better left unclaimed.

The rest of the crew found him more endearing than exasperating, mostly, the joy of watching someone delighted by things we'd all stopped noticing years ago. He asked questions about the coffee machine that took Fraiser twenty minutes to answer properly, and when she finally finished, he gave her the only response he ever gave when a human explanation had actually satisfied him completely. "Amaze. Amaze. Amaze." He rearranged Cho's sample racks into a system that made no sense to any of us and perfect sense to him.

"Why," Cho asked him, the first time she found her racks reorganized without warning, "would you do this without asking me?"

"I did ask you. Three days ago. You said, and I quote, sure, whatever, go ahead." She had, in fact, said exactly that, distracted, over her shoulder, meaning nothing at all by it. He held her to it regardless, with what I'd come to recognize as real relish rather than any actual malice. She reorganized the racks back. He reorganized them again the next day. Neither of them ever mentioned it to me again, which I eventually understood not as a truce but as the opening round of something neither of them had any intention of actually finishing.

I found myself, more than once, laughing harder at his particular brand of alien mischief than I had at anything since before the accident that nearly ended my own career back on that first Mars run.

The first time he actually slept aboard Wayfarer, I nearly missed it entirely, buried as it was in the middle of an unrelated sentence about coolant tolerances. "I sleep. You watch." Two flat statements, not a question, not a request, the same way another man might mention he needed the salt passed. I already knew why, by then. Fraiser had told the rest of us, quietly, not long after she'd learned it herself, the two flat words that explained the whole of it. "I watch. They no wake up." An Eridian asleep is an Eridian completely paralyzed, mind included, unable to be roused for any reason. Period. No hand shakes it loose. No sound reaches it. That absolute helplessness is exactly what makes his whole species so vulnerable, and so dependent on having someone else nearby who hasn't gone under at the same time. Watched or not watched was, for his entire species, the actual difference between safe and not. He had done exactly that, for his own crew, every one of them, and it had not been enough. I'd known, from the moment I heard it, that the same courtesy would eventually come back around to him.

I sat with him through that first cycle myself, more out of curiosity than any real understanding of what I was agreeing to, and found it considerably less remarkable to witness than it apparently was for him to have someone doing it. He said it again the next cycle, and the one after that, until watching Rocky sleep became one more item on a rotation nobody had ever formally assigned, alongside coolant checks and comms watch and everything else this crew simply did now because it needed doing. Grace told me, once, quietly, exactly what it had cost him the one time in his life nobody had been watching. After that I stopped thinking of it as strange at all, and started thinking of it as the smallest possible thing to ask in exchange for everything else he'd already given this program.

He tried, once, to help Reyes with the medical inventory, an offer nobody had actually solicited, and reorganized an entire cabinet of pharmaceuticals by molecular structure instead of by clinical use, a system that made flawless chemical sense and cost Reyes forty furious minutes locating a basic painkiller during an otherwise routine headache. She banned him from the medical bay for exactly one week, a punishment he accepted with what I can only describe as genuine, cheerful remorse, and then quietly began leaving small, precisely organized diagrams of alternative filing systems taped to her door each morning, unsigned, until she finally let him back in purely to make it stop.

"I have never," she told me afterward, not quite managing to sound as annoyed as she clearly wanted to, "met a creature so committed to being helpful in exactly the wrong way."

"Welcome to my last several months," I said.

His own ship stayed behind us in the dark the whole while, broken, unrecovered, its exact future undecided. Nobody suggested leaving it to drift forever. Nobody had a plan for it either, not yet. It simply came along in all our thinking as a loose thread nobody had closed, useful for something, someday, none of us quite sure what yet.

Cent POV: I picked the next one on purpose, not the way I'd picked anything before it. Fraiser had already told the rest of us what he'd said to her, quiet and unprompted, two flat words that explained an entire species' worth of grief in half a sentence. I sat with that for a few days before I understood what I actually wanted to give him back for it.

I held the record up to the glass the same as always, grooves out, and didn't say a word about what it was or why I'd chosen it. He went still the way he always went still for anything that actually mattered to him, reading it the way he read everything, structure first, meaning arriving somewhere behind it.

He didn't say anything for a long time after it ended. When he finally did, it wasn't a question.

"This one is about losing something, and being willing to give away everything else you have left just to hold it one more day." A pause, longer than his pauses usually ran. "I did not know your kind wrote music about that. I did not know anyone did."

I told him they did. I told him I'd chosen it because I finally understood, better than I had before, exactly what he'd actually been carrying since long before any of us ever found him out there. He didn't answer that directly. He just held very still against the glass a while longer, the same stillness I'd learned to read as him needing a moment nobody was going to rush him through, and I let him have it.

"Everything I Own," the label said, once he finally let me take it back down. I don't think I'd ever actually looked at the label until that exact moment.

I brought him another one not long after, a different song hitting the same grief from an angle I hadn't expected until I was already holding "Time in a Bottle" up to the glass. This one wasn't about losing someone and being willing to trade everything to have them back. It was about time itself, the ordinary, quietly desperate wish to save some of it up before it ran out, against exactly the kind of loss he'd already lived through and never gotten any warning for at all.

He read it the same patient way he read everything, and when he finally spoke, there was something almost rueful in the register the translator gave back to me.

"Your kind wants more time the same way mine does. I did not expect that. I expected the wanting to look different, coming from something built so differently from what I am."

"It doesn't, apparently," I said. "Wanting more time might be the one thing that looks exactly the same on every species that's ever lost something it couldn't get back."

He didn't answer that one at all. He didn't need to.

I brought him one more before I let the habit settle into something ordinary again, and "State of Mind" didn't ask anything hard of him at all. It's not a song about losing anything. It's about the strange, funny way an old melody can drop you straight back into a moment you'd half forgotten you still had, handing you back something good instead of something owed.

He held still for that one the same way he held still for all of them. When he finally spoke, there was something in the register I hadn't heard from him before, not grief, not the careful, patient tone he used for anything technical. Something closer to what I'd come to recognize, by then, as him actually smiling.

"This one is not a wound," he said. "This one is a friend, laughing, for no reason anyone could later explain, in a room I have not let myself remember in a very long time." He didn't say which friend. I didn't ask. Some memories, apparently, translate best held a little apart from the exact words that would name them.

I still brought him something new every so often after that, never at random, always tied to something we'd actually talked about, some thread from a real conversation that a particular record turned out to answer better than I could on my own, ever since that first night with "Johnny B. Goode" and my grandmother's decades-old advice finally put to some actual use. I never bothered with a speaker after that first time. I'd hold each new record up to the same pane of xenonite instead, grooves out, and let him read it straight off the vinyl the way he'd read that very first one, structure cut directly into the material, no translation required, no glass or air or anything else standing between him and the actual shape of the thing. He'd developed real opinions by then, strong ones, and they shifted more than I expected once I started paying attention to why. It was never just the structure of a thing anymore, not the way it had been with that first record. A song could have all the driving rhythm in the world and still earn nothing from him if it wasn't actually about anything he recognized. Meanwhile something structurally plain, if it landed on the right subject at the right moment, could earn a reaction the cleanest rhythm in my whole collection never had. He judged the bad ones, in his own blunt way, as "sound without structure." I'd learned, by then, that what he actually meant by that had less to do with the notes than with whether the thing underneath them had turned out to matter. I'd also learned not to argue the point with him. He was, infuriatingly, usually right.
