Chapter 81: Cent POV: Just the five of us went down for this one. Me, Truman, Iverson, Childs, Grebenchekov. Nobody had argued the point when Osei first raised it in mission planning, months back, and I knew why once we were actually strapped into Threshold and I looked around at how little room there was to spare.

"First landing doesn't need sixteen people aboard," Truman had said, back when we were still arguing crew rosters over a conference table instead of a live descent profile. "It needs the people who can actually fly it, fix it, and command it if something goes wrong. Everyone else comes down once we know the ground doesn't kill them."

Nobody had pretended that was about protecting the rest of the crew from danger, exactly. It was about not risking sixteen lives on a landing that only needed five sets of hands, which was its own kind of protection, just a more honest one.

Iverson had the left seat, same as he'd had it for every real first this program had ever handed him, twenty-some years of seniority sitting in his shoulders the way it always did, no swagger in it at all. Childs had the right, running approach data against Pathfinder's own mapping one more time, quiet and thorough, the way she did everything. Grebenchekov had strapped himself in behind me loose-jointed and calm, a man who'd already survived one structural near-miss in his life and apparently decided that gave him permission to be relaxed about the next one.

Everyone else stayed up on Wayfarer, in orbit, waiting on my own ferrying runs to bring them down properly once the site verified safe.

"Man, another deorbit," I said, mid-descent, dry, because by then I'd earned the right to be dry about it, four years and a hundred easier gates behind me. "This one didn't go as well as the last one."

"Like a boss," Grebenchekov said, grinning behind me, right before the shaking started.

It wasn't supposed to be there at all. We'd already bought ourselves six clean hours specifically so it wouldn't be. But the storm Harding had called days out had left something behind on its way past: a pressure wave, an outflow boundary, some lingering disturbance in an atmosphere none of us had a real model for yet, drifted straight across our own approach corridor in the gap between his last good read and our actual descent.

"Harding." My own voice, tighter than I wanted it. "Talk to me. What is this."

A pause that lasted several lifetimes, static crackling under it, Threshold shuddering under my hands in a rhythm that matched nothing in any simulator I'd ever trained on.

"Don't know yet," he said, and I'll give him this, he didn't dress it up as more certain than it was, not even now. "Not the cell itself. Wrong shape for that. Something it left behind. Never read anything like it. Not here. Not back home either."

"That's not the answer I needed."

"It's the true one. Best I can do right now is tell you what it isn't. Isn't turbulence the way you'd know turbulence. Isn't shear the way I'd call shear back on Earth. Feels layered. Feels like it's got structure I just don't have the eyes for yet."

"Structure that holds isn't automatically a bad sign," Iverson said, steady, no fear in his own voice either, just information laid alongside Harding's. "Jupiter's had a storm the size of Earth sitting in one spot since before anybody down there had a telescope good enough to notice it. Old and organized isn't the same thing as unstable. Might just mean whatever we're sitting in has been doing this a long time and knows how to hold its own shape."

"That's a comfort and a warning in the same breath," I said, "and I don't have time to sort out which half to listen to."

"Then listen to both," Iverson said. "Fly it like it's stable enough to have lasted this long, and respect it like something that's lasted this long doesn't care whether we make it through."

I didn't wait for him to find the eyes for it, because we didn't have the altitude to spare for him to. I let training take over where no model existed to trust at all: I can force or hold red, never force green, and there wasn't even a green to distrust this time, just Threshold's own frame telling me things no gauge had a name for. I felt for it the way you feel for a stair tread in the dark, correcting off the shudder in the airframe instead of any number on any board, because for once every number on every board was exactly as lost as I was.

Truman didn't say anything at all, and in the one half second I had to spare for anything beyond the frame shuddering under my own hands, I knew what that silence was costing him. He'd built his whole career out of watching danger he couldn't personally reach into and fix, going all the way back to something he'd never fully told me about, some night before NASA, before any of this. He was watching now, helpless in the specific way that had shaped him, and there wasn't a single thing his rank or his experience could do about it except sit there and let me fly.

So I flew.

Iverson called headings, steady, no fear in his voice at all, just information, exactly the way twenty years of seniority had taught him to deliver it even into a problem nobody in this program had ever actually rehearsed. Childs read what stress data she could still trust off a secondary system, flagging anything the moment it stopped correlating with anything else around it. Grebenchekov braced behind me and said nothing, which was its own kind of help, one less thing for me to process while my hands did the only job that mattered.

We came through. Rough. Hard. Real. But through.

"Nice flying," Truman said, once we'd leveled out into a stable descent, and I heard, underneath those two flat words, how close that had actually been for a man who'd spent his whole life unable to look away from exactly this kind of moment.

✳

Chapter 82: Childs POV: Once the crisis cleared, the rest of it ran the way it was supposed to run: careful, procedural, no shortcuts taken just because we'd already survived the hard part. I'd learned that discipline a long time before Reach, on a river landing that had never once let me forget what happens when good instincts get treated as a substitute for good process.

I verified the environment myself, ephemeris and approach confirmed, orbit stabilized, Pathfinder's own mapping checked directly against what my own eyes could see rather than trusted blind. Habitable had never been a word I let myself use loosely, and I wasn't starting now, not on a world where the atmosphere alone would kill an unsuited person inside a minute, where the terminator winds ran hard enough to flatten anything not anchored properly, where the light itself fell strange and long across a landscape none of us had ever stood on before.

We'd selected the landing zone the way the models had always said we would: rocky terminator highland, a protective ridge at our back, the safest ground this world actually offered. I'd staged the landing craft, run the full readiness review myself, every abort point checked and rechecked before I let anyone commit to opening the vehicle. That was the plan we flew toward. It was not, in the end, the plan we landed on.

The disturbance Harding never got a clean read on had cost us more than a rough few minutes. It had cost us track. By the time Cent had us stable again, we were committed to a final approach nowhere near the ridge we'd spent months certifying, descending instead toward flat, unmapped ground Pathfinder's own survey had barely glanced at in passing. I watched the terrain resolve beneath us through the forward ports, rust-colored rock under a strange, flare-tinted sky, no ridge anywhere in sight, and felt something in my chest tighten that had nothing to do with the flight itself.

The landing itself still set down soft, controlled, the ground taking our weight cleanly. Real ground, on a world four light-years from anything any of us had ever called home, farther from Earth than any human being had ever physically stood. Just not the ground I'd certified.

"Great job, Ms. Marvel," Grebenchekov said, into the quiet after the engines finally cut, something warm underneath the dry delivery. "But I believe we blew the landing by roughly eleven miles."

"I was aiming for alive," Cent said. "Precision was a stretch goal."

"Alive, you got. Handily." He unbuckled, already moving. "The other thing, we will simply have to earn on foot, like the primitive terrestrial mammals we apparently still are."

Boots on the ground. First ones, mine.

Iverson could have argued for it. Thirty years of seniority, every real first this program had ever handed him, and he stepped back without a word of protest the moment the landing checklist called for the pilot who'd flown the descent to take the first step onto whatever it had actually put us on. "Your ground now, certified or not," was all he said, and hearing it, I understood he wasn't handing me an honor so much as the same discipline that ran through everything he did: the person who'd had to trust instinct instead of a map gets to be the one who finds out, first, whether that trust actually held.

I stood in the airlock a moment before I stepped through, suit sealed, every reading confirmed as many times as unmapped ground actually allowed for, and thought, briefly, about a girl who'd grown up wanting exactly this and never quite believing it would actually happen, and about a river eleven years back, and forty-one people who'd survived a landing built on instinct instead of process. I'd spent this entire program making sure I never had to repeat that trade. Standing there, I understood I was about to make it anyway, on ground nobody's model had ever actually looked at. Then I stepped down, felt the ground actually give slightly under my boot, real, solid, alien, entirely unverified, and stood there a moment longer before calling it in, letting the weight of it actually land the way it deserved to.

"Threshold is down," I said, over the comm, my voice steadier than I expected it to be. "Childs has the ground."

Then I got back to work, because the job wasn't finished just because we'd arrived, and there was an entire outpost still waiting to be built on the strength of exactly this first, careful footstep.

✳

Chapter 83: Osei POV: The beacons went up first, and Cent started ferrying crew and cargo down by repeated jump the same way she'd ferried everything else her whole career, and Threshold folded into the outpost as fixed infrastructure, never flying again, exactly as planned. Just not where planned. Not the ridge we'd spent months certifying, but ground was ground once sixteen people had to actually live on it, and nobody wasted a single hour mourning the eleven miles.

I ran power and structural first, the frame everything else would need to stand on. Nothing glamorous about it. Necessary, though, the same way it always was, and this time it meant more than the usual amount of redesign before I ever cut a single strut. Every structural plan we'd brought down assumed the ridge would still be standing at our back, blocking the worst of the terminator wind the way months of modeling had promised it would. Out here, flat and exposed, that wind came at us from whatever direction it wanted, full strength, nothing upstream of us to take the first bite out of it. I redid the anchoring scheme from the ground up, deeper pilings, lower profiles, guy lines run in a pattern none of our original drawings had ever called for, and found, somewhere in the middle of that redesign, that I hadn't felt this specific kind of tired since the very first ballast push, years ago, when everything about this program was still theory instead of steel.

Alvarez POV: Habitat life support came next, and I felt the echo of Rocky's own module the whole time I worked it, the same discipline, the same iterate-and-adjust process, just aimed now at sixteen humans instead of one Eridian. It went faster this time. We'd learned something building his, and I found myself grateful, more than once, for a friendship none of us could have predicted teaching us how to build a human home better.

Fraiser POV: Medical went in alongside Park, both of us building the exact kind of space I hoped we'd never actually need for anything serious again, though I'd learned better than to assume that hope would hold, not after everything this crew had already survived together. We stocked it the way you stock a place you're quietly praying stays empty: thoroughly, without cutting a single corner.

Park POV: I narrated the entire installation out loud, the same cheerful running commentary I used for every EVA, because Fraiser had made the mistake, early on, of admitting she found it oddly calming, and I have never once passed up an opportunity to be useful and entertaining at the same time. "And here," I told an audience of nobody in particular, hanging the last suction line, "we complete humanity's first permanent trauma bay on a planet orbiting another star, a small step for surgical infrastructure, a giant leap for not dying of something treatable four light years from home." Fraiser threw a glove at my head. I considered it a review.

Nasser POV: The lab came together under Ochoa's and my own hands, geology and geophysics finally getting a permanent home instead of a temporary rig, real ground under us this time instead of a ship's own deck. I stood in the doorway of it the first evening it was finished, an entire alien world's worth of research waiting just outside, and thought that I had never, in my whole career, felt luckier to be where I was.

Cho POV: I built the radiation shelter last, planetary-protection discipline baked into every seam of it, the same care I'd have wanted for myself if our positions were reversed. This was the work that had brought me onto the mission in the first place, and finishing it, watching the last seal test clean, felt like closing a promise I'd made to myself the day I signed on.

Each system got verified before the next crew rotation came down, the rest of the sixteen finally arriving in waves once the ground proved itself safe. Twinned water went into the sealed structures only after every check cleared, the same discipline that had carried us the whole way here.

Earth saw all of it, eventually, roughly four and a quarter years after the fact, the delayed feed arriving on its own schedule while we lived it in real time on the other end. Wayfarer stayed occupied and operational throughout, never all at once abandoned, the crew rotating down in careful stages instead of a single triumphant descent.

We built a foothold. Slowly, carefully, exactly the way we'd built everything else.

Chapter 84: Cent POV: We closed the expedition once the outpost certified, rather than staying indefinitely just because we could. Samples and records went home through me, the same courier route I'd run a thousand times by then, full quarantine procedure intact on every single trip.

Wayfarer couldn't stay behind at Proxima b. No propellant of her own, no way to function as a stationed asset anywhere I couldn't already reach directly myself, and working through it, I knew where she actually belonged instead.

Erid. I'd made a promise, and keeping it meant a detour before anyone's own return home.

I expected it to be a small trip. Me, Rocky, Grace, maybe one or two others.

The whole crew wanted to come.

"Sheer curiosity," Iverson said, when I asked him why. "How many people get the chance to see a second inhabited system in one lifetime? None of us are missing this."

So all of us went, the entire sixteen, curiosity winning out over any argument for staying behind. Watching them all pile in for the detour, I recognized the same impulse that had put most of them on this mission in the first place.

The crossing itself took the better part of two years, ship time, kick and coast the same disciplined way we'd covered every other stretch of this whole program, except this time nobody was counting down toward a mission objective. We were just going somewhere, together, because a promise mattered more than a checklist did, and the whole crew treated those two years less like a transit log and more like the first real vacation any of us had taken since the program started. Iverson taught half the crew a card game none of us had ever heard of and refused, on principle, to explain its actual rules until we'd lost enough hands to deserve knowing them. Grebenchekov ran a standing bet on Rocky's opinion of every new record Cent brought out, and lost more of it than he ever admitted to out loud. Grace finally had the time he'd never quite found before to read Rocky whole books instead of songs, a chapter at a stretch whenever both of them had an evening to spare, and came out the other side of those two years with a running catalogue of which human narrative structures repeated themselves and which ones apparently only worked once.

Rocky spent most of that stretch quieter than I'd ever seen him, not withdrawn exactly, just turned inward in a way I recognized because I'd done the same thing myself once, counting down toward something too large to talk about casually. Grace kept mostly to himself too, more than usual, and I noticed but didn't push, the same way I'd learned not to push at anything he clearly needed to work through on his own first.

NASA had the whole two years to solve the suit problem properly, and solved it exactly the way this program solved everything else: calm, methodical, try and fail and try again, the same discipline that had built not-water and the gimbal and every other piece of hardware nobody had ever gotten right on the first attempt. A dedicated twenty-nine-atmosphere suit, engineered for Eridian conditions specifically rather than adapted from anything built for Earth or Proxima b, went through four separate pressure-hull failures on unmanned test rigs before the fifth design actually held. I ferried crew back to Earth a few at a time for fittings as the design matured, never more than three or four people gone from Wayfarer at once, until everyone had a suit built and tested specifically for their own body, which, by the way, was everyone, sitting ready in a locker long before any of us needed it.

Osei spent the last stretch of the approach with a tablet in one hand, running comparative structural analysis against every alien-built thing she'd ever gotten real data on, muttering to herself about load paths and material science she'd never once expected an entire planet's architecture to hand her all at once. Fraiser stood near the medical bay's own viewport instead of the main one, quieter than usual, already turning over what an actual clinical exam of an entire Eridian population might someday look like, if Grace's whole strange embassy ever needed one. Harding just watched the atmospheric readouts scroll past, hungry for it, a man who'd spent a career reading incomplete weather off a planet he already knew, finally handed a whole new sky nobody on Earth had ever modeled at all.

I stood beside Rocky at the observation port as Erid resolved out of the dark, a world unlike anything our own solar system had ever shown us, its own colors, its own light, its own atmosphere wrapped around it like something patient and old. I glanced at him and found something in his posture I hadn't seen since before the shielding failure, a kind of stillness that read, even across every difference between us, as unmistakably like coming home.

"Good?" I asked, the same word Grace had used, because I'd picked it up from him without meaning to.

The translation came back simple, the same way it always did with him.

"Good."

Rocky POV: I had heard my own world from outside itself exactly once before this. The first time, my whole crew stood together at instruments none of us fully trusted yet, newly built, newly launched, hearing Erid fall away beneath a ship none of us would ever bring home again. There had been no time to actually listen to it properly, not really, not with the object already waiting and every hour spent on a world instead of on work we did not have the hours to spare. I did not know, hearing it then, in whatever fraction of attention I could actually spare for it, that I was hearing something none of the rest of them would ever hear a second time. I did not know, either, that I would be the only one of us who ever did.

This time was different in every way that mattered. Not launched away from something, with an object still waiting and every wasted hour a cost someone would eventually have to answer for. Arriving back toward it instead, aboard a vessel built by hands that were not my own, in the company of a species that had found me half-dead in the dark and decided, for reasons I still do not fully understand and no longer feel any real need to question, that I was worth the keeping. Nothing was waiting on the other side of this arrival that required haste. For the first time in my life, I was permitted to simply listen to it, for as long as listening actually took, and I discovered, standing there, that I did not know how to do that quickly even once I no longer had any reason not to. My own crew heard their own world fall silent behind them, alone, uncertain, believing they would return one day to tell their own people what they had found. I was hearing mine resolve out of that same silence instead, uncertain in an entirely different direction, wondering only whether the people I was bringing home to it would still recognize the man who had left.

Cent stood beside me the whole time, silent, understanding, correctly, that this was not a moment that needed narration. Grace stood a little apart, giving me the same distance he had learned to give me for anything that actually mattered, which I had come to understand, by then, was its own kind of gift.

I do not have a word yet, in either of our languages, for what it is to be the only survivor of a launch and also the only one who gets to hear the return. I have had a very long time to think about it since. I still do not have one. Some things, I have decided, are not made worse by remaining unnamed.

Wayfarer settled into orbit, and getting down to the actual surface turned out to be its own new problem before I ever got as far as worrying about jump-site logistics. Twenty-nine atmospheres meant a completely different descent than anything Threshold had ever been certified for: thicker air braking us far harder, far earlier, than Proxima b's own thin, unforgiving atmosphere ever had, a heating curve that looked nothing like the one Iverson and Childs had spent years perfecting, drag numbers they ended up re-deriving from scratch rather than trust to a profile built for a world with a fraction of this one's own pressure.

"It's not harder," Childs told me, running the new numbers a third time before she'd sign off on any of them. "It's not easier either. It's just a completely different sky, and I'm not flying anybody into it on Proxima math."

Harding spent that same stretch buried in atmospheric data of his own, decades of confident weather-reading meeting a sky that ran on rules he'd never once had to account for. "Twenty-nine atmospheres of mostly ammonia doesn't behave like anything I've ever personally called weather," he told me, somewhere in the middle of it, "but it still behaves like something. Give me a few more days and I'll have opinions worth trusting." He did, eventually, hand Childs and Iverson a real profile of expected turbulence bands and pressure layering, built the same patient way he built every read of every sky he'd ever actually understood, one stubborn data point at a time.

We took the shuttle down once all three of them had actually finished, first trip to the surface with just Rocky and Grace aboard. The descent itself was rough almost from the first moment we hit the outer edge of that impossible atmosphere, the shuttle bucking harder than even Harding's own profile had fully prepared us for, ammonia haze streaking the outer ports a deep orange-brown, heat building on the hull in the same uneven, patchy way he'd warned us to expect and that none of us had quite believed until we felt it firsthand.

"This is certainly living up to your name," I told Rocky, teeth gritted through another hard jolt, strapped in beside me and entirely unbothered by any of it.

"Explain," he said, and I decided, mid-shudder, that now was not the moment.

We came through anyway, rough the whole way down, and set down on open, unmarked ground a careful distance out from the nearest cluster of Eridian structures, hard enough to rattle every strap in the cabin but clean enough that nothing on the hull ever came close to failing. Rough. Real. But through, same as it always seemed to be out here.

Setting foot on actual Eridian ground, boots first, in the suit NASA had spent two years building for exactly this pressure and nothing else, I still felt the weight of the place land on me the second I cleared the hatch: heat that fogged my own faceplate before I'd taken three full steps, an atmosphere so dense it moved around me more like water than like air, and the deep, almost unfathomable dark of a place that had never once seen light, my own helmet lamps carving out a small, largely failing exception against darkness this world had never once needed to make on its own behalf. The suit did exactly what two years of failed test rigs had promised it would, and not one gram more than that.

It was enough. A verified site, walked, real, mine to jump back to whenever I actually needed to. Standing there, boots on ground that had never once had a human footprint on it before mine, I felt something click into place that I hadn't fully planned for: once I had that, reachable directly, Wayfarer never needed to move under her own power again. She could simply stay in orbit above it. Lab. Relay. Depot. A permanent foothold, here instead of at Proxima b, exactly the working asset the program had always wanted, just in a different sky than anyone had originally planned, watching over a friendship none of us could have predicted when this whole mission first left the ground.

I called Truman before I called anyone else, standing back aboard Wayfarer with Erid still turning slow beneath us.

"She's not coming home," I told him. "Not in the way anyone planned for. She's staying here instead."

He was quiet a moment, and waiting through it, I knew he was doing the same math I'd already done myself: budgets, precedent, every institutional argument a program this size could throw at a decision this size.

"Good," he said finally. "That's a better use of her than anything we originally drew up for her anyway. Log it. I'll handle the rest from this end."

"That's it? No committee?"

"There'll be a committee. There's always a committee. But you already made the actual decision the second you saw what she meant to him, and no committee on Earth is going to argue you out of that one, so I'm not going to waste either of our time pretending otherwise."

I stood there a long while after the call ended, watching a world turn beneath a ship that had just quietly become its own permanent piece of history, and thought about how strange it was that the biggest institutional decision of the whole detour had taken less than a minute to actually make.

Chapter 85: Rocky POV: They came the second day, not the first. I had expected this. My own people do not rush toward strangers, whatever hunger for the answer might be sitting underneath the caution. Word of my return had reached them faster than any of us could physically travel, faster even than I had, in whatever way word travels among us when there is enough of it to actually matter, and by the time the small group actually approached our own landing site, I understood they had already spent a full day arguing among themselves about exactly this moment before ever risking it in person.

I went out to meet them alone first, no suit, no need for one, while the humans watched from inside Threshold's own hull, patient in a way I had come to recognize as deliberately, visibly patient, the specific kind that says I am giving you room without you having to ask for it. I do not think that patience was wasted on the small group waiting for me either.

I told them what I had already told the crew, months earlier, waiting for someone to finally ask. That I was alive. That my crew was not. That the beings who found me were outside our experience entirely, in a way none of us had any real framework for, because we had never even had myths of this kind of thing, no stories built in advance about strangers arriving from somewhere else, since no native mind on Erid had ever had reason to look up and wonder who else might be looking back. I did not tell them everything in that first hour. I told them enough to earn a second hour, and trusted the rest to come the same slow way everything else between us and the humans had come: one true thing at a time, mutually incomprehensible at first, until enough of them stacked up into something that finally meant something to everyone involved.

Cent POV: I watched it happen from the shuttle's own forward port, closer than protocol technically wanted me but not close enough to actually hear anything, and understood, watching Rocky's body language do things I'd never seen it do before, careful, formal, entirely unlike the mischief and warmth he'd shown all of us, that I was watching him become someone else for a moment. Not a different person. The same person, in a register none of us had ever needed him to use with us.

Cho POV: We ran the whole first contact under the same discipline we'd have used for any unverified biological environment, full planetary-protection protocol, no assumptions carried over from months of knowing one Eridian personally. One Eridian's own tolerances told us nothing certain about an entire species' worth of them, and I wasn't going to let a friendship, however real, talk me out of running the actual science the way the actual science demanded.

It cost us something in warmth, that first careful hour, everyone suited, everyone keeping the recommended distance, nobody reaching for anything resembling the ease Rocky and Grace had built over months of exactly the opposite approach. It was the right cost to pay anyway. Nobody in that landing party got sick. Nobody on their side did either, as far as any of us could ever verify. Some prices are worth paying specifically because nobody ever finds out what they bought you.

Grace POV: I discovered, inside that first hour, that everything I'd built with Rocky didn't automatically scale. A shared vocabulary built patiently between two individuals over months was not the same thing as a shared vocabulary an entire species could simply inherit secondhand. I was translating for strangers now, not for a friend, and strangers extended none of the goodwill Rocky had extended me on a night when neither of us had any reason yet to trust the other.

It should have discouraged me. It didn't, quite. I recognized the shape of the problem too well from thirty years of a classroom to let it discourage me: a new group of students always starts from zero, no matter how well the last group eventually understood you, and the actual job was never building understanding once. It was building it again, patiently, every single time a new mind showed up needing it built.

Cent POV: The access came slowly: a widening circle, one careful ring at a time, no different in its actual shape from the exact caution this crew had shown its own government back when Rocky first came aboard. They let Grace in second, after Rocky himself. They let the rest of us in third, weeks later, in twos and threes, never all at once, never without Rocky standing somewhere in the room as the one relationship every new one of theirs got measured against.

Nobody on either side ever called it a treaty. It functioned like one anyway: an agreement, never written down because neither species had a shared enough legal tradition yet to write it in, that trust would be extended exactly as fast as it was earned and not one measured step faster.

✳

Chapter 86: Cent: News of this kind never went through a channel. It went to the house, the way it always had, delivered directly and in person because some things had earned that specific respect and none of us were about to start cutting corners on this one just because the distance involved happened to be new.

I jumped to the grounds outside, not into the house itself, the same way I'd have knocked at any other door I actually respected, and walked the rest of it on my own two feet the way anyone else delivering news to that address would have had to. Some things are simply true regardless of which house they happen to land in. That didn't mean the house didn't still get to decide how I came through its own front door. Bartlett met me there before I'd made it halfway up the walk, unsurprised in the specific way that told me McGarrity had learned, by then, to keep somebody watching for exactly this.

"He's in his study," Bartlett said, already turning to lead me there himself rather than send anyone else. "How bad."

"Not bad. Big. There's a difference, and this is the second one, not the first."

He didn't ask what I meant by that. He'd have found out in about ninety seconds either way, and I think he understood that pressing me for it thirty seconds early wouldn't have actually bought him anything worth having.

McGarrity looked up from his desk the moment we came in, and I watched him read the room before either of us said a word, the specific fast calculation of a man who'd built an entire career on knowing, at a glance, whether a piece of news was going to cost him sleep. He didn't ask me to sit. He didn't offer me anything either. He just waited, the way he'd learned to wait for exactly this kind of arrival, and I handed him the drive without any preamble at all, because preamble would only have made both of us wait longer for the same eventual sentence.

"Rocky's own people," I said. "First contact, wider civilization, not just him. It went well. It's still going well. I wanted you to have it before the feed ever did."

Bartlett: I'd delivered a great many pieces of news in my own career, going back to rooms far smaller than this one, and I still hadn't fully learned how to predict, in advance, which ones would actually cost a man something to hear and which ones he'd absorb standing perfectly still. I watched McGarrity's face while he read the drive's own summary off his desk screen, waiting for whichever version this one turned out to be.

It cost him something. Not visibly, not in any way the Secret Service's own standing cameras, mounted openly in every room in that house the same way they'd been for decades, were ever built to notice, since a camera watching for threats has no actual reason to register a man's own arithmetic quietly catching up with him. I'd spent enough years standing exactly where I was standing to read the smaller version of it anyway: a held breath let out a half second slower than usual, one hand flat on the desk longer than the reading itself actually required, the particular stillness of a man doing arithmetic he'd never once expected to need in this job.

"An entire species," he said, finally, not quite a question. "Deciding, right now, in real time, whether any of this is safe to trust."

"That's the shape of it," Cent said.

"I know that shape." He looked up, and something in his face had settled by then into the specific calm I'd come to recognize as him having already decided how he intended to carry this, whatever it turned out to actually weigh. "I've spent four years watching this program ask people to trust things they had no framework for trusting yet. I didn't expect to watch someone else's whole civilization go through the identical exercise from the other side of it."

"Does that change anything about how you handle it?" I asked.

"No," he said. "It just means I finally understand exactly how much we were always asking of everyone who ever said yes to us. I'd like to think that's worth something, going forward. I intend to find out."

I stood at the edge of that room and understood, watching him fold one more piece of impossible history into the same steady hands that had already carried so much of it, that I was watching something I recognized from a career spent standing just outside rooms where history happened without waiting for anyone's permission: a species deciding, in real time, whether to trust something it had no framework for trusting yet, and a man on the other end of that decision finally understanding, for the first time from the inside, exactly what he'd spent four years asking of people who'd never once had the luxury of deciding otherwise. I did not envy either side that particular exercise. I recognized it anyway, watching it land in that room, and found that recognition was its own kind of respect.

✳

Chapter 87: Grace POV: I made the decision once we were actually in orbit, not before, because I didn't trust myself to know what I actually wanted until I was looking at the real thing instead of the idea of it.

I didn't have much waiting for me back on Earth. A rented apartment I'd never quite finished unpacking into. A classroom full of students who'd have a substitute by now, probably a good one, probably one who didn't need the job the way I once had. No spouse, no children, a sister in Ohio I called twice a year out of obligation more than closeness. I'd built my whole life, without ever quite admitting it to myself, around waiting for exactly this: a reason large enough to justify how little I'd actually put down roots anywhere.

Erid turned slowly beneath us, home to a species I'd spent months learning to understand, and I thought about the paper that had cost me my career, the one questioning whether life had to look like Earth's own idea of it. I'd been right. Nobody had believed me. I'd spent a decade telling seventh graders about mitochondria and photosynthesis while the actual answer to the question that ended my career turned slowly on a viewscreen in front of me, real, undeniable, patient.

"I want to stay," I said, to Rocky, before I'd said it to anyone else, because it felt important that he hear it from me directly, not secondhand through the crew. "Go home with you. Be there for whatever comes next."

He didn't seem surprised. I don't think he'd expected anything else from me, honestly, by that point, and something in that lack of surprise felt like its own kind of welcome.

"First alien schoolmarm," I said, half to myself, testing the phrase, and found I liked it more than I expected to. It felt, oddly, like the truest job title I'd ever held.

Cent POV: He came to tell me himself, later that day, and I watched him work up to it the way people work up to things they've already decided but haven't said out loud yet, visibly rehearsing the sentence in his head before he trusted it enough to actually say it.

"I'm staying," he said, finally. "Here. With Rocky. Teaching, I think, mostly. Whatever else turns out to be useful."

I didn't argue. I didn't try to talk him out of it. I thought about my own family, my own decision made in about four seconds in a kitchen years ago, and understood that some choices don't need anyone's approval to be exactly right for the person making them. This was his. I wasn't going to make it smaller by questioning it.

"Ok," I said. "I'll make sure you have care packages."

He laughed, surprised, like he'd expected something bigger from me and gotten exactly the right amount instead, and I thought, watching him laugh, that I was going to miss him more than I'd let myself admit yet, and that missing him was a small price for watching him find exactly the place he'd always been looking for.

✳

Chapter 88: Grace POV: The dome went up faster than I ever expected, once Rocky's own people actually decided to build it, less a construction project than an outpouring, dozens of Eridian hands trading shifts around the clock until something large enough to actually live inside stood finished in what any human contractor would have called an impossible span of time. I hadn't asked for it. I doubt I'd have known how to ask for something this large even if I'd tried. They built it anyway, the moment Rocky made it plain what I'd actually decided, and handed me the finished thing the way you hand someone a gift you've already decided they can't refuse.

I'd started arranging real Eridian stone across the grounds inside it almost immediately, one piece at a time, for no reason any of them could quite follow yet, since to them it was just gravel set down by an alien with strange priorities. I could already see the colors in it, colors no Eridian eye had ever needed to develop. That much, at least, was real, and it was mine to build slowly, the same unhurried way I meant to build the rest of this life.

The sky above it wasn't real yet either, not for lack of trying. Eridian engineers were already wrapping the dome in a projection system I'd more or less begged for within days of moving in, after I made the mistake of describing what I missed instead of what I actually needed, and they never once treated the distinction as a reason to give me less. Coastline, some days, still rough at the edges while they worked out the physics of it. A gray-blue horizon they were still tuning to hold one color regardless of what real light would have been doing at that hour. Past the last curve of that half-finished shoreline, out beyond the dome's own edge, Erid's true dark went on exactly the way it always had, patient and absolute, the way every native mind on this planet had never once needed it to be anything else. I was the only thing here that required a horizon to feel human. They were already building me one, and never once made me feel strange for needing it.

I taught the Eridians the way I'd taught actual middle schoolers for years before any of this, patient, experimental, meeting them where they were instead of where a textbook assumed they should be.

They had the same rambunctious energy my old students carried, I discovered, once I stopped expecting alien reserve and started actually watching them. Young Eridians interrupted each other. They got things wrong loudly, correcting one another with real enthusiasm rather than embarrassment. It felt, more than anything else since I'd left Earth, like coming home to a version of the job I'd loved before it cost me everything.

The room itself was mostly theirs, not mine. A single wall of clear xenonite split it straight down the middle, my own thin slice of lamplight and borrowed oxygen on one side, Erid's true atmosphere and true dark on the other, and my students sat their own side of that divide the way children anywhere sit for a lesson, more or less still, more or less listening, close enough that most days I forgot to think of the wall as a wall at all.

I built lessons the same way I always had: start from what they already understood, find the bridge to what they didn't, let them make the mistakes that actually taught them something instead of protecting them from ever being wrong.

My first real lesson, the one I still thought about afterward more than any other, was about cell division, of all things, mine and theirs both, the strange convergent fact that two completely different chemistries had both eventually needed some version of the same basic solution to the same basic problem of getting bigger without falling apart. I built the whole thing around a diagram, the same kind I'd drawn on whiteboards for actual middle schoolers a hundred times, translated now into shapes and vibrations that meant nothing to me and everything to them. One of my students, young enough that I'd started thinking of her as exactly that, my student, corrected my own diagram halfway through, pointing out an asymmetry in my drawing that didn't match how her own body actually worked. She was right. I'd assumed too much symmetry, projected an Earth bias onto biology that didn't share it.

I thanked her for the correction the same way I'd have thanked any sharp-eyed kid back home, and watched her practically vibrate with pride, a student who'd just taught the teacher something real.

Rocky had given her a name close enough to human pronunciation that I could actually use it, and I'd started calling her by it without asking whether that crossed some line I didn't understand yet. She never objected. If anything, she seemed to prefer it, the way any thirteen-year-old prefers being singled out by name over being lost in a crowd of "the class." She corrected me constantly after that first success, delighted by her own new authority, until I had to gently explain, through Rocky, that there was a difference between being right and being right about literally everything, a lesson I suspect translated across species rather more easily than the biology had.

It worked. Slowly, the way real teaching always works, but it worked.

I stood in front of a room, or whatever passed for a room in a species that didn't build the way we did, and found a satisfaction I hadn't actually felt in years: watching understanding land in someone else's mind, one patient step at a time, exactly the same magic it had always been, just aimed now at an entire species instead of thirty middle schoolers a period.

I was starting a life here, not finishing a mission. I knew that clearly, standing in front of my new students, and found I didn't mind the distinction at all.

Rocky had a new ball by then too. Sleeker than the old one, angular where the original had been simple curved pentagons, built in his first month home out of ideas he'd apparently spent the whole voyage collecting from watching Osei work and materials nobody at Erid had ever had reason to combine before. He showed it to me the way he showed off anything he was actually proud of, which was to say: without a word of preamble, simply appearing in it one day at the meeting-room wall and waiting for me to notice. I noticed. He made me guess what had changed for a good ten minutes before he'd tell me, visibly delighted with himself the entire time.

Some number of them, that first real class, sitting where twenty-six seventh graders once had, half a galaxy and one whole species removed from a Tuesday and a lesson on mitosis and a career I'd once believed was the only shape my life could ever take. I asked them, near the end of that first lesson, whether anyone could tell me how fast light traveled. It wasn't a fair question. Not one of them had ever seen light travel anywhere at all. A few guessed anyway, loud and wrong and entirely unembarrassed about being either, and listening to them get it wrong together, I understood I had never once missed my old classroom less.

✳

Chapter 89: Cent POV: I brought them home in planned groups, the same disciplined rhythm that had carried this whole mission from the very first ballast push years before. Nobody rushed. Nobody got left behind either.

Osei went in the first group, back to a lab full of people who'd spent years working her own numbers without ever seeing the actual ship those numbers built. Childs and Iverson went together, still measured, still unhurried, the exact same two pilots who'd landed a broken shuttle in a river once and never once let the size of a moment change how carefully they flew it. Carter went home to a career finally, fully vindicated, every year she'd spent passed over for a seat now sitting behind her like ballast she no longer had to carry. Cho found her own sample racks rearranged one final time when she went to pack them, Rocky's own particular logic imposed on them for what both of them apparently understood, without saying so, would be the last time either of them got the chance. "At least now I won't have to worry about reorganizing my racks every day," she told him, and he gave her back the gesture I'd learned to read as pure delight, no translation needed at all. Grebenchekov went last among the early groups, grinning the whole way through his own goodbyes, promising Rocky, who wasn't there to hear it, that he'd be back to visit the second he actually had the chance.

Truman closed out the mission itself, methodical to the last, walking every system down the same careful way he'd walked every system up, and came home in the final group, exactly the way I'd have expected him to. I followed after, last of all, making sure the ship was properly buttoned up and Wayfarer's new life at Erid was settled before I let myself finally leave it behind for good. I stood on her deck a long moment before I went, hand flat against a bulkhead that had carried all of us so far, and said something quiet to her that I never repeated to anyone, not even Joe.

The public feed was still catching up on things that had happened years ago in our own experienced time, the delay running its own patient course independent of anything we'd already lived through. It didn't matter anymore, not the way it once might have. The crew's actual return proved the mission had worked, whatever the feed said or hadn't yet gotten around to saying.

Roughly three point two years, aboard, for all of us. More than five, back here. I felt the difference in small ways I hadn't expected, in how the light fell at certain times of day, in faces that had aged a little more than mine had. Dad had more gray at his temples than I remembered. Mom's hands, when she finally got them around me, held on a beat longer than they used to, like she was checking, physically, that I was actually real and not some very good broadcast.

"You're taller," Joe said, which wasn't remotely true, and which I let him say anyway, because I knew what he actually meant by it.

We came home to a jubilant reception, the whole program's years of careful, disciplined work finally standing in front of the entire country as an undeniable, finished thing. A functioning human foothold under another sun. A second permanent jump site, besides, out at a world none of us had ever expected to actually visit when this whole thing started. Truman found me in the crowd at some point, offered a hand instead of a hug, which was exactly right for the two of us, and I shook it and felt four years of everything we'd built together pass silently between us in the grip of it.

"Suck it up, man," I said, quiet, just for him, and watched something in his face crack open into an actual smile.

"I didn't need it this time," he said. "Not for a long while now."

"I know."

I stood there in the noise of it, surrounded by family, by crew who'd become something closer to family themselves, and thought about a number I'd first done in my own head on an ordinary Tuesday, years ago, that had once felt like an impossible wall.

Three thousand one hundred years, straight line.

We'd found another way. We always did.

Somewhere out past all of it, still unresolved, still watching or not watching, still waiting or not waiting, an ancient object sat where we'd glimpsed it once, briefly, on our way past. We hadn't answered what it was. We hadn't tried to. That question was still out there, patient, unclaimed, and standing in the noise of everyone I loved welcoming me home, I knew some other day, some other reach, would eventually go looking for the answer.

Not today, though.

Today was just this: everyone home, nobody left behind, and a whole new sky, out there, that finally had our footprints on it.

THE END

Bonus Chapter: The Honest Trailer

The screen opened on a spreadsheet. One number sat in an ordinary cell and ruined everybody’s morning.

“In a world where one family has already been turned into transportation infrastructure…”

At 410 kilometers per second, Alpha Centauri was three thousand one hundred years away.

The other voice brightened.

“Better add another axis to the chair.”

Silence.

“I hate that you’re right.”

This is Reach.

Watch eleven thousand engineers study one girl in one chair until “impossible” becomes a manufacturing schedule, and one girl’s ability gets turned into high-c travel.

“Is anyone else thirsty now?”

“Keep moving.”

Because the difference between “a teenager can push things” and “we can travel near lightspeed” is apparently one big brain and an unlimited price tag.

Meet Project Reach: Pathfinder, Wayfarer, and Threshold—one Nissan, one pair of Ray-Bans, and Target’s housewares line—because NASA’s naming committee had errands.

Experience testing. More testing… yet more testing of the tests themselves. And one final test that proves that the green light can, indeed, lie.

“The failure mode passed every test.”

“See? The test worked.”

“Leave.”

Meet Osei, who must build a ship light enough for Cent to kick to another star and strong enough to not need a cup.

Meet Dan Truman, commanding the impossible with a go/no-go poll, a flat voice, and the accumulated emotional damage of Armageddon.

Meet Joe, who can find Cent anywhere but cannot choose when, because the universe invented supernatural caller ID without a call button.

Then Truman does it too, forcing the research team to save PERSON-LOCK-FINAL-FINAL-USE-THIS-ONE.xlsx.

Ride aboard Wayfarer, propelled by thousands of tiny kicks, wrapped in shielding water, and crewed according to the strict requirement that everyone survive at least one other movie.

Featuring Childs and Iverson, graduates of the LA River School of Runway Identification.

Grebenchekov, posting through time dilation to followers who age faster than his upload schedule.

And Ryland Grace: middle-school teacher, disgraced scientist, and proof that somebody read Project Hail Mary and mistook it for a résumé.

Watch Cent become an interstellar courier, because even at light-speed the mail must get through.

Watch Earth age faster than she does. Watch visits home acquire quarantine rules, calendar arithmetic, and gray hair that was not there when she left.

The other voice tried to help.

“At least the trip is shorter for her.”

“Yes. She misses the years faster.”

Arrive at Proxima Centauri. Survive the false-green fault. Land on another world. Ignore the giant ancient Object because apparently the schedule is tight.

And meet Rocky.

He breathes ammonia, bleeds mercury, builds with xenonite, speaks in vibration, and cannot see light.

Prepare for Close Encounters of the HGTV Kind: an ammonia suite, texture TV, movie night, and a spouse named Adrian.

Rocky experiences Cent’s drive and delivers his peer review:

“Amaze. Amaze. Amaze.”

Then he risks himself for Osei without calling it sacrifice.

The crew takes turns guarding his sleep, because sleeping with one eye open is difficult for a species with no eyes.

The other voice inhaled.

“No.”

“Still didn’t say anything.”

“Good. We’re leaving this one lean and unmolested.”

“How unmolested?”

“Completely.”

“So how much molestation is allowable when writing a trailer?”

“None.”

“Not even a little tasteful molestation?”

“Why would you put those words together?”

“I feel like we should have a standard.”

“We do. Zero molestation.”

“That seems restrictive.”

“Leave.”

Watch Grace go from a disgraced middle-school teacher to the first alien middle-school teacher without passing Go or collecting $200.

He stays on Erid.

Watch as Grace misses the sky.

“We build sky.”

Inside it, Grace teaches alien children who interrupt, guess loudly, and correct him, proving middle school is not a place. It is a universal constant.

## Relativistic Calendar Roulette

Pick one of Cent’s trips. Guess how many birthdays Earth gets before she returns.

No changing your answer after Osei revises the velocity profile.

Lose a turn whenever someone says it is “only” a few years.

Bonus points if Cent returns the same age with a thumb drive that destroys an academic discipline.

Nobody wins. Cent wins every round.

## Starring

Millicent Rice as The Girl Who Gives Zero Fucks About Physics.

Ryland Grace as Welcome Back, Mister Kotter, Four Light-Years from Home.

The other voice lit up.

“NICE! A Welcome Back, Kotter joke! Where’s John Travolta?”

“Different alien movie. One that sucked.”

Rocky as Adrian’s Better Half.

Osei as This Old Starship.

Dan Truman as Armageddon II: This Time We Kick the Asteroid.

Joe as Can You Hear Me Now? No, But I Know Where You Are.

Quincy as Per My Massive Brain.

Childs and Iverson as The Core Curriculum.

Grebenchekov as One Small Post for Man.

Wayfarer as The Most Literal Kickstarter.

And the ancient Object as 2001: A Sequel-Hook Odyssey.

Alternate titles appeared:

The Chair Stuff.

Neither Snow nor Rain nor Time Dilation.

Quincy Flagged This Title.

The other voice raised a hand.

“The Chair: Tokyo Drift?”

“Wrong title. Possibly the right chair.”

The real title burned across the screen.

# REACH

Directed by: physics, overruled on day one.

Written by: a pack of monkeys who apparently know more physics than NASA.

Propulsion supplied by: one girl, one chair. Possibly two.

Special thanks to: the Eridian children, for being able to jump up and down on five legs and somehow make it look graceful.

The screen returned to the ancient Object, already behind them.

“They crossed four light-years, met an alien, built him a house, moved a teacher to Erid, and didn’t examine that?”

“They had a schedule.”

“Reasonable.”

“No. Consistent.”

McGarrity POV: I'd delivered a great many pieces of news to the American public by then, going all the way back to my first days as Chief of Staff standing quietly at the edge of someone else's briefings.   • 74. Rocky POV: They built me a space to live in. I had not expected this. I do not think I fully understand, even now, why it moved me the way it did.
  • 75. Fraiser POV: I asked Carter to be in the room for this one, because I could explain the biology and she could explain the physics, and I already knew, going in, that neither one of us was going to be enough alone.
  • 76. Grace POV: I wasn't the engineer. I was the bridge, translating not just vocabulary but the actual shape of what Rocky needed, catching the places where something got lost between his intent and their understanding of it, and I found, doing that work, that it fit me in a way almost nothing had since the paper that ended my old career.
  • 77. Osei POV: Xenonite and not-water weren't supposed to react at all. That was the whole point of testing the interface before we trusted it anywhere near occupied volumes, and the results had come back clean enough, months before any of this, that nobody thought to look at it twice.
  • 78. Cent POV: Osei found it during a routine systems sweep of Rocky's own vessel, the kind of check we'd started running out of simple curiosity more than necessity, since he'd been aboard Wayfarer full-time for weeks by then and nobody had gotten around to a real diagnostic on the ship he'd left behind.
  • 79. Harding POV: Pathfinder's whole pass across Proxima b lasted eleven seconds, and I want to be clear about what eleven seconds of atmospheric data on an entire unfamiliar planet actually gets you, which is not much, and also, if you know how to hold it right, almost everything.
  • 80. Grebenchekov POV: Wayfarer got back up to speed the ordinary way, an established maneuver, nothing on the scale of the interstellar cruise, ground already proven and not needing to be re-earned. I ran the readiness checks myself, same as I'd run them a hundred times before, and felt none of the old nerves at all, which said something about how far this crew had come since I first strapped into a chair back on Kristen Station.
  • 81. Cent POV: Just the five of us went down for this one. Me, Truman, Iverson, Childs, Grebenchekov. Nobody had argued the point when Osei first raised it in mission planning, months back, and I knew why once we were actually strapped into Threshold and I looked around at how little room there was to spare.
  • 82. Childs POV: Once the crisis cleared, the rest of it ran the way it was supposed to run: careful, procedural, no shortcuts taken just because we'd already survived the hard part. I'd learned that discipline a long time before Reach, on a river landing that had never once let me forget what happens when good instincts get treated as a substitute for good process.
  • 83. Osei POV: The beacons went up first, and Cent started ferrying crew and cargo down by repeated jump the same way she'd ferried everything else her whole career, and Threshold folded into the outpost as fixed infrastructure, never flying again, exactly as planned. Just not where planned. Not the ridge we'd spent months certifying, but ground was ground once sixteen people had to actually live on it, and nobody wasted a single hour mourning the eleven miles.
  • 84. Cent POV: We closed the expedition once the outpost certified, rather than staying indefinitely just because we could. Samples and records went home through me, the same courier route I'd run a thousand times by then, full quarantine procedure intact on every single trip.
  • 85. Rocky POV: They came the second day, not the first. I had expected this. My own people do not rush toward strangers, whatever hunger for the answer might be sitting underneath the caution. Word of my return had reached them faster than any of us could physically travel, faster even than I had, in whatever way word travels among us when there is enough of it to actually matter, and by the time the small group actually approached our own landing site, I understood they had already spent a full day arguing among themselves about exactly this moment before ever risking it in person.
  • 86. Cent: News of this kind never went through a channel. It went to the house, the way it always had, delivered directly and in person because some things had earned that specific respect and none of us were about to start cutting corners on this one just because the distance involved happened to be new.
  • 87. Grace POV: I made the decision once we were actually in orbit, not before, because I didn't trust myself to know what I actually wanted until I was looking at the real thing instead of the idea of it.
  • 88. Grace POV: The dome went up faster than I ever expected, once Rocky's own people actually decided to build it, less a construction project than an outpouring, dozens of Eridian hands trading shifts around the clock until something large enough to actually live inside stood finished in what any human contractor would have called an impossible span of time. I hadn't asked for it. I doubt I'd have known how to ask for something this large even if I'd tried. They built it anyway, the moment Rocky made it plain what I'd actually decided, and handed me the finished thing the way you hand someone a gift you've already decided they can't refuse.
  • 89. Cent POV: I brought them home in planned groups, the same disciplined rhythm that had carried this whole mission from the very first ballast push years before. Nobody rushed. Nobody got left behind either.
